# Workshop administration: the approval bots, the console and the admin server

The design of zab's workshop administration, kept in step with what is built (issue #267).

**Status at v0.2.19.**
- **Built:** the admin server, which serves several workshops at once, each through its own admin bot, and a global admin bot (§2.1); the command grammar, dry-run plans, approvals bound to a plan's hash, scrubbing and the hash-chained audit log (§2.2–§2.8); every workshop verb of §2.2's table but `!rebind`, the end-of-workshop verbs and `!retire` included (§3); the automated month-end, `!keep-until` and `!month-end` (§11.5); `!new-workshop` and its root config helper (§2.9); the deploy gate and `deploy.sh` (§1.6), and the in-band `!upgrade-server` with its two root helpers (§1.6, #569, from v0.2.19); encrypted backups (§1.7); the waker, its starter and the wake monitor, and the export sandbox (§1.2); the read-only console (§4).
- **Not built:** `!cancel`, which the bots answer as not offered yet (`grammar.LATER`), as they answer `!rebind`, which is not planned (#369): a deployer rebinds by hand (§2.5); the `Provider` protocol of §6; the console's sweep of every VM (§4.1); a daily backup (§1.7). §8 lists the stages, built and not.
- **Where the procedures are:** setting up a server and its first workshop, `docs/BOOTSTRAP.md`; adding a workshop, `docs/NEW-WORKSHOP.md`; running one, `docs/ORGANIZERS.md`; deploying, backups and recovery, `workshop/adminserver/README.md`. The manual path, the scripts of `workshop/admin/README.md` and `workshop/aws/README.md` from a deployer's laptop, stays the fallback.

**Goal.** An organizer other than the maintainer can run a workshop end to end, from roster to teardown, through:

- **a Zulip bot for each workshop**, in that workshop's private organizers channel, which changes the fleet only after a human approves a dry-run plan;
- **a read-only web console**, which shows the fleet.

All of zab's administration happens on Zulip or the web. Only the one-time bootstrap of a server is manual.

Both run on one **admin server** in the AWS account that runs the fleets (Q1). One server serves several workshops at once. It holds every key the fleets need, no coding agent can reach it, and it runs only code that a deployer reviewed and signed.

## 0. Decisions at a glance

| | Decision | Section |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | One small admin server in the account that runs the fleets (Q1). It is the only place that holds the fleets' keys. It carries **its own tag**, never a fleet's, and termination protection. | §1 |
| 2 | The server runs only a **final release tag signed with a deployer's dedicated key**, which is never on a machine where coding agents run (Q5). Before running it, the server checks the signature, that the tag's name is the one requested, and that the version is not older than the running one. A signature certifies that the commit passed CI, automated code review and the end-to-end suite, and that the deployer read its privileged surface themselves: `deploy.sh`, the systemd units, `sysusers.conf`, `tmpfiles.conf`, the nftables rules, the Caddyfile, and `render_policy.py`, and the root helper's code and the build's inputs, which `workshop/adminserver/privileged-paths` lists (§1.6; Q11, D31, D32). A deployer upgrades the server in-band, `!upgrade-server` on the global admin bot, whose root helpers make the checks again and install the release (#569); `deploy.sh` from their machine stays the bootstrap and the fallback (§1.6). | §1.6 |
| 3 | No LLM and no agent runs on the server. The bot is a deterministic parser: a fixed grammar, values validated against the roster and the config, and argument lists only. | §2.2, §2.3 |
| 4 | Every change follows request → dry-run plan → approval bound to the plan's hash (10-minute expiry) → apply. At apply time the plan's targets are checked again. Verbs that only reconcile live state are hashed on their inputs, not on their dry-run output. | §2.4 |
| 5 | An approval is a typed message from an organizer or a global admin, to the plan's own bot: `!approve P-12 3f9a2c`. Zulip's `zform` buttons, where they render, only send that text. A message that begins with a mention of anyone but the bot itself is not a command. Reactions are not used. | §2.2, §2.5 |
| 6 | Destructive actions, and re-binding a participant's identity, need two approvers, or a global admin alone (#558), and a typed phrase (Q3). The rebind verb is not planned (#369): a deployer rebinds by hand. | §2.5, Q3 |
| 7 | An append-only JSON Lines audit log with a hash chain. Each result posted by a bot carries the chain head, 12 hex digits. Encrypted backups go to the deployers after every change. | §2.8, §1.7 |
| 8 | The console runs as its own Unix user with no keys and no network access beyond localhost. Only root and the key-holding service users can reach the instance metadata service. Sign-in is a one-time link sent by Zulip DM. An organizer sees only their own workshop. TLS comes from Caddy, at the host of `console_url` (Q4). | §4, §1.2 |
| 9 | Each workshop's fleet is scoped by its tag (`workshop=<name>`) and lives in one region; workshops may use different regions. **The instance role is scoped by a fleet-wide tag, `zab-scope=<scope>` (#422):** it may change only resources in the server's scope that name some workshop, never the server's own, and may not move either tag. Each region is set up once, so a new workshop needs no AWS or IAM step; the bots, not IAM, keep one workshop's fleet from another's. | §5 |
| 10 | Provider-specific work stays behind the CLI contract of the `workshop/<provider>/` scripts; a small `Provider` protocol for the Python code is planned. No account IDs or regions in code. | §6 |
| 11 | **One admin bot per workshop.** Each is bound to exactly one workshop, and through it to one region and one tag scope. Organizers address their workshop's bot and never type a workshop name or a region. A workshop's organizers are recognized only by its bot; global admins, by every bot. A global admin bot serves the server-level verbs. | §2.1 |
| 12 | The design must not preclude 25 workshops of 40 participants over six months, several at once. Idle stop and the waker are essential to that budget. | §11 |
| 13 | No cap on the waker's starts. The wake monitor alerts the global admins about every start that no message of the VM's participant explains. Any message from the participant that one of the VM's bots received counts, mentioned or not, since that is what the waker wakes for; a roster row without `zulip_user_id` is a failed check, which alerts (#391). | §1.2 |

## 1. Architecture

### 1.1 Where it runs

```
    Zulip Cloud             GitHub                  deployer's laptop               organizers' browsers
 (admin channels, DMs)  (repos, invitations)   (deploy.sh: signed tag, review)                     │
        ▲   │                    ▲                            │ SSH, sudo                          │ HTTPS
 ┌──────┼───┼────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────┼────┐
 │      │   ▼                    │                            ▼                              Caddy :443 │
 │ zab-admin-bot ──argv──▶ workshop/*.py ◀── /opt/zab-admin/current (signed tree, pinned venv)     │    │
 │   │   │                    │     │ │                                                            │    │
 │   │   │                    │     │ └─ archive fd ─▶ zab-archive-check@ (no network)             │    │
 │   │   └─▶ audit.jsonl,     │     │                                                              ▼    │
 │   │       backups          │     │                  zab-admin-collector ── snapshot ──▶  zab-console │
 │   │                        │     │                                                        (no keys)  │
 │   └─ request ──────────────┼─────┼────▶ zab-admin-config (root) ─▶ admin.toml, secrets/bots/         │
 │                            │     │                                                                   │
 │                            │     │      zab-waker ── start.sock ──▶ zab-admin-starter                │
 │                            │     │      (opted-in bots' events)           │                          │
 │                            │     │                                        │                          │
 │                            │     │      IMDS: only root, zab-admin and    │                          │
 │                            │     │      zab-starter reach it (nftables)   │                          │
 │                            │     │                                        │                          │
 │                            │     │      admin server: zab-admin=<name>    │                          │
 │                            │     │      tag, termination protection       │                          │
 └────────────────────────────┼─────┼────────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┘
                   SSH, pinned│     │EC2 API, tag-scoped role                │StartInstances
                              ▼     ▼                                        ▼
                        participant VMs (tag workshop=<ws>, participant=<slug>)
```

Not drawn: the collector's own reads, EC2 and SSH, read-only (§4.1); the bot's wake monitor, which reads the starter's start log (§1.2); and the machines where coding agents run, which have no route to the server (§1.4).

- **Account.** The admin server lives in the account that runs the participant VMs (Q1). The deployer creates its IAM role and instance profile, security group, Elastic IP and DNS record, the console's host (Q4), with the account's own credentials (or the account's owner does), following BOOTSTRAP §3–§4. The bootstrap, step by step, is `docs/BOOTSTRAP.md`.
  - **Several workshops.** One server serves several workshops at once, one admin bot each (§2.1). Each workshop has exactly one region; different workshops may use different regions. Participants who opt in keep their VM for one month after every workshop (Q14), so workshops overlap: a workshop's kept VMs are still served while the next one runs.
  - **The bootstrap sets up the server and a first workshop:** the global admin bot, `#zab-admins`, and one workshop made by hand (BOOTSTRAP). Every later workshop is created through `!new-workshop` (§2.9, `docs/NEW-WORKSHOP.md`) and run with the organizers' runbook, entirely on Zulip.
  - A workshop in another account is a later seam (§6, §11.8).
- **The server's own tag.** The server is tagged `zab-admin=<name>`, never `workshop=…`. It has termination protection (`DisableApiTermination`), and its Elastic IP and security group carry the same tag. This keeps it out of `teardown.py --tag workshop=<ws>`, out of the fleet as the server reads it (`starter.fleet_vm`, §6), and out of its own role's reach. The role also explicitly denies every mutating action on resources tagged `zab-admin` (§5). As a second guard, `teardown.py` and `starter.fleet_vm` skip any instance that has no `participant` tag (§3.2).
- **Instance.** A `t4g.small` (arm64, 2 GiB) running stock Ubuntu 24.04, not the workshop AMI. It has a 30 GiB encrypted gp3 volume, IMDSv2 with a hop limit of 1, an Elastic IP and the instance profile of §5. The e2e run (§7) measures whether 2 GiB is enough with `--parallel 8` (V6).
- **Network.** Its own security group, `zab-admin`, distinct from the fleet's. Inbound: 443 and 80 (Caddy, with ACME and a redirect) from anywhere; 22 only from the deployers' CIDRs. A deployer points the 22 rule at their own address right before an SSH step, never from a network an agent machine shares, with `workshop/adminserver/ssh-rule.sh` (BOOTSTRAP §3.1, #674). Outbound: unrestricted, for Zulip, GitHub, the AWS API and SSH to the VMs.
- **The files host** (`workshop/files/README.md`) is a server of its own. The admin server's ties to it are each workshop's `refserve_url` and its `github_org`, the organization the files host serves, which it passes to the VMs (#693).
- **The waker** runs on the admin server, in *starter mode* (§1.2): it holds no AWS credential, and the server, in the fleets' account, starts the VMs for it. A setup without an admin server runs it in *direct mode*, on a host of its own whose role may start the fleet's VMs (`workshop/waker/README.md`).

### 1.2 Processes

| Unit | Unix user | Holds | Network | Stage (§8.1) |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `zab-admin-bot.service` | `zab-admin` | the admin bots' zuliprcs, one event queue each (§2.1); read access to the provisioner zuliprc, the GitHub token and the fleet SSH key; every workshop's roster and `out/`; reads the starter's start log (the wake monitor, below); writes the audit log | Zulip, GitHub, AWS, SSH | 1 |
| `zab-admin-collector.service` (a loop) | `zab-admin` | the same keys, used read-only: EC2 describe and the status sweep | AWS, SSH | 5 |
| `zab-console.service` | `zab-console` | its own session secret only; reads `snapshot.json`, the audit view and the allowlists | `IPAddressDeny=any`, `IPAddressAllow=localhost`: listens on 127.0.0.1:8090 only | 5 |
| `caddy.service` | `caddy` | the TLS certificate | 80, 443; no IMDS | 5 |
| `zab-waker.service` | `zab-waker` | group read on the opted-in participant bots' zuliprcs (in `waker/`); it may connect to the starter's socket and writes nothing outside its own state; **none** of the provisioner key, the GitHub token, the fleet SSH key or the instance role | Zulip; no IMDS | 4 |
| `zab-admin-starter.service` (started by its `.socket`) | `zab-starter` | holds the role and calls only `DescribeInstances` and `StartInstances`; no key; can read nothing under `/var/lib/zab-admin` or `secrets/`; its state in `/var/lib/zab-starter`, with the start log the bot reads | AWS | 4 |
| `zab-archive-check@.service` (one instance per connection to its `.socket`) | its own per instance, allocated and released by systemd (`DynamicUser`) | nothing: the connection systemd passes it, and a participant's export archive as a read-only descriptor; it can reach nothing under `/etc/zab-admin` or `/var/lib/zab-admin` and writes nothing | none (`PrivateNetwork`, no address family) | 3 |
| `zab-admin-config.service` (oneshot, started by `zab-admin-config.path`; #418) | root | a new workshop bot's key, in transit only: it validates the new `[[workshop]]` entry and the whole new `admin.toml` with the strict loader, then writes the key to `secrets/bots/<ws>.zuliprc` and writes `admin.toml` (§2.9) | none (`PrivateNetwork`) | 7 |
| `zab-admin-upgrade-plan.service` (oneshot, started by `zab-admin-upgrade-plan.path`; #569) | root | the release repository's deploy key (`secrets/release-key`), alone of `secrets/`: it fetches two tags, checks them as `deploy.sh` does, and has the running release's `deploy.sh --review` the release (§1.6). Its sandbox: it writes only its own state; `/var/lib/zab-admin`, the console's and Caddy's state, the SSH host keys and the password hashes are hidden | GitHub; no IMDS | 6 |
| `zab-admin-upgrade.service` (oneshot, started by `zab-admin-upgrade.path`; #569) | root | what `sudo bash deploy.sh --server` holds over SSH: it runs the release's own `deploy.sh`, so it keeps root's capabilities, with `/usr` and `/boot` read-only (`ProtectSystem=true`), no home directories, its own `/tmp` and no new privileges (§1.6) | what `deploy.sh` needs: uv's tarball, PyPI's wheels | 6 |
| `zab-admin-imds.service` (oneshot, at boot, before the network) | root | nothing: it loads the IMDS rule (below), `/etc/zab-admin/imds.nft` | netlink only | 2 |

- **Hardening.** Every unit gets the hardening of `workshop/files/zab-files.service`: `ProtectSystem=strict`, `ProtectHome`, `PrivateTmp`, `NoNewPrivileges`, an empty `CapabilityBoundingSet` and `UMask=0077`. Three keep capabilities: `zab-admin-imds`, `CAP_NET_ADMIN`, to load the rule; Caddy, `CAP_NET_BIND_SERVICE`, through its drop-in; and the config helper, `CAP_DAC_OVERRIDE` and `CAP_CHOWN`: it reads and deletes the request in `zab-admin`'s 0700 directory, and gives the key its group.
- **The config helper's mounts** (#437). It is root, so its unit names what it may write. Under `/etc/zab-admin`: the directory itself, since `admin.toml` is replaced by a rename there (its temporary file, and `admin.toml.bak`, a link to the old one), and `secrets/bots/`, where it creates the new key. Every other file `deploy.sh` installs there is a read-only mount: `allowed_signers`, `backup_recipients`, `break-glass/`, and the nftables files root loads, `imds.nft`, `nftables-imds.nft` and `nftables-stop-imds.nft`, so it can't change the IMDS rule. Each is named without `-`: one that is missing, which the helper could otherwise create, keeps the unit from starting. The rest of `secrets/` is hidden under an empty read-only tmpfs, and `/var/lib/zab-admin`, with the fleet key, the bot's state and the participants' keys, is inaccessible. A deploy test checks these names against everything a bootstrap installs under `/etc/zab-admin` and every path there that a unit or rule names, and, where a user systemd manager can run them, the mounts against systemd's own. What stays open: a new file directly in `/etc/zab-admin`, which nothing reads unless a unit names it (the same test), and the other bots' keys in `secrets/bots/`, since a unit can name only paths that exist when it starts.
- **IMDS.** The instance role's credentials come from IMDS. An nftables rule installed at bootstrap rejects traffic to `169.254.169.254` from every UID except root, `zab-admin` and `zab-starter`, and the same for `[fd00:ec2::254]` if IPv6 IMDS is ever turned on. `nftables.service` flushes the ruleset when it starts, reloads or stops; a drop-in makes each of those commit the rule in the same transaction as the flush, so the ruleset is never without it. Caddy, the only internet-facing process, the console and the waker therefore can't obtain the role (`workshop/adminserver/README.md`).
- **Why the waker gets its own user.** It handles untrusted input: any Zulip user's DMs to participant bots. So it is the process to isolate from the keys that matter, the role included: through `StopInstances`, `ModifyInstanceAttribute` (user data) and `StartInstances`, the role is root on every fleet VM, as the fleet SSH key is.
- **The waker's start path** (#329, implemented in PR 21). The waker sends its requests, a workshop and instance IDs to describe or one to start, to `/run/zab-admin-starter/start.sock`, which only root and the `zab-waker` group may use (`src/zab/admin/starter.py` documents the protocol). `zab-admin-starter`, under its own user `zab-starter` (a process of the bot's uid could read the bot's keys through `/proc/<pid>/root`, whatever its mounts hide), checks the peer (`SO_PEERCRED`) and each instance against the workshop in `admin.toml` (every scope tag, a non-blank participant tag, no `zab-admin` tag, the region), answers states for those VMs only, rate-limits starts per VM, and calls `DescribeInstances` and `StartInstances`, nothing else. A compromised waker can at most ask for starts of stopped fleet VMs. The message that triggered a start stays with the waker: the starter could not check it, and nothing it logs comes from Zulip.
  - **Deployed** by `deploy.sh` (#348), which enables and restarts `zab-admin-starter.socket` and `zab-waker.service` at every deploy. The socket goes first, and its service is stopped, so the next request runs the new release; the waker goes after the bot. The new release's unit files are installed and loaded only after the switch, so a failed deploy leaves the running release's units in place. A large `!waker on` can hold the opt-in lock past its 60 s wait (one `DescribeInstances` per participant): a concurrent run of another workshop is then refused cleanly and is run again afterwards.
  - **A restart catches up** (#403). The waker exits when its config changes (`!waker on|off`), within 30 s, and systemd starts it again about 5 s later; deploys and reboots restart it too. Its new queues see only what comes after them, so it then catches up on each bot's messages since its stored mark: at most 100 per bot, none of the VM's own bots' and 5 minutes back, at most 8 bots at a time (#717), through the same checks as live ones. At each start it forgets the marks of bots its config no longer lists, so a bot just opted in, or opted in again, catches up from the config's write: a message sent while it wasn't opted in wakes nothing. A message sent more than 31 s before the waker last saw the VM pending or running wakes nothing either (30 s for the clocks and Zulip's whole second; SPEC §13.4.3).
    - **Best effort.** A fetch that fails is tried 4 times, between the queue's polls; a restart meanwhile fetches again. After the fourth failure (Zulip unreachable for about a minute or more), what it would have fetched is missed. So are messages older than 5 minutes, and those before a bot's newest 100, its VM's bots' own left out.
    - **A bot with no mark** (no message delivered to it since its opt-in) catches up from the config's last write, 30 s back. So a config change made during an outage (any workshop's `!waker on|off`) loses what that bot was sent more than 30 s before the change.
    - **Its memory** (#717). On Nov 1 each start, with about 118 bots after two workshop days, was killed at the unit's `MemoryMax=512M` (about 1.3 GB with swap): every bot fetched its newest 100 messages at once, each with its edit history, and each bot's two httpx clients built a TLS context of about 0.7 MB. Since #717 the catch-up fetches only what is new, from the newest message back, 10 at a time, and keeps of each only what the waker reads; at most 8 bots at a time, each turn, registration and catch-up together, for at most 30 s; and the process has one TLS context. Measured with the load rig (`tests/waker/load_rig.py`, DECISIONS' log, Python 3.11 to 3.14): 57 to 81 MB at 150 to 340 bots; up to about 270 MB after a long outage in a channel the VMs share, where another VM's bot's edited status messages can't be left out: 266 MB at most, in several runs at 340 bots on Python 3.14 with every message of the outage in its last 5 minutes (`--recent`), and 205 to 208 MB on the server's Python 3.12. `MemoryMax=512M` stays, nearly twice that. When no ⏰ comes for anyone: §1.8.
    - **The wake monitor.** A start for a caught-up message is within its 10 minutes, so it is `matched`, unless the wake first waited for a VM still stopping (up to 5 minutes) and then retried for capacity: that start alerts as unmatched, a false alarm.
  - **One opt-in run at a time.** `push_waker.py --config-dir` takes `waker/.lock`, a 0600 file of `zab-admin`'s that the waker can't open. A read-only fd would do for an `flock`, so a lock on anything the waker can read would let it block `!waker on`/`off`.
- **The wake monitor** (#359, `src/zab/admin/wakes.py`; chosen over a cap on starts). It watches the waker from outside, so that a compromised waker can't hide what it starts:
  - **The start log.** Before each `StartInstances`, the starter appends a line to `/var/lib/zab-starter/log/starts.jsonl`: the time, the workshop, the VM's `participant` tag, the instance and the peer that asked (root or `zab-waker`). It makes no start it can't log. `log/` is `zab-starter:zab-admin` 2750 and each file 0640: the bot reads it, and nothing of the waker's can write it. The bots refuse to start when its directory has any other owner or mode (`bot --check` says so). At 1 MiB the file becomes `starts.jsonl.1`.
  - **Opted in?** A start the waker asked for (`peer` `zab-waker`), of a VM that the waker's config doesn't list, alerts at once, with no message check: the config is `waker/config.toml`, which the bots write (`!waker on|off`) and the waker only reads (hardened on #391). When the config changed after the start, this can't be told, and the start is checked as any other.
  - **The check.** Every 2 minutes the bot process, as `zab-admin`, takes each start of the last 6 hours it hasn't checked: the participant is the roster row's `zulip_user_id`, the sender the waker obeys, never the bot's Zulip owner (the provisioner since #364). With each of the VM's two bots' own keys from `out/`, `GET /messages` narrowed to that sender returns what that bot received: a DM, or a message in a channel it is subscribed to, as the waker's queues get them. One sent from 10 minutes before the start to 30 s after is a match.
  - **Decided** (#391): any message from the participant that one of the VM's bots received counts, a DM or a channel message, mentioned or not. The waker wakes a VM for each of them (SPEC §13.4), so a narrower rule (DMs and mentions only) would alert on legitimate wakes. A row bound by e-mail only, with no `zulip_user_id`, is not looked up by its e-mail: its check fails, and alerts.
  - **No match:** an alert in `#zab-admins`, from the global admin bot (the workshop's bot when there is no `[global]`), naming the workshop, the slug, the instance, the time, who asked and the participant's user ID, never a message's text; at most one per VM an hour, the next counting the starts held back. An alert Zulip refuses is posted again on each pass, and the start is recorded only once it goes out, or 6 hours on, as undelivered. **A check that fails** alerts with its reason: at once when another try can't help (no key, no roster row or `zulip_user_id`), and after two more passes when it might (a Zulip error, a timeout). So does a start log the bot can't trust or read.
  - **Nothing ages out silently.** A start still unchecked 6 hours after it was made (a check that kept failing, a backlog, the bots down for longer) gets a record, and the pass's batch one summary alert that counts and names them, at most one an hour; the next counts those held back. A summary Zulip refuses is posted again on later passes, as an alert is. Only a start that left the log's two files before the bots read it is lost: about 7,000 starts later.
  - **Held starts.** Starts held back by a VM's hourly limit are counted, and listed by their times, in its next alert. If no later start of that VM comes, they are only in the audit log.
  - **A `wake_check` audit record** either way (§2.8). The records are the monitor's memory across restarts.
  - **Bounds per pass:** 20 starts, 40 `GET /messages` of 50 messages, 2 pages per bot and start, 20 s a check, 90 s a pass. What is left waits for the next pass.

- **The export re-pack** (#374). A participant's export archive is untrusted input to `gzip` and `tarfile`, so no process that holds a key parses it. `collect_exports.py`, run by the bot as `zab-admin`, connects to `/run/zab-archive-check/check.sock` (root:zab-admin 0660; the directory is root's) and sends one line, `repack <budget>`, with the fetched archive as a read-only descriptor (`SCM_RIGHTS`). The socket starts an instance of `zab-archive-check@.service` per connection (`Accept=yes`), which:
  - runs as a user of its own, allocated for it and released when it ends (`DynamicUser`, named after the instance: the connection's number and the caller's pid and uid), so no two instances, and no other process, share a uid;
  - has no network and makes no socket: `PrivateNetwork`, `IPAddressDeny=any`, `RestrictAddressFamilies=none` and no `socket` or `connect`, and **no io_uring** (`~@aio`), whose operations make and connect sockets past both (#384: through it a probe reached D-Bus and resolved's varlink socket, and so DNS); the shared sockets listed are inaccessible too (D-Bus, resolved's and userdb's varlink, journald's stdout socket, the users' runtime directories, the starter's socket; other sockets under `/run` stay visible but can't be reached without a socket), but not `/run` or `/run/systemd` as a whole: PID 1 needs `/run/systemd` to set the unit up, and with either hidden every instance exited 226/NAMESPACE (A19 on the rig, #392). It can reach nothing under `/etc/zab-admin`, `/var/lib/zab-admin` or the other services' state (`InaccessiblePaths`), and writes nothing (`ProtectSystem=strict`, its private `/tmp` read-only, `LimitFSIZE=0`);
  - has a system call filter: `@system-service` without `@privileged`, `@process` (no new process), `@resources`, `@debug`, `@aio` and the like, nor `socket`, `connect`, `userfaultfd` or `process_vm_readv`; with `MemoryDenyWriteExecute`, `NoNewPrivileges` and no capability;
  - is bounded: `MemoryMax=256M`, `CPUQuota=100%`, `CPUWeight=20` (the bots come first), `RuntimeMaxSec=960`, and `archive_check.py`'s own rlimits, which it sets again before it reads a byte; at most 4 connections at once (`MaxConnections`): one more gets no answer, and the table says the checker may be busy (`collect_exports.MAX_CHECKS`);
  - answers only root and `zab-admin` (`SO_PEERCRED`), and `collect_exports.py` talks only to a socket that is root's.

  It answers with the re-packed archive, framed, or a refusal (`archive_check.py`'s docstring states what the stored archive is). A parser exploit, code run in the checker, gets an empty sandbox: no key, no network, no socket, no other archive. **The stored archive's claim holds as long as the checker runs its own code:** such code can answer any bytes as the re-pack, and `collect_exports.py` stores what comes, unexamined. Those bytes go back to the participant, who controls the input anyway. `systemd-analyze security` rates the unit 0.4 ("SAFE"); `PrivateUsers=` stays off, since it would show `zab-admin` to `SO_PEERCRED` as `nobody`. `deploy.sh` enables and restarts the socket with the others, before the bot; each instance ends with its connection, so the next one runs the new release.

### 1.3 Files

| Path | Owner, mode | What |
|---|---|---|
| `/etc/zab-admin/admin.toml` | root 0644 | config: the workshops, the allowlists, provider settings (§2.1, §6). It holds no secrets, so the console can read the allowlists from it. A deployer edits it over SSH; `!new-workshop` adds an entry through the root helper (§2.9). |
| `/etc/zab-admin/allowed_signers` | root 0644 | the release signers' SSH public keys (§1.6) |
| `/etc/zab-admin/backup_recipients` | root 0644 | the deployers' keys, used as `age` recipients (§1.7): `ssh-ed25519` public keys or X25519 `age1…` keys (their bech32 checksum checked), one per line; the bots don't start unless every line is one |
| `/etc/zab-admin/secrets/{global.zuliprc,provisioner.zuliprc,github-token}` | root:zab-admin 0640 | the global admin bot's key, the provisioner's and the GitHub token, entered with `systemd-ask-password` at bootstrap, as the files host does |
| `/etc/zab-admin/secrets/bots/<ws>.zuliprc` | root:zab-admin 0640 | each workshop's admin bot key: entered at bootstrap for the workshops that exist then, and written by the config helper for `!new-workshop` (§2.9). The loader accepts a bot's key only under `secrets/` (PR 5a); the subdirectory keeps a workshop's name from colliding with the other secrets. |
| `/opt/zab-admin/releases/vX.Y.Z/` | root 0755 | the verified source tree of a tag, and its venv of third-party wheels (§1.6) |
| `/opt/zab-admin/current` | root | symlink to the running release |
| `/var/lib/zab-admin/` | zab-admin 0711 | the state directory; `zab-console` needs search permission to reach `console/` |
| `/var/lib/zab-admin/.ssh/id_ed25519` | zab-admin 0600 | the fleet SSH key, generated on the server; never leaves it, and is not backed up (§1.7) |
| `/var/lib/zab-admin/ws/<ws>/{roster.csv,out/}` | zab-admin 0700 | per workshop: the roster, `known_hosts`, `spares.json`, the participant bot zuliprcs (the waker reads copies of the opted-in ones, below), final-sync reports. Where this document writes `ws/out/`, it means the workshop's own `ws/<ws>/out/`. |
| `/var/lib/zab-admin/state.db` | zab-admin 0600 | plans, approvals, the apply locks (SQLite) |
| `/var/lib/zab-admin/retired-slugs.txt` | zab-admin 0600 | the slugs of retired workshops, never reused on this server (§2.1) |
| `/var/lib/zab-admin/audit.jsonl` | zab-admin 0600, `chattr +a` | the audit log (§2.8) |
| `/var/lib/zab-admin/runs/P-<n>/` | zab-admin 0700 | full scrubbed dry-run and apply output per plan (`dry-run.txt`, `apply.txt`) |
| `/var/lib/zab-admin/tmp/P-<n>/` | zab-admin 0700 | a plan's `TMPDIR`, only while its scripts run (§2.3) |
| `/var/lib/zab-admin/console/{snapshot.json,audit-view.jsonl}` | zab-admin:zab-console 0750; files 0640, set explicitly (below) | what the console may read |
| `/var/lib/zab-admin/collector/` | zab-admin 0700 | the collector's own state: the cost history and the last status values (`state.json`), and each workshop's `!status` reads, which the bot writes there (`reads/<ws>.json`); each 0600 |
| `/var/lib/zab-admin/console/tokens/` | zab-admin:zab-console 2770 | sign-in token hashes: the bot writes them, the console deletes them on use (§4.4). Token files are `zab-admin:zab-console` 0640, set explicitly (below) |
| `/var/lib/zab-console/secret` | zab-console 0600 | the console's cookie-signing secret |
| `/var/lib/zab-admin/waker/` | zab-admin:zab-waker 2750 (setgid); directories 0750, files 0640 | what the waker reads: its `config.toml`, and copies of the opted-in bots' zuliprcs, `<ws>/<slug>/{codex,claude}.zuliprc`, which `push_waker.py --config-dir` writes for `!waker on` and removes for `!waker off` (§3, action 5); and `.lock`, zab-admin 0600, which the waker can't open (§1.2) |
| `/var/lib/zab-waker/` | zab-waker 0711; `status/` zab-waker:zab-admin 2750 | the waker's own state (its catch-up marks), and `status/waker.json`, 0640, which the collector reads (§4.1) |
| `/var/lib/zab-starter/log/starts.jsonl` (and `.1`) | zab-starter:zab-admin 0640, in `log/` 2750 under the starter's state 0711 | the start log: one line per `StartInstances` the starter makes, which the wake monitor checks (§1.2, #359) |
| `/var/lib/zab-admin/releases/wheels/` | zab-admin 0700 | fleet wheels, placed by `deploy.sh` (§1.6) |
| `/var/lib/zab-admin-config/requests/request.json` | zab-admin 0600, in its 0700 directory inside the helper's root 0755 state | `!new-workshop`'s request to the config helper: the new entry and the new bot's key; the helper deletes it once read (§2.9) |
| `/var/lib/zab-admin-config/{result.json,changes.jsonl}` | root 0644, in root's 0755 state directory | the config helper's outcome of its last request, which the plan's apply reads, and one line per change it made to `admin.toml`, which the bot can't change (§2.9) |
| `/etc/zab-admin/secrets/release-key`, and its `.pub` | root 0600; the `.pub` 0644 | the deploy key of `admin.toml`'s `release_repo`, read-only on GitHub: made on the server by a deployer (BOOTSTRAP §13.4), never copied off it; only the upgrade's plan helper reads it (§1.6, #569) |
| `/var/lib/zab-admin-upgrade/requests/{plan,apply}.json` | zab-admin 0600, in its 0700 directory inside the helpers' root 0755 state | `!upgrade-server`'s requests to its two root helpers; each helper deletes its request once read (§1.6) |
| `/var/lib/zab-admin-upgrade/results/P-<n>.{plan,apply}.json` | root 0644, in root's 0755 `results/` | the helpers' outcomes, which the bot's script reads; the apply helper trusts the plan helper's, root's own record of the plan (§1.6) |
| `/var/lib/zab-admin-upgrade/{repo.git,work}/` | root 0700 | the plan helper's copy of the two tags it fetched (`refs/fetched/`, and in `refs/tags/` the ones it took, never moved to another commit), and each run's review and logs |

**Files the console reads.** Every unit runs with `UMask=0077`, and a setgid directory gives a new file its group but not group read. So the bot and the collector write each file the console must read (token files, `snapshot.json`, `audit-view.jsonl`) under a temporary name, `fchmod` it to 0640, and then rename it into place. A file the console can't read would break sign-in or the page; one that is world-readable would leak.

### 1.4 Why the coding agents can't reach the keys

1. **No credentials.** No coding agent, and no machine where one runs, has a credential that can change anything in the fleet itself, or any credential for the admin server or its keys. The key that reaches the server's port 22 is the deployer's dedicated key, never a personal one, and it is never on a machine where coding agents run (Q5). Coding agents touch only test infrastructure, tagged `zab-role=test`: that rule is enforced by where the keys are, not by instructions.
2. **No agent on the server.** No coding agent or LLM runs on the server. Its only input is commands typed by allowlisted organizers, which a fixed grammar maps to fixed argument lists (§2.2).
3. **Only reviewed and signed code runs.** What protects the keys is the review of each change (CI, automated code review, the end-to-end suite), the deployer's own reading of the privileged surface before they sign a tag, and the server's checks of that signature (§1.6; Q11, D31).
4. **The participant VMs are untrusted.** Their agents have sudo.
   - Connections from the server use pinned host keys, `BatchMode`, and no agent, X11 or port forwarding, as the scripts already do.
   - Anything a VM prints is data: size-capped, scrubbed and fenced (§2.7). It is never interpreted and never parsed as an approval.
5. **The internet-facing processes hold nothing.**
   - The console can't read the secrets (another Unix user) and can't reach IMDS (systemd IP filter and nftables).
   - Caddy can't reach IMDS either (nftables).
6. **The allowlists are not editable through a workshop's bot.** Global admins and deployers are edited only in `admin.toml`, over SSH, by a deployer. A workshop's organizers are set there too, or by `!new-workshop` on the global admin bot, which only a global admin approves (§2.9). A compromised organizer account can't add an accomplice. A compromised global admin account can, as it can approve a destructive plan alone (#558): the global admins can delete the VMs and deactivate the bots in AWS and Zulip without any approval already, so a second approver among them guards nothing that their own accounts don't.

### 1.5 Threats

| Threat | Mitigation |
|---|---|
| A participant's agent (sudo on its VM) attacks the server through SSH output | Pinned host keys; no forwarding; output is data (capped, scrubbed, mentions neutralized, fenced); JSON parsed with size limits; nothing from a VM reaches a shell or the approval parser |
| **A participant's agent posts in an organizers channel with its bot's key.** The agent can read that key (SPEC §12.2: it runs as the same Unix user, and so can the participant). SPEC §12.2 understates what the key allows: Zulip lets a bot send to any channel its *owner* can (`access_stream_for_send_message`: "Bots can send to any stream their owner can"), and accepts `widget_content` from any sender. On the manual path (`workshop/admin/README.md`), the deployer's account owns every participant bot, so every VM could post fake plans, fake results, fake chain heads and fake buttons into any private channel the deployer is in. | The participant bots are owned by the `zab-provisioner` account, which is never subscribed to any admin bot's channel (Q6); each bot checks this at start and hourly. Bot senders are never obeyed. A message from anyone other than a person the bot recognizes or the bot itself gets a `⚠` warning and an audit record (§2.1). A click on a widget posted by another sender is prefixed with that sender's mention, so it is not a command (§2.2). Approvals name a real pending plan and its hash. Bots made by hand from a person's account, as for a fleet launched before its server, can post in every admin channel that person is in until `move_bots.py` gives them to `zab-provisioner` with an organization administrator's key (§8.4; Zulip Cloud refuses it to any other, the bots' owner's included: #758); until then the foreign-sender guard is the only defense. |
| A non-organizer drives a bot | Allowlist of Zulip user IDs; refusals answered and logged |
| An organizer of one workshop acts on another's fleet | Each bot serves one workshop and recognizes only its organizers and the global admins; an approval counts only through the plan's own bot (§2.1, §2.5). The instance role is shared by the account's workshops, so this separation rests on the bots, not on IAM (§5, §11.6). |
| An organizer's Zulip account is compromised | A destructive plan (`!teardown`, `!deactivate-bots`) needs a second, distinct approver, unless a global admin approves it: one global admin's approval completes it alone (D36, #558), so a compromised global admin's account can end a fleet by itself. Identity re-binding is not offered through the bots (#369). Any approver can `!reject` a pending plan; every plan and result is visible in the channel; the account reaches only its own workshop; the allowlists are not editable through a workshop's bot. **Not a defence here:** the typed phrase of a stop, rollout or idle stop of more than half the fleet (§2.5). It stops a blind approval, a click or a pasted line, not an account that can type it; and two plans of half the fleet each need none |
| A plan is approved, then the world changes (roster edited, new instances, another wheel) | The approval binds to the plan hash, which covers the argument lists, the input files and each verb's target set; at apply the targets are recomputed and a difference makes the plan `STALE` (§2.4) |
| Malicious code reaches the server | Signed final tags only. The server checks the signature, that the tag's name is the one requested, and that the version is not older than the running one. Third-party wheels are installed only from `uv.lock`, with hashes, and no build backend runs on the server. The signature certifies the reviewed commit and the deployer's reading of its privileged surface; the rest is the PR reviews' and the tests' (§1.6, Q11, Q12, D31). |
| The instance role is abused against the account's other resources (the account holds others besides the fleet) | Tag conditions on every mutating action, on the fleet-wide `zab-scope`; an explicit deny on resources tagged `zab-admin`; `CreateTags` may not add or change the `workshop` or `zab-scope` key, so nothing of the account's is ever tagged into the scope; an `ec2:InstanceType` condition on `RunInstances`; no `iam:PassRole`, so no VM can get a role (§5) |
| **The waker is compromised** (it reads any Zulip user's messages to the opted-in bots, with their keys) and keeps starting stopped VMs | It can only ask the starter, which starts only stopped fleet VMs, each at most once every 30 s; idle stop stops them again. **The wake monitor** (§1.2, #359) reads the starter's start log, which the waker can't write: the waker can't hide a start *from the log*. A start it asks for of a VM its config doesn't opt in alerts at once, unless the config changed after the start: then opt-in can't be judged, and the start falls back to the message check. Any other alerts unless a message from the VM's participant reached one of its bots in the 10 minutes before. **What the monitor doesn't stop:** the waker holds the same bot keys the monitor reads with. So it can make a start look explained: subscribe the bots to a public channel, or DM the participant as the bot to draw a reply, and any participant message there explains a start for 10 minutes. It can make checks fail, by spending a bot's API rate limit: a "could not be checked" alert deserves the same look as a "no message" one, and the VM's hourly limit then holds its next alerts back (the next one counts and lists them). Starts within 10 minutes of any message the participant's bots received go unnoticed, and so does anything the waker does besides starting VMs, such as reading the opted-in bots' messages. A start the starter refused isn't logged: it changed nothing. **A known false alert:** during a Zulip hiccup the waker retries a message's owner check on every poll while the VM stays stopped, so a legitimate start can come more than 10 minutes after its message, and alert as unexplained. **Another:** a wake already under way when `!waker off` rewrites the config, a start within about 30 s after it, before the waker reloads, alerts as not opted in. |
| The server is lost mid-workshop | Termination protection; encrypted backups to the deployers after every change; break-glass organizer keys on every VM; the manual path of `workshop/admin/README.md` from a deployer's laptop (§1.7) |
| Secrets reach Zulip or a log | Exact-value scrubbing of every secret the server knows, plus the SPEC §11.6 patterns, before any post, log line or console snapshot; the e2e sweeps the channel history and the audit log for them (§7, A12) |

### 1.6 The deploy gate

**What the server runs.** `deploy.sh` installs only the paths that `workshop/adminserver/release-paths` lists (PR 12). The rest of the repository, the bridge included, never reaches the server. The list holds:

- the import closure of `zab.admin`, and of `zab.waker`, which runs there too (#348): `zab.admin` itself plus the few `zab` modules it imports, and what the waker adds. `zab.render.redact` and `zab.render.mentions` import nothing from `zab` (PR 8). The bridge's Zulip transport, which the bot reuses (PR 9), is `zab.zulip.client` and `zab.zulip.budget`, with `zab.core.metrics` and `zab.core.watchdog`, which import only the standard library. The waker adds `zab.config`, `zab.core.store`, `zab.core.types` and `zab.zulip.poster`, and the bridge's reading of a mention, `zab.core.addressing`, with its code protection, `zab.render.code`, which import only the standard library (#683). `httpx` is the one third-party package both need; boto3 is the starter's. `release-paths` lists all of it. Two tests guard it: `tests/admin/test_admin_imports.py` for `zab.admin`, and the deploy tests' walk of the waker's closure from `python -m zab.waker`, as `zab-waker.service` runs it. The self-check imports every module of that closure before the switch (`python -m zab.waker selfcheck`);
- of `workshop/` (no script there imports `zab`), only what the server runs or installs (#432): `workshop/adminserver`; the scripts the bot runs (`zab.admin.checks.SCRIPTS`), `archive_check.py` (its unit), `import_key_pair.py` (`deploy.sh`), `stage_server.py` (§8.4) and `trust_fix.py` (`workshop/admin/README.md` §10, #816); the scripts they import; and what they copy to the VMs or into a repository (`export.sh`, `final_sync_remote.py`, `trust_fix_remote.py` with the AMI's `trust_stores.py`, `agent_instructions.py`, `idle_stop.py`, `warm_cache.py`, `tools_list.py` and the Oct 9 image's list, `sage_wrapper.py`, the AMI's `agent-instructions.md`, `lean-project` and `sage`, `workshop/template`). Not the files host (`workshop/files`, `workshop/waker`), the AMI build, the laptop's `join_server.py` and `move_bots.py`, or the READMEs. A deploy test derives that set from those entry points and fails on a path missing from the list or listed for nothing;
- `pyproject.toml`, `uv.lock` and `build-constraints.txt`.

A module that joins the closure must join that list, or the server's self-check fails: a test checks the list against the closure, and PR 5's test keeps the closure on its allowlist.

**What a signature certifies** (Q11, D31). A signature certifies that this exact commit passed CI, automated code review and the end-to-end suite, and that the deployer read its **privileged surface** themselves: `deploy.sh`, the systemd units, `sysusers.conf`, `tmpfiles.conf`, the nftables rules, the Caddyfile and `render_policy.py` (or the policy it renders, BOOTSTRAP §10). `workshop/adminserver/privileged-paths` is that list, which `deploy.sh --review` marks:
- `workshop/adminserver`, whole: `deploy.sh`, which root runs; what it installs as root (`sysusers.conf`, `tmpfiles.conf`, the nftables rules, every unit, socket, path unit and drop-in, the Caddyfile); `render_policy.py`; both lists. A file added there is privileged at once.
- **Added on Oct 18 (D32):**
  - the root helper's own code and the two modules whose functions it runs as root (`src/zab/admin/confighelper.py`, `config.py`, `checks.py`; §2.9);
  - `pyproject.toml` and `build-constraints.txt`, which decide `deploy.sh --wheel`'s build on the laptop;
  - `uv.lock`, the third-party code in the server's venv, which the deployer reads through the review's summary by package.

The unprivileged code holds strong credentials (the provisioner's Zulip key, the GitHub token, the server's role), so bad code there is caught only by the automated PR reviews and the tests; the IAM policy the deployer reads bounds the AWS side.

**What root runs in the config helper** (§2.9, #437). `zab-admin-config.service` runs `/usr/bin/python3 -I -B -S -c …`: the system interpreter the release's venv is built from (`deploy.sh`'s `PYTHON`), never the venv's, so nothing of the venv runs as root. The venv is `zab-build`'s while third-party code runs during the build, and its `pyvenv.cfg` would choose the standard library even under `-I -S` (#438). The code appends the release's `src/`, resolved once through `current`, to the standard library's path and calls `zab.admin.confighelper.main`. `-S` skips site processing, so no `.pth` line runs and no installed distribution is on the path; `-I` ignores the environment and the working directory; `-B` writes no bytecode. So root runs `confighelper`, the strict loader of `config` (`parse_admin_config`) and the file check of `checks` (`check_file`, `Modes`), the import-time code of `zab` and `zab.admin`, and the standard library, nothing else of `zab.admin`. Of these, the three modules are the privileged list's (D32); `zab/__init__.py` and `zab/admin/__init__.py` hold a version string and a docstring. `tests/admin/test_admin_imports.py` pins the modules (`HELPER_IMPORTS`), statically and by running the unit's own command with its interpreter, and the self-check runs that command from the new release before the switch. It runs inside its unit's sandbox (§1.2): no network, two capabilities, and writes only to its own state, `/etc/zab-admin` itself (for `admin.toml`'s rename) and `secrets/bots/`; the IMDS rule's nftables files, the trust files and the break-glass keys are read-only to it (fixed by #437).

**What runs on the deployer's laptop.** `deploy.sh --wheel` builds the fleet wheel from `pyproject.toml`, `build-constraints.txt`, `src/zab` and the readme, license and `.gitignore` alone. It refuses a `hatch.toml`, and any `pyproject.toml` that configures the build beyond hatchling's own backend and the wheel's packages, such as a build or metadata hook, an in-tree backend, a dynamic field or a `[tool.uv]` table, whose sources would replace the pinned backend; `uv build` also runs with `--no-sources` (#436). So only the pinned hatchling runs there, as the privileged files decide. The runbooks' `uv run` steps also run the tree's code as the deployer: BOOTSTRAP §9's `zab.admin.config check`, and the laptop halves of `join_server.py`, `move_bots.py` and `stage_server.py` (§8.4). Each also syncs the `dev` group and builds the project with whatever hatchling the index serves. Those steps are the PR process's to vouch for, not the privileged read's. `render_policy.py` needs only the standard library, so it runs as `python3 -I`.

**The review.** `deploy.sh --review`, and every deploy, writes the reviewed diff to a file: the release paths' full diff since the last signed tag, with whole every path new to the list and not under a path it held. It records that file's SHA-256 in `/opt/zab-admin/deploys.jsonl`, which the bot copies into the audit log (PR 9). What it shows, and writes beside it, is a review made from that diff, display only:
- one line per file: privileged or not, its status, its lines added and removed, text or binary;
- the privileged files' diff. `uv.lock` and `build-constraints.txt` appear there as data, never as their hash lines (D32). `uv.lock` shows each changed package with its versions and source, flagged when a file changed without its version or the source isn't PyPI. For a package on both sides, every other field that changed is shown too, across a version change (the project's own entry changes version at every release): a dependency, a marker, a group, and the lock's own fields such as `requires-python`. A package new or gone is its own line, and its dependencies are packages of their own. Every name, version, source and field from the lock is printed as JSON, quoted and escaped, so that no string in it can make a line of its own (#435). `build-constraints.txt` shows each changed pin, every line of one name, flagged when its hashes changed without its version. Their raw diff stays in the `.diff` file. Where a summary can't be made (a `python3` without `tomllib`, such as macOS's 3.9, or a file it can't read), the file's raw diff takes its place, with the error on stderr. Which packages the server installs rests on `pyproject.toml`'s `[dependency-groups] admin`, which is privileged and shown whole or as its diff, and on CI's `uv sync --locked`, which fails a lock that disagrees with it; the newly-privileged listing of `uv.lock` names its packages, not their dependency structure;
- whole, once, every file that is privileged at the commit but was not at the base (`config.py`, for instance, at v0.2.5; `uv.lock` and `build-constraints.txt` as every package and pin);
- the rest's diff.

A file is marked privileged when the base's list or the commit's covers it. A commit without the list, after a base that has one, is refused.

**What it refuses**, in place of listing what it couldn't show:
- a binary file outside the privileged paths: a `.pyc`, for one, shadows its source under `-I -B`;
- any `__pycache__/`, `*.pyc`, `*.so` or `*.pth` under the release paths, and any file name outside `[A-Za-z0-9._@+-]`.

It pages with `less` in secure mode, without the input preprocessor of `LESSOPEN`. A tag ruleset on the repository keeps final tags the deployers' (Q12).

**The checks**, made by `deploy.sh` on the deployer's laptop, then again on the server by its root half with the server's own `allowed_signers` and `admin.toml` (and, in-band, by the upgrade's plan helper, then the apply helper and the release's own `deploy.sh`, below):

1. **Signature.** The tag is annotated and signed by a key in `allowed_signers`: `git -c gpg.format=ssh -c gpg.ssh.allowedSignersFile=… verify-tag`.
2. **Name.** The `tag` header of `git cat-file tag refs/tags/<name>` equals the requested name. `verify-tag` checks only the object's signature: without this check, a ref `refs/tags/v0.3.0` pushed to point at the signed `v0.2.2` object would verify, and the plan would say v0.3.0.
3. **Pattern.** The name matches the pattern that `release` names (PR 5a): `final`, `^v\d+\.\d+\.\d+$`, in production, so no `rc` and never a branch; `rc` also allows release candidates, on the test server. The config takes no free regex.
4. **No silent downgrade.** The version is not older than the running one, unless the deployer says `--downgrade` to `deploy.sh`. The in-band upgrade never downgrades (below).

**Installing, without a build backend and without root-owned builds, at the final path.** The venv's `.pth` file and its entry-point shebangs hold absolute paths, so a venv built in a staging directory and then moved would break after validation. The release is therefore built where it will run. Root creates `/opt/zab-admin/releases/vX.Y.Z/`, which must not exist yet, and hands it to `zab-build` for the build. Then, as `zab-build`, in that directory:

- `git archive` of the release paths at the tag, unpacked into it. The server's repository holds an `info/attributes` that turns off every attribute `git archive` would apply (`export-subst`, `export-ignore`, `ident`, `text`, `eol`, `filter`), so a `.gitattributes` in the tree, reviewed or not, changes nothing. Root checks it anyway: every file against its blob and mode, right after unpacking and before anything runs there or is installed from it (the bootstrap's own files included), and again after the build;
- third-party wheels from the tag's `uv.lock`, each checked against its hash: `uv export --frozen --only-group admin --no-emit-project`, then `uv pip install --require-hashes --no-deps --no-build` into a venv on the system Python. Not `uv sync`: it checks the hashes the lock holds, but installs an entry that has none (V9). The lock's `admin` group is every third-party package that `zab.admin` and the scripts of `workshop/admin`, `workshop/aws` and `workshop/adminserver` import, `boto3` and `httpx` today, with their dependencies; a test keeps it so.
- zab itself is not built or installed as a wheel. A `.pth` file in the venv points at that release's own `src/`, by its final absolute path, which has the effect the files host gets from `PYTHONPATH`, and still works under `python -E`. So no `hatchling` code runs on the server.
- `python -m zab.admin selfcheck`, still as `zab-build`. It checks only what that context can: `zab-build` can't read the secrets, a bootstrap runs it before they exist, and it runs under `env -i`, without IMDS, a terminal or new privileges. So: the strict load of `admin.toml`; that every subcommand's modules import from the release's tree and venv (the starter's boto3, the bot's Zulip client and httpx); and the `--help` of every script the grammar uses. The owners and modes of `admin.toml`, the keys and the break-glass files, and each bot's `GET /users/me`, are the bot's to check at its start, which it refuses without them; `python -m zab.admin bot --check` runs them alone, as `zab-admin` (PR 9). `deploy.sh` runs it on every deploy, whatever the release, and with it the waker's `python -m zab.waker selfcheck` and the archive check's `archive_check.py selfcheck` (#374), which re-packs a small archive in memory as its unit runs it; a release whose self-check fails is never switched to.

Only then does root take the tree back (`chown -R root:root`, read-only for everyone else), switch `current` atomically, restart the units, and append an `upgrade` audit record. Nothing is moved after the self-check, so what was validated is what runs. A failed build leaves no `current` change, and root removes the directory.

**Fleet wheels.** These go to participant VMs, not the server. `deploy.sh --wheel vX.Y.Z` builds them on the deployer's laptop from the verified tag with a pinned build backend: `hatchling` at an exact version, and `uv build --build-constraints build-constraints.txt --require-hashes` (V9). It copies them to `releases/wheels/`. `!rollout` can name only a version whose wheel is there.

**A deployer deploys from their machine** for the bootstrap, and for any upgrade the in-band path can't make (below; BOOTSTRAP §13.4 lists them). `workshop/adminserver/deploy.sh --host HOST --etc DIR [--since TAG] [--downgrade] vX.Y.Z`, or with `--wheel` for a fleet wheel (the usage at its top), makes the checks above locally, shows the diff, and drives the steps above over their SSH session, with `sudo` for the root part. That is still a human approval, by the deployer. Bootstrap is the same script with `--bootstrap --secrets DIR`:

- installs `python3 git caddy openssh-client age nftables curl` with apt, and `uv` from its release tarball, pinned by SHA-256;
- creates the users, units, directories and the nftables rule;
- generates the fleet SSH key;
- installs the secrets from the deployer's `--secrets` directory, root:zab-admin 0640: the global admin bot's zuliprc, the provisioner's, the GitHub token, and the zuliprc of each workshop bot that exists at bootstrap. A zuliprc is several lines, which `systemd-ask-password` can't take (PR 12);
- installs `admin.toml`, `allowed_signers` and `backup_recipients` from the deployer's local, uncommitted copies, and the break-glass public keys that `admin_keys` points to (`/etc/zab-admin/break-glass/*.pub`). The global admins and the deployers are named here (Q2), and so are the workshops that exist at bootstrap; later ones come from `!new-workshop` (§2.9);
- has the server import the public half of its fleet key as the EC2 key pair `zab-admin-<ws>` of each workshop, in its region, using the role's `ImportKeyPair`.

Agents write and test these scripts on the test server (§7). They never run them in a workshop account.

**The in-band upgrade: `!upgrade-server vX.Y.Z sha256=<your review's SHA-256>`** (stage 6, #569, from v0.2.19). On the global admin bot (§2.1), requested and approved by a deployer (§2.5's deploy class), as a server plan, which holds every workshop's lock (§2.4 step 5). The deployer reviews on their own machine, as before, and sends the command with the SHA-256 their review printed, once the release's signed tag is in `admin.toml`'s `release_repo`: the public repository, `MathAgents/zulip-agent-bridge`, whose published releases carry it (#958), or a repository of the deployer's own, to which they push it (BOOTSTRAP §13.4). It only goes forward: a rollback is a deployer's `deploy.sh --downgrade`, from their machine (#569; BOOTSTRAP §13.4). A compromised bot process can then have only a correctly signed release of the `release` pattern installed, newer than the running one: the helpers trust nothing in a request but a version of the pattern, the SHA-256 and, for the apply, the commit that the plan helper's own record names. The `sha256=` check keeps a deployer's request to the diff they reviewed; it is no secret (a refused plan shows the right value), so it is no barrier against a compromised bot.
- **Two root helpers, one module** (`zab.admin.upgradehelper`, privileged), each started by a path unit on a request the bot writes (`/var/lib/zab-admin-upgrade/requests/{plan,apply}.json`), and each run as the config helper runs: the system `python3 -I -B -S`, the standard library and `current`'s `src/`. A request is claimed by a rename and deleted, its outcome written to `results/P-n.<action>.json` with the request's nonce.
- **The plan** (`zab-admin-upgrade-plan.service`, sandboxed, §1.2), the plan's dry run and its recheck before the apply:
  1. `admin.toml`'s `release` pattern and `release_repo` (GitHub over SSH; unset, the in-band upgrade is off);
  2. the fetch of exactly two tags, the release and the running one, the review's base, with the root-only deploy key (`secrets/release-key`) and GitHub's pinned host keys (`workshop/adminserver/github-known-hosts`), into the helper's own repository under `refs/fetched/`;
  3. the checks above, again: signature, name, pattern, `pyproject.toml`'s version, newer than the running release, never older (a request naming `downgrade` is refused before anything else); and the running release's tag at the commit `deploys.jsonl` records for it, as `base_is_running` checks. So the running release must have been deployed from `release_repo`'s own commit: one deployed from another repository's commit (the clone the release was first tagged in, say) takes one hand deploy of a published release first (BOOTSTRAP §13.4, *Onto the public repository's commits*);
  4. the review, by the **running** release's `deploy.sh --review C --since <running>`, whose SHA-256 must be the command's `sha256=`: a deployer's request installs only the diff they reviewed, from the base they reviewed it from (no barrier against a compromised bot, which can learn the value from a refused plan). The deployer reviews with that same copy (BOOTSTRAP §13.4);
  5. the tags enter the helper's `refs/tags/`, which never moves one to another commit (a tag signed again for the same commit replaces the copy; one moved to another commit is refused, every time);
  6. the plan's text, which the plan is hashed on: both tags by object, commit, signer and key fingerprint, the SHA-256 and the release tree, the privileged files, whether `render_policy.py` changed (the server never renders the policy, §2.9: the deployer renders and compares it on their machine), the review without its unprivileged rest, and the release's `CHANGELOG.md` section. Git's words and the changelog reach it quoted or with what a terminal hides made visible.
- **The apply** (`zab-admin-upgrade.service`, root, as `sudo` over SSH, with `/usr` read-only: §1.2):
  1. the request held to the plan helper's record of the plan, from the release that runs now;
  2. the checks again on the server's own copies of the tags, with no fetch;
  3. the release's own `deploy.sh`, copied from its commit byte for byte and checked against its blob, with a bundle of its tag, run twice: `--build-wheel`, the release's fleet wheel built as `zab-build` with the pinned backend and placed for `!rollout`, unless one is there (after the IMDS rule's check, as every build); then `--server … --in-band P-n`, which checks everything once more, builds at the final path, switches `current`, records the plan in `deploys.jsonl`, installs the units and restarts them, all but the bots';
  4. the outcome, with whether `current` moved and `deploy.sh`'s last lines.
- **The bots' restart.** The bot hosts the apply, so it posts the result and then restarts itself whenever `current` names another release than it runs, whatever the outcome, holding every workshop's lock until then (§2.9's rule, after `!new-workshop`). The bot runs `current`'s scripts, so it must not run on.
- **Failure.** Before the switch, nothing changes, and the plan says why. After it, the release is current and the result names what `deploy.sh` left undone. A release whose new bot doesn't come up, or any change the in-band path can't make, is deployed with `deploy.sh` (BOOTSTRAP §13.4).
- **What the helpers can't check:** the approvals and the locks, which live in the bot's `state.db`. Root never parses it, since a forged one could say anything; the bot holds the locks.

### 1.7 Backup and recovery

- **What exists only on the server:** each workshop's roster and `out/` (participant bot zuliprcs, `known_hosts`, `spares.json`), `retired-slugs.txt`, the audit log, `state.db`, the workshops' admin bot keys and the fleet SSH key.
- **Protection.** Termination protection, and the server's own tag, outside the role's reach (§1.1).
- **Backups** (`zab.admin.backup`, PR 13).
  - **When:** after every apply during which anything under `ws/` or `retired-slugs.txt` changed: `!roster import`, `!launch`, provisioning, `!adopt-spare`, the ending verbs. A backup is also made after the next apply, whatever that changes, while one is owed: when the last one failed; and at the bot's start, when the files differ from the state the last sent backup recorded in its record (a digest of names, inodes, sizes and mtimes), whatever happened in between (an interrupted apply, a crash), or when applies have run and no backup was ever sent. A daily backup is not built yet (deferred from #355): the audit records written between applies (reads, refusals) wait for the next backup.
  - **What:** `backup-<time>.tar.gz.age`, a gzipped tar encrypted with the `age` binary (D28) to the keys of `backup_recipients`: the deployers' SSH public keys (`age` accepts `ssh-ed25519` recipients, so there are no new keys), or X25519 keys. Its paths are those of the state directory: `ws/`, `/var/lib/zab-admin/ws/`, whole, with owners and modes. That is every workshop's directory (the roster, its `.bak`, `out/`, wherever admin.toml puts them under it), a retired workshop's too, whose entry admin.toml no longer has (§11.5); a `ws/<name>` that is a symbolic link is not followed, and is named. Then `retired-slugs.txt`, and last `audit.jsonl`, read under the log's lock, through the head the DM names. Each member is a directory or a regular file with all its content, never a link, so that GNU tar and Python's `tarfile` read every member. The tar is written straight into `age`: no plaintext reaches the disk, and nothing is left behind.
  - **Where:** the plan's bot uploads the ciphertext, and only it, and DMs its link to each deployer, whether or not they are in its channel. It is ciphertext, so it skips scrubbing.
  - **Who can fetch it.** Zulip serves an upload only to its owner and to users who received a message linking it, or who can see a channel message linking it (`validate_attachment_request`). That is a second layer, not the protection:
    - anyone with the workshop bot's API key can fetch it: the server, the bot's owner in Zulip, and the organization's admins, who can regenerate the key;
    - a deployer who quotes or forwards the DM into a channel opens it to everyone who can read that channel;
    - the Zulip operator, and full exports of the organization, have it.

    So the backups' secrecy rests on `age` and the recipients' private keys.
  - **Old backups are never deleted: no retention.** Every past backup stays retrievable, with the participant bot keys it held. Each is `age`-encrypted to the deployers' keys of its day, so whoever fetches one with the workshop bot's key, or as a Zulip administrator, gets ciphertext. Removing a key from `backup_recipients`, or suspecting one, therefore means rotating the participant bot keys (and the regenerated workshop bot keys hold nothing a backup has), or `!deactivate-bots` at the workshop's end: either leaves the participant bot keys in every old backup dead. The rest of an old backup stays readable to whoever holds that key and the upload: the rosters and their `.bak`, `out/`'s `known_hosts` and `spares.json`, the other files of `ws/`, `retired-slugs.txt` and the audit log.
  - **The record.** Each backup is a `backup` audit record, with the ciphertext's SHA-256 and what was left out. A failure is recorded with `outcome: failed` and said in the plan's topic, after its result: no `age`; a bad `backup_recipients`; an `age` that exits non-zero, stops reading, or writes less than the ciphertext of all it was given; over the upload limit; an upload or a DM refused. It never blocks or rolls back the apply.
  - **Decryption needs a key file.** `age` reads the SSH private key from a file, so a FIDO key (`sk-ssh-ed25519`) or a key that lives only in an agent can't decrypt a backup. Each recipient key must be a plain `ssh-ed25519` key file: the deployer's dedicated key, which is also their break-glass key (Q5).
  - **Left out:** the fleet SSH key (the break-glass keys replace it), and everything in `secrets/`: the global admin bot's key, the provisioner key and the GitHub token, which the deployer holds, and the workshops' bot keys. Those bots' owner regenerates their keys on recovery: the provisioner, since the bootstrap creates its admin bots signed in as `zab-provisioner` (BOOTSTRAP §5.3) and `!new-workshop` creates the others with its key (§2.9). A bot made by hand from another account is that account's to regenerate, until it is given to the provisioner (*Organization settings → Bots*, V15). Inside `ws/`, and named in the DM: whatever could be a private key, by name (`id_*` but `*.pub`, `*_ed25519`, `*_rsa`, `*_ecdsa`, `*_dsa`, their `_sk` forms, `*.ppk`), by place (a `keys/` or `.ssh/` directory inside a workshop's) or by content: a PEM or OpenSSH private key's header, an RFC 4716 one (`---- BEGIN SSH2 … PRIVATE KEY ----`), a PuTTY key, or a file that is one DER private key (PKCS#1, PKCS#8, encrypted PKCS#8, SEC1, PKCS#12). A key re-encoded (base64, UTF-16) is not recognized. Also left out: `exports/` (PR 18's archives of participants' work, up to 4 GB each); symbolic links, which are never followed; and anything else that is not a regular file or a directory, such as a FIFO.
  - It needs no new cloud resource.
- **Break-glass access.** Every VM gets the deployers' dedicated keys for `ubuntu` at first boot (`admin_keys`, through `launch.py --admin-key`: `workshop/aws/README.md`, `launch.py`). A deployer can then reach the fleet without the server.
- **If the server is lost mid-workshop**, the fallback is the manual path:
  1. A deployer decrypts the latest backup into `~/zab-admin` (each workshop's `roster.csv` and `out/`).
  2. The deployer runs the scripts of `workshop/admin/README.md` and `workshop/aws/README.md` from their laptop, with the break-glass key and their own AWS credential for the account (Q1). The break-glass key is their dedicated key (Q5), also as `launch.py --admin-key ~/.ssh/zab-deployer.pub` (BOOTSTRAP §2.1) for any launch, never a personal key.
     The fleet's waker went with the server, so a VM that idle stop stops stays stopped: turn idle stop off from the laptop (`push_config.py --idle-stop 0`, the stopped VMs started first), or run a waker in direct mode (`workshop/waker/README.md`).
  3. A replacement server is bootstrapped later and restored from the same backup, with the keys of the admin bots that the deployer's bundle lacks, those `!new-workshop` made, regenerated. The lost server's key pairs, `zab-admin-<ws>`, go before the new bootstrap imports its own key under the same names (`import_key_pair.py` refuses a name that holds another key), their public half saved first from one of them. The new fleet key is appended to the VMs over the break-glass key, with `workshop/admin/join_server.py`, and the lost server's key, that saved file, removed with its `--remove --server-key`. BOOTSTRAP §13.3 has the commands.

  The exact steps, decryption and restore, are in `workshop/adminserver/README.md`, "Backups and recovery"; `tests/admin/test_admin_backup.py` follows them. A restored audit log passes `audit verify` against the head the backup's DM names, and the new server's bot copies its own `deploys.jsonl` lines into it by digest (#344).
- **If the server may be compromised,** it is cut off and never used again. BOOTSTRAP §13.3 has the steps and their commands; this is what they undo, and why:
  - **The instance role.** Through `StopInstances`, `ModifyInstanceAttribute` (user data) and `StartInstances` it is root on every VM of the scope (§1.2). So the server is stopped through AWS, never over SSH (a sudo password typed on it would be handed over too), and the account owner detaches the role's policies, which stops its credentials wherever they were copied. Detaching holds only until the policies are attached again for a new server, so the role's active sessions are revoked before then.
  - **The keys it held that work from any machine.** The provisioner's key owns every participant bot and every admin bot, so it can read, regenerate or give away their keys: it is replaced at once, and the admin bots' keys with it. So is the GitHub token. The participant bots' keys are regenerated twice. First at once, from a deployer's laptop with the provisioner's new key, so that the copies the server held stop working; the VMs' bridges then hold dead keys, and the agents don't answer, until the new server rolls new ones out. Then again on the new server, since the backup it restores holds the keys of before. Each run is `zulip_provision.py` with `--no-rebind`, and `--hold` while the workshop is held: without them, it could rebind rows, or release a held workshop.
  - **The fleet key's sudo on every VM.** Its public half is saved from one of the key pairs `zab-admin-<ws>`, which are then deleted, so that no launch authorizes it again and the new server can import its own under those names. Over a break-glass key, `join_server.py --remove --server-key <that file>` takes it off each VM; a stopped VM keeps it until it is started. The spares keep it too, since no roster row lists them and `join_server.py` never reaches them: they are torn down instead. The break-glass keys are the way back in: the server never holds them.
  - **What the fleet key reached.** It logs in as `ubuntu`, which has sudo, so the server could read anything on the VMs, the participants' agent sign-ins included. Where that matters, the participants sign their agents out and in again, or revoke those sessions in their accounts.
  - **What the server may have left on a VM,** which removing its key doesn't undo: keys in `~ubuntu/.ssh/authorized_keys*` or another home's `.ssh/`, new users, `/etc/sudoers.d/`, systemd units and timers, the instance's user data, and the idle-stop read user of §4.1 (`zab-monitor`'s shell and groups, `/etc/sudoers.d/zab-monitor`, and root's `status_read_login` and `status_read.sh` in `/usr/local/lib/zab/`, which `push_config.py --idle-stop N` rewrites). Where it matters, the VM is replaced once its work is on GitHub: a final sync and an export (`workshop/admin/README.md` §7.1–§7.2), a new VM, the old one torn down.

### 1.8 No ⏰ for anyone: the waker (#717)

When no participant's message gets a ⏰ or a `⏳ Starting your VM (about 30 seconds)…`, the waker is down. On Nov 1 it was OOM-killed at every start, about 10 s in, for 90 minutes after a config change, and nothing said so (#717). Organizers start VMs meanwhile with `!start alice`, or `all`: the bot doesn't need the waker (ORGANIZERS §4.1).

**How it shows** (#717, `zab.admin.wakerdown`). The waker writes its status file every 30 s, at each start too, with each workshop's `ready_at`: when that workshop's waker last served, once it had run a minute, kept in its store across restarts. A waker killed at every start rewrites the file but never moves `ready_at`. A workshop that the waker's config opts in, whose `ready_at` is more than 5 minutes old, has its waker down since then; a restart for a config change leaves it at most about 155 s old. Then:
- the workshop's bot posts `⚠ waker down since …` once in its `alerts_channel` (topic `waker`), and `✅ waker back` once it serves again; each post is a `waker` audit record (§2.8), so a restart of the bots posts neither again;
- `!status` ends with the same warning, and the console shows it (§4.2).

It doesn't catch a waker that dies after more than a minute at each start, which serves for that minute.
- **A reboot of the server longer than about 3 minutes** gives a down and a back post: the waker's `ready_at` moves only a minute after its start.
- **A waker whose Zulip calls all fail still counts as up:** `ready_at` says that its run lasted, not that its queues work. The journal says why (`Waker Zulip error (…)`, `Waker catch-up failed (…)`).
- **A post** Zulip surely never took is made again on the next check; one that may have landed is not, nor one cut short by the bots' stop, whose record goes first. A post whose audit record the log refuses is made again after the bots' next restart. The deployer, over SSH (BOOTSTRAP §3.6):

1. **The unit:**

   ```sh
   ssh "$HOST" 'systemctl show zab-waker.service -p ActiveState -p NRestarts -p MemoryMax -p MemoryPeak -p MemorySwapPeak'
   ```

   It prints `NRestarts=`, `MemoryPeak=`, `MemorySwapPeak=`, `MemoryMax=` and `ActiveState=`, one a line, in that order: systemd's (255, Ubuntu 24.04's), not the command's. A waker that runs shows `ActiveState=active`, `MemoryMax=536870912` (the unit's 512M) and a `MemoryPeak` under 300 MB (314572800). `NRestarts` counts its restarts since it was last started by hand (a deploy, step 4): one per config change (`!waker on|off`), and no more.
2. **The journal:**

   ```sh
   ssh "$HOST" 'sudo journalctl -u zab-waker.service -n 30 --no-pager'
   ```

   An OOM kill is these lines, again every few seconds, the counter rising (Nov 1's reached 324):

   ```
   zab-waker.service: A process of this unit has been killed by the OOM killer.
   zab-waker.service: Main process exited, code=killed, status=9/KILL
   zab-waker.service: Failed with result 'oom-kill'.
   zab-waker.service: Scheduled restart job, restart counter is at 3.
   ```

   Anything else, such as a traceback's last line or `Workshop <ws>: no waker (<reason>)`: open an issue with those lines, and keep the VMs going with `!start`.
3. **For an OOM kill, Nov 1's mitigation,** until a release fixes the cause. First more memory for the unit:

   ```sh
   ssh "$HOST" 'sudo systemctl set-property zab-waker.service MemoryMax=1G && systemctl show zab-waker.service -p MemoryMax'
   # MemoryMax=1073741824
   ```
