   `set-property` writes `/etc/systemd/system.control/zab-waker.service.d/50-MemoryMax.conf`, which the unit's next start, within 5 s, uses. If step 2's lines come again, add swap: BOOTSTRAP §3.9's two commands, if `ssh "$HOST" swapon --show` prints nothing. The waker's memory may then spill into swap, slowly, instead of being killed. It runs again when step 1, run twice a minute apart, shows `ActiveState=active` and the same `NRestarts`.
4. **Afterwards,** once a release with the fix runs, remove the drop-in, so that the unit's own `MemoryMax` holds again. On a server without it, as every server but Nov 1's, `rm -f` does nothing:

   ```sh
   ssh "$HOST" 'sudo rm -f /etc/systemd/system.control/zab-waker.service.d/50-MemoryMax.conf && sudo systemctl daemon-reload && sudo systemctl restart zab-waker.service && systemctl show zab-waker.service -p MemoryMax -p ActiveState'
   # MemoryMax=536870912, then: ActiveState=active
   ```

   About 90 s later, once it has caught up and run a minute, the same restart count, and its peak:

   ```sh
   ssh "$HOST" 'systemctl show zab-waker.service -p ActiveState -p NRestarts -p MemoryPeak'
   # NRestarts=0 (a restart by hand starts the count again; 1 or more is a restart since), then
   # MemoryPeak= a number under 314572800 (300 MB), then ActiveState=active (systemd's order)
   ```

   The swap file stays, in `/etc/fstab` (BOOTSTRAP §3.9).

## 2. The approval bots

### 2.1 The bots, their channels and the configuration

- **One admin bot per workshop.** The server runs several admin bots, one for each workshop, each a generic Zulip bot named `Workshop admin · <ws>`. A bot is bound to exactly one workshop, and through it to one region and one tag scope.
  - Organizers address their workshop's bot, in their workshop's private organizers channel (`#<ws>-organizers`). They never type a workshop name or a region.
  - A bot acts only on its own fleet. A workshop's organizers are recognized only by that workshop's bot. Global admins (`admins`, Q2) are recognized by every bot and can act through any of them.
  - **The global admin bot,** `Workshop admin`, in the private `#zab-admins` channel, serves the server-level verbs: `!version`, `!audit`, `!new-workshop` (§2.2, §2.9) and `!upgrade-server` (§1.6, #569). It has no roster and takes no workshop verb. It recognizes only the global admins and the deployers. Its keys in `[global]` are optional, all together; without them the server has no in-band server verbs. `[global]` itself is required: it holds the fleet's scope (§5).
  - **A channel belongs to one bot.** Two bots in one channel would both obey a bare `!stop all`, each against its own fleet, so the loader refuses a channel bound to two bots.
  - Each bot reads commands only from its own channels (any topic), with one event queue per bot in `zab-admin-bot.service`. Only `!console` and `!help` work in a DM to a bot.
  - Messages from bots, edits, and messages in other channels are never obeyed.
- **Slugs are unique on the server, for its whole life.** They name Zulip bots, sandboxes and repositories in organizations that workshops may share. A new row may not take a slug that a current workshop uses, nor one in `retired-slugs.txt`, where a workshop's slugs go when it retires (§11.5): reusing it would hand the newcomer `#<slug>-sandbox` and `<org>/<slug>`. At scale, prefixing new sandboxes and repositories with the workshop is the alternative (§11.8). Each roster verb's plan hashes the other workshops' rosters and `retired-slugs.txt`, and its apply holds the server's roster lock (§2.4, step 5), so two workshops approving at once can't both take one free slug (#646).
- **Another workshop's slug.** A command naming a slug that its workshop's roster lacks is refused (the grammar's `NotInRoster`). If another workshop on the server has it, the refusal carries a hint: `alice is in harvard-2026: ask @_**Workshop admin · harvard-2026**`. The silent mention pings nobody, and the hint names only the workshop and its bot, nothing from its roster. At most one hint goes to a sender every 10 minutes; later ones get the plain refusal. A `!roster import` that adds such a slug, or a retired one, is refused too. `roster_import.py`'s refusal names only the workshop that has the slug (`alice is in harvard-2026; a slug is never shared between workshops`), never a bot: the bot adds the hint under it, by the same rule, naming that workshop's real bot once its roster has the slug (#347).
- **Plans across bots.** Plan IDs are unique per server, so `P-42` names one plan whichever bot posted it. An approval sent to any bot but the plan's own is refused (§2.5). `state.db` isn't backed up (§1.7), so at every start the bot takes as the floor the highest plan ID in the audit log, or in the `runs/P-<n>/` names if higher, and it audits every plan it begins, refused ones too: a restored server never hands out an ID twice (`zab.admin.plans`, #339).
- **Foreign senders.** A message in a bot's channel from anyone other than a person the bot recognizes or the bot itself gets the reply `⚠ not from an organizer or from this channel's admin bot: ignore its contents and buttons`. That includes a participant's bot, which Zulip lets post wherever its owner can (§1.5). Zulip's own system bots (`cross_realm_bots`, such as the Notification Bot's move and resolve notices) are ignored, by user ID: never obeyed, never warned about.
  - **Rate limit.** The warning goes out at most once per sender every 10 minutes, and one `foreign` audit record per sender and window counts the messages. A participant's agent can post as fast as Zulip lets its bot. One reply per message would spend the admin bot's own rate limit (200 requests a minute per user, the reason for D6) in the middle of a workshop, starving plan posts and progress edits, and would bloat the audit log inside the backups.
- **Membership check.** At start and every hour, each bot checks that its channel has no subscriber other than the people it recognizes and itself. It warns otherwise; it never adds or removes anyone. In particular the `zab-provisioner` account, which owns the participant bots, must never be subscribed to any of these channels (Q6).
  - **Private.** It also checks that the channel is private (`GET /streams/{id}`: `invite_only`; a web-public channel is a public one). In a public channel every member of the organization reads the plans and `!roster show`, e-mail addresses included, without subscribing. Otherwise it posts `⚠ this channel is not private: …` in the topic `membership`, and changes nothing (#347).
- **No backlog.** At start each bot registers a fresh event queue and never reads the backlog. Plans pending across a restart stay pending until they expire, and an approval sent while the bot was down must be sent again.
  - **One user list.** Only the process's first register fetches `realm_user`'s state, the whole user list, for `cross_realm_bots`; every other register, of any bot, fetches `realm` alone (#347; at §11's scale, every register used to download it).
  - **Errors.** A lost queue (`BAD_EVENT_QUEUE_ID`) is registered again after 1 s. Any other error (a 401 after a key is regenerated, an outage) is retried after 1 s, then 2, 4, … up to 2 minutes (`BACKOFF_CAP`), and at least what the error's `Retry-After` asks, taken up to the same 2 minutes, so that a proxy's 503 asking for an hour can't park a bot that long; an answer resets the wait (#347). That cap is the listener's own wait: a 429's `retry-after` is still honoured in full by the client's request budget, which holds every request of that bot until then, as Zulip's limits require (SPEC §4.10).
- **User lookups.** A stranger's `GET /users/{id}` is kept for 10 minutes; a failed one for a minute (`LOOKUP_RETRY`), so a sender whose lookup fails costs one request a minute, not one per message (#347).
- **The acknowledgement** (#466). A bot adds 👀 (`1f440`, the participant bots' acknowledgement, SPEC §4.7) to every message it takes as a command, as soon as it has parsed it: the reaction is started before the command's read or plan (`!status all` reads every VM over SSH, 5–10 s for 18, and a plan's dry runs can take minutes), though an answer the bot gives at once, with no read or plan, may reach Zulip before it.
  - **Nothing else gets one:** a stranger's message, one the bot doesn't read (another channel, a group DM, an edit, a leading mention of anyone else), talk, a command sent as code (§2.2, #546), or a line the grammar refuses at once with `❌` or the hint. A command the bot takes and then refuses (an approval with the wrong `hash6`, a deployer's `!new-workshop`) has its 👀 and then the `❌`.
  - **It stays** when the answer comes: removing it would cost a second request per command, from the budget the answers share, and would have to follow each command's answer across the planner's tasks.
  - **It is best effort, not a receipt.** A 👀 means the bot took the command; its absence alone doesn't mean it didn't, since a reaction skipped for the budget or failed (below) leaves the command running. Most often, though, a command without one was never read (sent while the bot was down, or not one the bot takes), so the runbook says to wait a few minutes for an answer before sending a change again (ORGANIZERS §2.1, §8.1).
  - **It never delays or fails the command.** It goes out in a task of its own, only while the bot's request budget keeps the third of its window that it leaves to answers (SPEC §4.10, #443); a failure is logged, never raised or retried. Reactions are still never read as input (§0, row 5).
- **`admin.toml`** (example; the real one is never committed). One server, several workshops, each in one region, all in one fleet-wide scope (§5). PR 5a's loader (#321), with #422's `[global]` scope, reads exactly this shape:

  ```toml
  provider = "aws"
  admins = [101, 102]                 # global admins (Q2): the deployer and the lead organizer(s); every bot obeys them
  deployers = [101]                   # SSH and deploy; request and approve !upgrade-server
  destructive_approvals = 2           # Q3; the test server uses 1
  plan_ttl_minutes = 10
  release = "final"                   # "rc" on the test server; never a free regex
  console_url = "https://admin.example.org"             # Q4; its host goes into the Caddyfile

  [global]                            # the fleet's scope, required (§5); the global admin bot
  scope = "example"                   # every fleet resource carries zab-scope=example
  regions = ["us-east-2", "us-west-2"]   # the role's regions, each set up once (§5)
  instance_types = ["m7a.2xlarge"]    # the role launches only these
  tags = []                           # more scope tags, optional (the test server: zab-role=test)
  zuliprc = "/etc/zab-admin/secrets/global.zuliprc"   # the global admin bot's keys: optional
  user_id = 900                       # required on every bot; checked against GET /users/me
  channels = [41]                     # #zab-admins

  [[workshop]]
  name = "harvard-2026"               # its fleet's tag: workshop=harvard-2026, beside [global]'s
  github_org = "example-org"
  refserve_url = "https://files.example.org"
  roster = "/var/lib/zab-admin/ws/harvard-2026/roster.csv"
  out_dir = "/var/lib/zab-admin/ws/harvard-2026/out"
  organizers = [103, 104]             # this workshop's bot only
  mention_after_s = 120               # optional (#711): below
  alerts_channel = 44                 # #harvard-2026-fleet-alerts, the waker's (§3, action 5); optional
  channel = 46                        # its participants' channel, by ID (#627): !roster sync reads its roster there; optional
  gifts_step = "Accept your gift subscriptions: Claude Max through the invitation Anthropic e-mailed you, and ChatGPT Pro through [the workshop's form](https://forms.example.org/harvard-2026)."   # optional (#709): below
  spare_idle_stop = 10                # a spare's own idle stop, in minutes, 10 to 240 (#715; ORGANIZERS §3.7); optional, 10 without
  [workshop.bot]
  zuliprc = "/etc/zab-admin/secrets/bots/harvard-2026.zuliprc"
  user_id = 901
  email = "harvard-admin-bot@example.zulipchat.com"      # optional
  channels = [42]                     # its private organizers channel, where it takes commands
  [workshop.aws]
  region = "us-west-2"                # exactly one, of [global] regions; workshops may differ
  ami = "ami-…"                       # the region's image and group, in the scope (§5)
  security_group = "sg-…"
  instance_types = ["m7a.2xlarge"]    # the first is the default; of [global]'s
  key_name = "zab-admin-harvard-2026" # imported by the server from its own key
  admin_keys = ["/etc/zab-admin/break-glass/zab-deployer.pub"]

  [[workshop]]
  name = "mit-2027"                   # another workshop, in either region
  # … the same keys, with its own bot, channels, organizers, roster, out_dir and region
  ```

  **`gifts_step`** (#709) is the first sentence of step 1 of each sandbox's "Start here", and of its description: the workshop's own words for the gift plans its participants get, such as the e-mail that brings each invitation and the address of a form. "Start here" goes on with `Sign in to Claude and ChatGPT with the accounts that received them.`, so the sentence says what to accept, not how to sign in, and it is for a workshop that gives plans (one that gives none says so in its announcement, ORGANIZERS §3.8). Without it, both say `zulip_provision.GIFTS_STEP`: ``Accept any gift subscriptions: Claude Max (from Anthropic) and ChatGPT Pro (from OpenAI).``
  - **Its rule:** one line, at most 500 bytes in UTF-8, without control characters; neither its first nor its last character is whitespace of any kind, Unicode's included, and the first is not `-`. The loader refuses anything else, and `zulip_provision.py` checks it again.
  - **It is Zulip Markdown,** posted as the participant's Claude bot and shown in each sandbox's description. Keep its Markdown balanced (each backtick and `**` closed on the line), and never put a mention in it: `@**all**` or a person's `@**name**` would notify the participant of every sandbox. Preview it first: paste it into a message to yourself in Zulip, without sending, and read the preview. The plan of `!provision zulip` shows it in its dry run.
  - **How it gets there:** `!provision zulip` and `!adopt-spare` pass it to `zulip_provision.py` as `--gifts-step-b64`, its base64url, since every word of the bot's argument lists is in a safe alphabet (§2.3); a hand run passes it as `--gifts-step`. "Start here" is posted once, so a change reaches only the sandboxes posted after it; the next full `!provision zulip` rewrites every description that differs.
  - **Adding it to a running server's `admin.toml`,** or changing it: a deployer follows BOOTSTRAP §6.5's steps 2 and 4, with the workshop's `gifts_step` line, under its `channel` or `alerts_channel` line, as the one change, where those steps change the regions line. It changes no policy, so step 3 is skipped. Step 4 restarts the bot, which reads the key at its start. Only a server whose release has this key (0.2.17 or later) takes it: an older loader refuses `admin.toml` with an unknown key, and step 4's check stops before it is installed.
  - **`!new-workshop`** makes an entry without it, whatever its `like=`: add the new workshop's afterwards, as above.

  Every value a script needs comes from here: scope tags, AMI, security group, region, instance type, key pair, organization, file server. A workshop's scope tags are `workshop=<name>`, then `[global]`'s `zab-scope=<scope>` and `tags`: every resource its scripts create carries all of them, and every lookup requires all of them. Chat supplies only the choices of §2.2. The bot never relies on the scripts' built-in defaults (`nt-agents-2026`, `us-west-2`, `MathAgents`, `files.mathagents.org`): it passes every flag explicitly.

  **`mention_after_s`** (#711), whole seconds from 0 to 86400, 120 (the bridge's default) when absent: each VM's `[bridge] mention_after_s` (SPEC §4.8). A participant's bot mentions them when a turn fails, needs an approval, or ends after running that long or longer, so a topic that waits on them after such a turn shows **@** in Zulip's left sidebar; at 0, every turn that completes or fails does. Two endings never mention, whatever the value: a turn that a bridge restart ended, whose status message says so instead (SPEC §7.8.1), and a failed sign-in, which posts the `!login` guidance instead (`Session.publish_completion`). The quick-start says 2 minutes: organizers edit their copy where theirs differs (ORGANIZERS §3.8, step 6).
  - **How it gets there:** every `push_config.py` step that installs a release, and so writes `config.toml`, passes it as `--mention-after-s`: `!rollout`'s, whose dry run prints `mention after: 120 s (…)`, and `!adopt-spare`'s two. A VM keeps its value until such a step rewrites its config: after a change, send the fleet's last `!rollout` again, with its release and its words. A rollout without them would set `steer`, `multi-party` and `raw-log` back to their defaults on every VM, a kill switch included (ORGANIZERS §3.5, "Most settings don't stick").
  - **Adding it to a running server's `admin.toml`,** or changing it, by a deployer:
    1. **First, the release.** Deploy 0.2.17 or later to the server (`deploy.sh`, §1.6), if it runs an older one. An older loader refuses an `admin.toml` with an unknown key, and the bots exit at their start on a file they refuse: step 4's check would stop before installing it.
    2. **Then the key.** BOOTSTRAP §6.5's steps 2 and 4, with the workshop's `mention_after_s` line as the one change, where those steps change the regions line. It changes no policy, so step 3 is skipped. Step 4 restarts the bot, which reads the key at its start.
    3. **Then the VMs:** the fleet's last `!rollout` again, as above.
  - **`!new-workshop`** makes an entry without it, whatever its `like=`: 120, until a deployer adds the new workshop's, as above.

  **The loader refuses** an unknown key, a wrong type, and a value that could become a flag. It also refuses:
  - an `admin.toml` without `[global] scope`, `regions` and `instance_types`, or with a workshop's own `tag`: the shape of v0.2.4 and earlier (CHANGELOG 0.2.5), refused rather than guessed;
  - a workshop whose region isn't one of `[global] regions`, or with an instance type `[global]` lacks: the role would refuse its launches;
  - an extra scope tag (`[global] tags`) with a reserved key, in any case: `workshop`, `zab-scope`, `zab-admin`, `Name`, `participant`, `aws:…`;
  - two workshops that share a name, a scope tag, a roster or an `out_dir`;
  - two workshops that share a `refserve_url` (a trailing `/` aside) with different `github_org` values, compared ignoring case (#771): a files host serves one GitHub organization (#693), and `!rollout` writes each workshop's `github_org` into its bridges' `refserve_owner`, so the other workshop's file links would fail. The error names both workshops, their `refserve_url` and their `github_org`. `!new-workshop` copies both from its `like=` workshop, so only a hand edit can split them;
  - two bots that share a zuliprc, a user ID or an e-mail;
  - a channel bound to two bots;
  - an `alerts_channel` that two workshops share, or that is a bot's command channel: the waker's alerts name a fleet's VMs, and its participant bots post them;
  - a `channel`, the participants' channel (#627), that two workshops share, or that is a bot's command channel or an `alerts_channel`: its subscribers become the workshop's roster;
  - a bot's user ID on any allowlist, since bots are never obeyed;
  - a file outside its fixed root (rosters and `out_dir`s under `/var/lib/zab-admin/ws/<name>/`, bot zuliprcs under `/etc/zab-admin/secrets/`, admin keys under `/etc/zab-admin/break-glass/`), or one file in two roles;
  - a workshop that can't complete a destructive plan: no global admin, and fewer than `destructive_approvals` organizers (#558).

### 2.2 Command grammar

```
message     = [ self-mention 1*gap ] "!" verb { SP arg }            ; first line only
self-mention = "@**" <the bot's full name> [ "|" <the bot's user ID> ] "**"
gap         = SP | %xA0 | HTAB                                      ; any run of them (#650)
arg         = slug | "all" | "spares" | version | minutes | size | until | keyword | key "=" value | plan-id | hash6 | ws-name | phrase-word
phrase-word = verb | 1*DIGIT | slug | ws-name                        ; only after !approve P-n hash6, e.g. "stop 35"
slug        = [a-z0-9] ( [a-z0-9-]{0,30} [a-z0-9] )?                ; a roster row (zab-spare-N where allowed)
ws-name     = [a-z0-9] ( [a-z0-9-]{0,45} [a-z0-9] )?                ; !new-workshop only; at most 47 characters
version     = "v" 1*DIGIT "." 1*DIGIT "." 1*DIGIT [ "rc" 1*DIGIT ]    ; the config's `release` decides whether rcN is allowed
plan-id     = "P-" 1*DIGIT
hash6       = 6HEXDIG
minutes     = "0" | 10..240                                         ; push_config.py's minimum is 10
size        = ( "1".."9" ) 0*3DIGIT ( "GB" | "gb" )                 ; !grow-disk only: 1 to 1000 (grammar.MAX_DISK_GB)
until       = 4DIGIT "-" 2DIGIT "-" 2DIGIT [ ( "T" | "t" ) 2DIGIT ":" 2DIGIT ] | "none"
                                                                    ; !keep-until only: a real date, UTC; alone, at 15:00 (#570)
key=value   = "spares=" 0..10 | "idle-stop=" minutes | "steer=" ("on"|"off")
            | "multi-party=" ("on"|"off") | "raw-log=" ("off"|"meta")
            | "region=" region | "like=" ws-name                      ; !new-workshop only
            | "organizers=" 1*DIGIT *( "," 1*DIGIT )                   ; !new-workshop only: user IDs
            | "channel=" ( channel-quote channel-name channel-quote | channel-word )
                                                                    ; !new-workshop only, optional (#627)
channel-quote = DQUOTE | "“" | "”"                                   ; a phone's curly quotes too, either at either end
channel-name = 1*60( channel-char | " " )                           ; single inner spaces only
channel-word = 1*60channel-char                                     ; channel-name without a space, unquoted
channel-char = letter | mark | digit | "-" | "_" | "." | "," | "'" | "&" | "(" | ")" | "+" | ":" | "!" | "?" | "/"
                                                                    ; letter, mark, digit: of any script
region      = 2ALPHA 1*( "-" 1*ALPHA ) "-" 1*2DIGIT                 ; lower case, e.g. us-east-2
```

- **The leading mention.** A message may begin with a mention only if it resolves to the bot's **own user ID**:
  - `@**<its full name>**`, with the name the bot reads from `GET /users/me` at start and from its own `realm_user` update events;
  - or `@**<name>|<its user ID>**`, the form Zulip's `people.get_mention_syntax` emits when two users share a name.

  A message that begins with **any other mention is not a command**, and gets no action. This is what makes a button on someone else's widget inert: in a channel, Zulip's web client prefixes a click's reply with a mention of the widget message's *sender* (`transmit.reply_message`), so a click on a fake widget posted by a participant's bot reads `@**Claude · bob** !approve …`.
- **After the mention** (#650), any run of spaces, no-break spaces (U+00A0) and tabs, then the `!`. Zulip's mention autocomplete ends the mention with a space, and a sender types another; a phone's keyboard or a paste can give a no-break space, a paste a tab. The rendered message shows only a gap, and nothing tells a space from a no-break space or a tab, so the bot reads any run of them as one (`grammar.MENTION_GAP`). The blanks are dropped, never read: the command is what follows the `!`, parsed as a command that starts with `!`, so `@**…**  !!stop all` is the unknown verb `!stop`, never `!stop all`. A mention glued to its command, `@**…**!status`, is refused with ``❌ put a space between the mention and the command``, and any other white-space character there (`str.isspace`: U+3000, U+2028, …) with ``❌ use plain spaces between the mention and the command``. An invisible character that isn't white space (U+200B, U+FEFF, …) leaves the message talk, which gets no answer: the silent, safe side. A human stranger's command after a no-break space, a tab or other white space is then a command like any other of theirs, and gets the `🚫` refusal (§2.4), where it got the `⚠` warning in a channel, or nothing in a DM; a participant bot's message still gets the warning (§2.1).
- **One line.** Only the first line of a message is a command. A roster upload is found as a `/user_uploads/…` link anywhere in the same message.
- **Strict parsing.** Tokens are lower case and separated by single spaces. An unknown verb, an unknown keyword, a repeated or contradictory keyword, or a slug not in the roster rejects the **whole** message, with a one-line reason. Nothing runs.
- **A command sent as code** (#546). A first line that starts with a backtick is not a command, whatever is inside, and that rule is unchanged. A paste often makes one: Zulip's compose box turns a copied code span, or one line copied from a code block, into an inline code span, and a block copied with its newline into a fenced block whose info string is `Text only`, Pygments' name for `text` (`web/src/compose_paste.ts`, its `fencedCodeBlock` rule and `get_code_block_language`). The organizer then sees a line that looks like the bot's, and no answer. So:
  - **What gets the hint.** A message from a sender the bot recognizes, in a place it reads (§2.1), that the grammar finds no command in, and that is, whole, one inline code span or one fenced block of one line, fenced with backticks and with no info string, `text` or `Text only`, with only blank space around it, whose text starts with `!` and a verb of the table below (`grammar.code_wrapped`). It gets one fixed answer, which never quotes the message: ``❌ not run: your message is formatted as code, so it is not a command. Send it as plain text, starting with `!`: copy it with the code block's copy button (top right), or paste it and press Ctrl+Z (Cmd+Z on a Mac) once to undo the formatting. On a phone, type it.`` Every such message gets it, with no window: only senders the bot already answers can send one, and silence on a second try is the very failure this fixes. No 👀, and no audit record. Its two ways out are Zulip's own: a code block's copy button copies plain text (`web/src/rendered_markdown.ts`), and `compose_paste.ts`'s `do_paste_text` writes a formatted paste in two steps, the plain text first, "so that undo restores the pre-formatting syntax": one Ctrl+Z (Cmd+Z) leaves the bare line.
  - **What doesn't.** Any other code (a snippet, a block of several lines, a `quote` or `spoiler` block, a command quoted inside a sentence, a word that is not a verb of this table, such as a participant bot's `!login`) gets no answer. A stranger's message gets what any stranger's gets (§2.1), never the hint.
  - **Why the boundary holds.** The bot asks `code_wrapped` only after `parse` has returned `NotCommand` for the message as it was sent, and returns right after the hint. `code_wrapped` returns a bool, never the text inside the code, so nothing from a code span reaches `parse`, a plan, an approval or a script: the hint is not a second parser. `parse` itself is unchanged, and a test checks that it only ever reads such a message as sent.
- **No workshop token.** No command names a workshop to act on, or a region: a workshop's bot acts on its own workshop (§2.1), and the global admin bot takes only the server-level verbs, never a workshop's. The one exception is `!new-workshop` (stage 7, #418), on the global admin bot alone, which names the workshop it creates and one to copy settings from (§2.9).
- **Reserved words.** `all`, `spares` and every bare keyword (`hold`, `protect`, `lean-shared`, `restart-codex`, `invites-only`, `no-invite`, `unsynced`, `export`) are keywords wherever a slug may stand, so no roster slug may be one of them; nor may a slug look like `zab-spare-N`. `!roster import` refuses such rows.
- **Selections are explicit.**
  - A fleet-wide action needs the word `all`, which means every participant row and never the spares.
  - `spares` means every spare in `spares.json`, and only where the verb allows it.
  - An empty selection is an error. There is no implicit "everyone".

| Verb and arguments | Class | Runs | Bot | Stage (§8.1) |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `!help [verb]`, `!version`, `!plans`, `!plan P-n` | read | the bot itself; a bot lists only its own plans | any | 1 |
| `!audit [n]` | read | the bot itself: a workshop's bot shows that workshop's records; the global admin bot, every record | any | 1 |
| `!status SEL` | read | `status.py --json`, shown as the plans' status table and a few readable lines, never as JSON (#439); and the workshop's cost estimate (§11.2) | workshop | 1; cost with 5 |
| `!roster show [slug]` | read | the bot itself | workshop | 1 |
| `!console` (DM or channel) | read | a one-time sign-in link, by DM (§4.4) | any | 5 |
| `!roster import` + an attached CSV | change; a change of a provisioned row's `email` or `github` would be a **rebind**, which it refuses (§3.1); one that changes no row, no plan (§2.4 step 2, #708) | `roster_import.py` (new) | workshop | 1 |
| `!roster sync` | change: the roster from the workshop's channel (§3.1a, #627); with nothing to change, no plan (§2.4 step 2, #708) | `roster_sync.py --sync` (new) | workshop | 1 |
| `!roster remove slug`, `!roster restore slug`, `!roster rename slug new-slug` | change: one row, nothing provisioned for it (§3.1a) | `roster_sync.py` (new) | workshop | 1 |
| `!provision zulip [hold] SEL` | change; always with `--no-rebind`, so it binds a newly joined participant but never replaces an existing binding | `zulip_provision.py` | workshop | 1 |
| `!provision repos [invites-only \| no-invite] SEL` | change; always with `--no-rebind`, so it never takes repository access away from a login that has accepted it; `invites-only` with nothing to change, no plan (§2.4 step 2, #708) | `provision_repos.py` | workshop | 1 |
| `!launch SEL [spares=N] [protect]` | change | `launch.py`; with spares, then `spare_idle_stop.py` (new, #715); then `scan_hosts.py` | workshop | 1 |
| `!protect on\|off SEL` | change | `protect.py` | workshop | 1 |
| `!rollout VERSION SEL [steer=on\|off] [lean-shared] [idle-stop=N] [restart-codex] [multi-party=on\|off] [raw-log=off\|meta]` | change; phrase above half the fleet | `push_config.py`, then `status.py --json`. Steering is on by default (D25): `steer=off` passes `--no-claude-steer`, `steer=on` passes `--claude-steer`, and without it neither is passed. Without `lean-shared`, `--lean-shared-default`; without `idle-stop=`, `--idle-stop-default 60`, with `--has-waker S=INSTANCE` for each target whose waker is on (action 4). | workshop | 1 |
| `!idle-stop N SEL` | change; phrase above half the fleet | `push_config.py --idle-stop N` | workshop | 1 |
| `!start SEL\|spares` | change | `power.py` (new); with spares, then `spare_idle_stop.py` (new, #715) | workshop | 1 |
| `!stop SEL\|spares` | change; phrase above half the fleet | `power.py` (new) | workshop | 1 |
| `!adopt-spare slug zab-spare-N` | change | `adopt_spare.py` → `scan_hosts.py` → `zulip_provision.py` → (`push_waker.py`, when the fleet's waker is on) → `push_config.py --adopt-spare` → `provision_repos.py` → `push_config.py` | workshop | 1 |
| `!grow-disk slug NGB` | change: one row, a size of 1 to 1000 GB (#553) | `grow_disk.py` | workshop | 1 |
| `!keep-until DATE\|none SEL` | change: each row's `keep_until`, a kept VM's end (§11.5, #570); when every row named has that date already, no plan (§2.4 step 2, #708) | `keep_until.py` (new) | workshop | 8 |
| `!final-sync SEL` | change | `final_sync.py` | workshop | 3 |
| `!export SEL` | change | `collect_exports.py` (PR 18) | workshop | 3 |
| `!uninstall SEL` | change; phrase above half the fleet | `push_config.py --uninstall` | workshop | 3 |
| `!teardown SEL\|spares\|zab-spare-N [unsynced]` | **destructive** | `teardown.py --yes` | workshop | 3 |
| `!deactivate-bots SEL` | **destructive** | `zulip_provision.py --deactivate` | workshop | 3 |
| `!retire slug… [export] [unsynced]` | **destructive**; named rows only, never `all` (#559) | the separate verbs' scripts, each row alone (§3.3) | workshop | 3 |
| `!month-end schedule` | **destructive**: every row in service with a `keep_until`, never named (§11.5, #570) | none at its apply, which stores the schedule in `state.db`; the teardown's dry run in its plan | workshop | 8 |
| `!month-end show` | read: the schedule (§11.5); cut to fit Zulip's limit, with a count of its rows by state, the whole attached (`month-end.txt`), as `!status` (#767) | the bot itself | workshop | 8 |
| `!month-end cancel SEL` | a veto, as `!reject` is a plan's: at once, from an approver of the workshop (§2.5), audited (`month_end`); it only takes rows out of the schedule (§11.5). Answered `busy:` while a `!month-end schedule` of the workshop is approved or applying, whose apply would bring the rows back | the bot itself | workshop | 8 |
| `!rebind slug [github]` | **rebind**. Not built, and not planned (#369): the bot answers it as not offered | `zulip_provision.py --rebind SLUG=OLD:NEW`, or with `github` `provision_repos.py --rebind SLUG=OLD:NEW`, flags not built either (§2.5, §3.2) | workshop | 3, not planned |
| `!waker on\|off SEL` | change | `push_waker.py` (local) | workshop | 4 |
| `!upgrade-server VERSION sha256=<your review's SHA-256>` | **deploy**: requested by a deployer; `sha256=` is the deployer's own review's, which the plan must match; forward only, `downgrade` refused with the way back (#569) | the upgrade's two root helpers, through `upgrade_server.py` (§1.6) | global | 6 |
| `!new-workshop NAME region=R like=WS organizers=ID,… [channel="NAME"]` | **server** | the provisioner's key, then the config helper (§2.9) | global | 7 |
| `!approve P-n hash6 [phrase…]`, `!reject P-n` | (approval) | §2.5; only on the plan's own bot | any | 1 |

`SEL` is `all`, or one or more slugs of the bot's own workshop. E-mail addresses appear only in the organizers channels (in `!roster show`, `!status` and plans), never in the console's page titles or in logs outside the server.

Left out on purpose: `--claude-raw-log content` (the participant's work), `--regenerate-keys`, `--force` of `provision_repos.py` and `adopt_spare.py`, `scan_hosts.py --replace`, `--refserve-url`, and any free-text argument. Each stays a deployer's job over SSH, with the scripts as today.

### 2.3 Parameter validation

The bot builds every argument list itself, as `list[str]`, and runs it with `subprocess` and `shell=False`. Every value comes from one of three places:

- **Config** (`admin.toml`): paths, tag, AMI, security group, region, instance type, organization, URLs, always from the entry of the bot's own workshop; and its `gifts_step`, free text, which goes base64url-encoded (§2.1).
- **The roster:**
  - a slug is accepted only if it matches the slug regex **and** names a roster row;
  - `zab-spare-N` and `spares` are accepted only by `!adopt-spare`, `!start`, `!stop` and `!teardown`, and only for spares listed in `spares.json`.
- **The grammar:** keywords map to fixed flags, through a table in `src/zab/admin/actions.py`; integers are range-checked; versions match the pattern that the config's `release` names.

No token from a message is ever copied into an argument list as it stands: it is looked up, and the looked-up value is used. A slug like `--yes` or `a;b` fails the regex before it gets near a list.

- **Interpreter and paths.** Script paths are absolute, under `/opt/zab-admin/current/`. The interpreter is the release venv's `python`, with `-E -s -B -u`: no `PYTHON*` variables, no user site, no bytecode written into the release, and output as it comes, for the progress line (`-E` would ignore `PYTHONUNBUFFERED`). It is not `-I`, because the scripts import their sibling modules from the script directory. `PYTHONPATH` is not used: the bot and the scripts find `zab` through the venv's `.pth` file, which points at the release's `src/` (§1.6).
- **Environment,** built by the runner, never inherited (`zab.admin.runner`):
  - `PATH=/usr/bin:/bin`, `HOME=/var/lib/zab-admin`, `LANG=C.UTF-8`;
  - `TMPDIR=/var/lib/zab-admin/tmp/P-<n>`, also the working directory: a fresh directory for each run of a plan's scripts (its dry runs, then its apply), removed after (§2.4);
  - `GH_TOKEN`, for `provision_repos.py`; for `roster_import.py`, which only reads a recorded login's access (§3.1); and for `final_sync.py` (`!final-sync`, `!retire`), which only reads whether each row's repository still exists (#714): a repository GitHub names at another owner's is gone at once, with no confirmation, unlike the files host's rule, since a gone row is only reported, never torn down. A step that names the token fails when `secrets/github-token` can't be read, so `!final-sync` and `!retire` now need it, as `!provision repos` does (BOOTSTRAP §8.1 installs it);
  - stdin is `/dev/null`, and each run leads its own process group, which the runner kills whole: at its timeout, when the bot stops, and whatever its leader left behind. A process that leaves the group (`setsid`) is beyond it: the run waits 5 s for the output it holds, and the unit's cgroup ends it when the bot stops: with `KillMode=mixed` (`zab-admin-bot.service`), the SIGKILL that follows the bot's exit, or `TimeoutStopSec`, reaches every process left in the unit.
- **AWS** access comes from the instance role, through boto3's default chain. There are no AWS keys anywhere.
- **SSH** uses the default identity in `$HOME/.ssh`, and every script gets `--known-hosts /var/lib/zab-admin/ws/out/known_hosts`.

### 2.4 Plan, approval, apply

```
request ──▶ PLANNING ──▶ PENDING ──(approvals complete)──▶ APPLYING ──▶ DONE | FAILED | INTERRUPTED
                │            │ ├── !reject (any organizer) ──▶ REJECTED
                │            │ ├── TTL passed ─────────────▶ EXPIRED
                │            │ └── targets differ ─────────▶ STALE (at apply start)
                ├── invalid ───────────▶ REFUSED
                └── nothing to change ─▶ UNCHANGED (step 2, #708)
```

1. **Request.** An organizer sends a command.
   - If the sender isn't on the allowlist, the bot answers once, `🚫 <name> is not an organizer here; nothing was done`, and logs `refused`. As for foreign senders (§2.1), it answers at most once per sender and bot every 10 minutes, and one `refused` record per window counts the commands (`messages`, `first`, `last`). The record is written when the window closes, or when the bot stops. Anyone in the organization can DM a bot, and one record or reply per message would let a flood bloat the audit log and its backups, and spend the bot's rate limit.
   - A grammar or validation error gets a one-line answer, and nothing more.
2. **Plan.** The bot runs every step's dry run (§3 lists which flag that is). For actions on VMs it also runs a read-only `status.py --json --only …` of the targets, for their current state, zab version and running turns. It posts **one plan message** in the request's topic:
   - **Header:** `P-42 · harvard-2026 · rollout v0.2.2 · requested by Alice · expires 14:12 ET`.
   - **Targets:** their slugs.
   - **The exact commands,** as code: one argument list per step.
   - **The dry-run output,** scrubbed (§2.7). After 40 lines it is cut. `push_config.py --dry-run` prints one line per VM, which names each command and the files it sends (#361), so a few VMs' rollout fits.
   - **The status table,** from the status read, outside the dry-run block: one line per target VM, with its slug, state (`active`, `stopped`, `unreachable`, …), zab version, idle stop and running turns (`0`, `1 claude`; `?` when the VM's zab can't say). It is built on the server from `status.py --json`, scrubbed with its escapes undone (§2.7, JSON in script output), and each cell is scrubbed again before it is cut. After 50 lines, its header included, it is cut, so that a workshop of about 40 VMs (§11) shows whole (#764). The design's warnings in words ("bob: 1 Claude turn running; the bridge restart interrupts it") are not written yet: the running-turns column carries that fact.
   - **The hash:** `plan 3f9a2c…`, and how to approve, including the typed phrase where one is needed.

   The header, the hash with the approve and reject commands, and the chain head are never cut. When the rest doesn't fit, in 40 lines of dry run, 50 of status table and Zulip's message limit, the message says what it leaves out, and the whole plan is attached as a `.txt` upload: the targets, the commands, the dry run, the status table, and the status read as `status.py` printed it. A one-VM `!stop` and a three-VM `!rollout` fit with no attachment; `!stop all` on 120 VMs is cut.

   Right after it comes a second, short **approval message**: the `zform` widget where it renders, or, for a plan that needs a typed phrase, the line to send, in a code block of its own (§2.5).

   **Nothing to change** (#708). A verb whose script can say so for certain (`actions.Verb.nothing`, of one step) has its dry run exit 3 (`actions.NOTHING`) when there is nothing to change: `!roster sync`; `!roster import`, whose upload then changes no row; `!provision repos invites-only`, whose dry run then has nothing to invite or withdraw, no acceptance or other state to record (so no roster write) and no failure; and `!keep-until`, when every row it names has that date already (#570). The plan is then neither posted nor refused: it becomes `UNCHANGED` (`plans.UNCHANGED`, its note `nothing to change, as its dry run says`), the audit log records `unchanged`, never `refused`, and the bot posts one message, a success with its own mark, `☑` (U+2611, `planning.UNCHANGED_MARK`; the scrubber deletes a variation selector): ``☑ **P-42** · `!roster sync`: nothing to change, as its dry run says; no plan to approve``, then the dry run, scrubbed and fenced like a refusal's, and the chain head. A check that can't tell never ends in "nothing to change": `roster_sync.py` refuses a sync whose only news is a row whose holdings can't be checked; `provision_repos.py` takes the GitHub writes it would make from `grant_access` itself (`InviteResult.writes`), never from the state's words, which can quote a participant, and compares a state as the roster stores it (`STATE_CHARS`); `roster_import.py`'s "no row changes" needs no live check. `!provision repos` says so only with `--invites-only` (`Verb.nothing_flag`). Exit 3 from any other verb's dry run, or from a step of a plan of several, is a failed dry run, refused as before. A failed dry run, a refusal, and a dry run that can't tell keep `❌`. The other verbs post their plan whatever their dry run shows (`!waker on all`, for one, is recorded by its apply for `!adopt-spare`: `fleet.Ledger`, ORGANIZERS §3.5), and `!rollout` and a full `!provision zulip` reinstall or reset something on purpose.
3. **Plan hash.** SHA-256 of the canonical JSON of:
   - the server's release and commit;
   - the workshop and the user ID of the bot that posted the plan;
   - the verb and its normalized arguments;
   - each step's argument list;
   - the input hashes: the current roster file, the uploaded CSV, the wheel;
   - the verb's **target material**, from this table:

   | Verbs | Target material in the hash |
   |---|---|
   | `launch`, `adopt-spare`, `start`, `stop`, `protect`, `grow-disk` | the target set, computed by the bot, not parsed from output: for each target, the IDs of the VMs a script could act on, those tagged with its name and those at its address (its roster `ip`, or a spare's in `spares.json`, as `power.py` finds them; `provider.targets`), the spare names, the bot user IDs from `out/` |
   | `deactivate-bots` | the bots' identities from `out/`: each named row's `out/<slug>/claude.zuliprc` and `codex.zuliprc`, among the inputs (their SHA-256, or none), so that a bot provisioned or re-keyed between plan and apply makes the plan `STALE` |
   | `rollout`, `idle-stop`, `uninstall`; `teardown`, whose dry run lists what it would terminate, keep (protected), skip and release: its lines sorted and each instance's state dropped (`actions.normalize_teardown`), so that a VM protected or unprotected, or an address gained or lost, between plan and apply makes the plan `STALE`, and idle stop doesn't | the normalized dry-run output. It prints only commands, so it is deterministic once normalized (below). |
   | `retire` (#559, §3.3); `month-end schedule` (#570, §11.5) | each row's VMs, as for `launch`, and the teardown's dry run of every row, normalized as `teardown`'s; for the schedule, the roster (so each row's `keep_until`) and the rows' bot keys are among the inputs, as for `retire` |
   | `waker` | the normalized dry-run output of `push_waker.py --config-dir`: the workshop's waker entry as it will be written (each VM's instance ID, the allowlists, the key paths), and nothing of another workshop's |
   | `roster import`; `roster remove`, `roster restore`, `roster rename` (#627) | nothing more: the upload and the current roster are already inputs, with the other workshops' rosters and the retired slugs. No live Zulip lookup is hashed (#589): the scripts check Zulip at their dry run and again at apply, which refuses what changed since |
   | `keep-until` (#570) | nothing more: the roster is an input, and the rows and the date are the arguments. The bot checks the date against its clock at plan time and again at apply, when it builds the plan's action again (`Planning.rebuild`): an end passed in between makes the plan STALE |
   | `roster sync` (#627) | the whole dry-run output, not normalized (`normalize_exact`): every row it adds, renames, removes, leaves alone or lists as excluded, so that the apply makes exactly the approved changes; a join between plan and approval makes the plan `STALE` |
   | `provision zulip`, `provision repos`; with stage 3, `final-sync` and `export` | **nothing more: these are reconcilers.** Their dry runs report live state (who has joined Zulip, which invitations are pending or accepted), which changes by the minute in a workshop's first hour, while the participants accept their invitations and sign in. The dry run is shown but not hashed, so the plan doesn't turn `STALE` whenever someone accepts an invitation. The scripts are idempotent and reconcile whatever they find at apply. **Identity is not such state.** `zulip_provision.py` always runs with `--no-rebind`: it may bind a row that has no `zulip_user_id` yet, by the roster e-mail that the approved roster holds, but it never replaces an existing `zulip_user_id`. A row whose e-mail now matches another Zulip user, or none, is reported (`binding changed: refused`) and left untouched. The same holds for GitHub: `provision_repos.py --no-rebind` invites a row's first login, and may replace a login whose invitation is still pending (a participant correcting a typo in their Zulip GitHub field, the flow of `workshop/admin/README.md` §1b, while the old login never had access). It never removes a login that has collaborator access (checked live on GitHub). Such a row is reported (`login changed: refused`). Whatever happens between plan and apply, a reconciler can't hand a participant's VM or repository to someone else. |

   **The status read is shown but never hashed** (#361 kept this, and chose it over hashing the table). Load and memory change by the second, and running turns start and end by the minute: a hash of them would make plans `STALE` for nothing during a workshop. The apply doesn't read the status again either, so a hash of it would guard nothing. The rule that the approver sees everything the hash covers therefore concerns the target material above, never the table: a hashed dry run is shown whole, inline or in the attachment, or the plan is refused (the runner's `KEEP // 2`). The status read's whole output is kept in `runs/P-<n>/dry-run.txt`, after the dry runs, and attached when the plan is cut.

   **Normalization contract**, for the verbs whose dry run is hashed:
   - **Order.** Dry runs always run with `--parallel 1`, so rows print in roster order, not completion order.
   - **Generated paths.** Paths under the plan's `TMPDIR`, such as `push_config.py`'s `zab-push-…` directories in `--dry-run --verbose`'s `scp` lines, become `<tmp>`. Its default dry run names each file as the VM gets it, and a bot key by its `out/` directory, so it prints no such path.
   - **What `push_config.py`'s dry run binds.** Its line per VM names the VM's address, each command in order, and each file sent. The commands' text depends on the release, the flags and the roster row, which the hash covers anyway, and on which `out/` directory the keys come from, which the line shows (`alice/codex.zuliprc`, or a legacy `Alice/codex.zuliprc`). Before, the hashed text also bound each command's literal text and the release's paths; now that binding rests on the release string, which the deploy gate makes unique per tree (§1.6). A command it can't name is shown cut (`ssh …`), never left out, and a test requires a name for each of the script's own.
   - **Times.** Timestamps and durations are removed.
   - **Tests.** Each verb's normalizer lives next to its argument-list builder in `actions.py`. A test runs every hashed verb's dry run twice on identical inputs and requires the same hash. For each reconciler, a test checks that a changed live state with the same inputs keeps the hash (§8, PR 6).
4. **Approve.** §2.5 says who may approve and how.
5. **Apply.**
   - **Lock.** The bot takes its workshop's apply lock: one apply at a time per workshop, so that workshops don't wait on each other, except for the roster lock (below). An approval that arrives while another plan of the same workshop applies is answered with "busy: P-41 is applying" and not recorded. It can be sent again before the plan expires. The server verbs `!new-workshop` and `!upgrade-server` need every workshop's lock, so each is refused while anything applies. Read commands are never blocked.
   - **The roster lock** (#646). An apply of a roster verb (`!roster import`, `sync`, `remove`, `restore` and `rename`) also takes the server's one roster lock, after its workshop's apply lock and never before it, and holds it from the hash's recheck (*Staleness*, below) to the apply's end. Slugs are unique on the server (§2.1), and each such plan hashes the other workshops' rosters and `retired-slugs.txt`: under the lock, the recheck reads them again, and the script checks its slugs against them at apply, while no other roster verb runs. The other verbs write other columns of their own workshop's roster, never a slug. Of two workshops that approve at once, the first applies, and the second, whose inputs the first changed, goes STALE and is requested again.
     - **The wait.** A plan that waits for the lock says so in its topic, once: ``⏳ P-43 waits for P-42 of zab-test: slugs are unique on the server, so roster plans apply one at a time. Meanwhile this workshop answers its other approvals with `busy:`.``, with `and <n> more` after the workshop when <n> more plans wait before it. It holds its workshop's apply lock while it waits, as the order requires, so every other approval in its workshop is answered `busy: P-43 is applying` until it has applied, though it hasn't begun: for as long as the roster applies before it last, each its recheck, up to 15 minutes of steps, its result and its backup.
     - **Past the TTL.** A plan whose TTL passed while it waited is EXPIRED, not STALE, whatever the first apply changed: the bot checks the TTL once it has the lock, before the recheck, and says `❌ P-43 is expired (not applied within the TTL): nothing was done. Request again.`
   - **Staleness.** The bot recomputes the hash: the inputs, plus the target set or the normalized dry run, whichever the verb uses. On any difference the plan becomes `STALE`, the bot posts the difference, and the organizer requests again. The difference is named from what the runner kept at plan time: the release, the commands, each input file, each target's VMs (`alice: i-1 → i-9`), a teardown's rows' states (`alice: synced after its uninstall → uninstalled, not synced since`, from `Action.ends`, #376), the dry run.
   - **Steps.** The real steps run in order and stop at the first failing exit. `apply_start` comes before the first, and `apply_end` after the last however the apply ends. The bot edits one progress message at most once a minute and at most 19 times (D6, D41; #754), with the step and the last whole line of the output so far (stdout and stderr merged), from everything so far scrubbed whole, and only when they change: it shows no time, and the result says how long the apply took. Its 19th edit says that the progress is paused and that the result follows; an edit within a minute of the last one, or past the 19th, is dropped, since the next holds everything so far. Then it posts the result: the outcome with the exit code and the duration, the last 40 lines of the scrubbed output in a code block (the script's own table), the whole of it attached when that cuts it, and `audit #1234 · chain a1b2c3d4e5f6` (§2.8). The post is tried three times, 2 s and then 4 s apart, but only while each try surely never landed: Zulip refused it with a 4xx answer, or no connection was made. Any other failure may hide a post that landed (a 5xx, from a worker killed after the commit or a proxy; a 2xx whose body can't be read; a response lost after the request went out), so the result is not sent again. It may then be missing from the topic, never doubled: a Zulip that answers 5xx with nothing stored gets no retry either. When Zulip is down, the outcome is still in the audit log (`apply_end`) and `runs/P-<n>/apply.txt`, and the progress message stays as it was. A result whose `apply_end` could not be written says so, without a chain head.
   - **Timeouts:** 60 min for `launch`, 30 min for `rollout`, `final-sync`, `export` and `teardown` (whose retries of a VM that EC2 still refuses as protected take up to 5 min, #378), 15 min otherwise, for all of an apply's steps together; `retire`'s grows with its rows, 60 min or 20 min a row, whichever is more, and 55 more a row with `export` (§3.3); a plan's dry runs and `!status` get the same. On a timeout the bot sends SIGTERM to the script's process group, waits 30 s, then sends SIGKILL, and reports it.
6. **Restart.** If the bot restarts mid-apply, the plan is marked `INTERRUPTED`, in the channel and in the log. On systemd's stop (SIGTERM to the bot alone, `KillMode=mixed`), the runner stops each running script's process group within 5 s and writes `apply_end` with the outcome `interrupted`; the next start marks the plan. Nothing resumes on its own: the scripts are idempotent, and a fresh request reconciles. A hard stop (a kill, a crash) writes no `apply_end`; so each step of a `!retire`'s row is on the audit log as it ends, a `retire_step` record, from which the ledger marks the row in place (uninstalled, synced, retired), and one that can't be written stops the apply, as an `apply_start` that can't does (#837). The next start's message for a cut `!retire` says what was done to each row and what is left, from those records. The automated month-end's runs are the one exception (§11.5): a run a restart interrupts is resumed by the bot's next look, each row at its first unfinished step. `!cancel` is not built (§8.3, P-d).

### 2.5 Approval rules

- **Who.** The **approvers** of a workshop's plan are its `organizers` and the global `admins`. An approval counts only if:
  - it comes from an approver of the plan's workshop (`deployers` for the deploy class, global admins for the server class);
  - that user is active and human at approval time (`GET /users/{id}`);
  - it is sent to the plan's own bot, in that bot's channel. Plan IDs are unique per server, and an approval on another bot is refused;
  - its message ID is greater than the plan's approval message's ID;
  - it arrives through the live event queue (§2.1).

  Anything else is refused with a reply and an audit record, and the plan stays pending. The e2e's `zab-tester2` exercises exactly this.
- **Binding.** `!approve P-42 3f9a2c` must name a pending plan and the first six hex digits of its hash. The full hash is checked again at apply (§2.4, step 5).
- **Expiry.** `plan_ttl_minutes` (10) from the plan's post. After that the plan is `EXPIRED`: request again.
- **Classes:**

  | Class | Approvals | Typed confirmation | Requester may approve | Stage (§8.1) |
  |---|---|---|---|---|
  | change | 1 approver | no | yes | 1 |
  | change of more than half the fleet (`!stop`, `!rollout`, `!idle-stop`; with stage 3, `!uninstall`), however the selection is written, as `all` or as a list of slugs | 1 approver | `!approve P-n hash6 <verb> <count>`, e.g. `stop 35` | yes | 1 |
  | destructive (`teardown`, `deactivate-bots`, `retire`, `month-end schedule`) | `destructive_approvals` distinct approvers (2, Q3), or a global admin alone (#558) | `<verb> <count>`, e.g. `teardown 3`; `teardown <count> unsynced` or `retire <count> unsynced` when the command says `unsynced` (§3, action 7; §3.3); `month-end <count>` for the schedule (§11.5) | yes, as one of them | 3; the schedule, 8 |
  | rebind (a change of a provisioned row's `email` or `github` through `!roster import`; or `!rebind slug [github]`, which replaces an existing `zulip_user_id` with `zulip_provision.py --rebind SLUG=OLD:NEW`, or a GitHub login that has access with `provision_repos.py --rebind SLUG=OLD:NEW`, the old and new identities in the hash) | as destructive | `rebind <slug>` | yes, as one of them | 3, not planned (#369): refused, and a deployer rebinds by hand (below) |
  | deploy (`!upgrade-server`, #569) | 1 deployer | no | yes | 6 |
  | server (`!new-workshop`: it creates a bot and channels and adds an allowlist, §2.9) | 1 global admin (#558) | `new-workshop <name>` | yes | 7 |

  - **A global admin's approval alone completes** a plan of the destructive, rebind or server class (#558, amending Q3): the global admins can delete the VMs in AWS and deactivate their bots in Zulip without any approval anyway, so the multi-approver rule binds the workshop organizers, not the global admins. So the plan is complete when `destructive_approvals` distinct approvers have approved it, or when a global admin has, as `admin.toml` has them at approval time, whatever was given before; the requester may be that admin. An organizer who is not a global admin still needs a second, distinct approver: another organizer, or a global admin. Every other guard holds as for any approval. **The typed phrase stays** (`teardown <n>`, `deactivate-bots <n>`, `new-workshop <name>`): it guards against a misclick, not against the admins. The answer names the rule that completed the plan: `✅ P-50 approved (a global admin's approval)`, or `✅ P-50 approved (2 distinct approvers)`; the approval message says `It takes 2 distinct approvers, or a global admin alone`, and a server plan's `It takes 1 global admin.` (`approvals.takes`).
  - **Self-approval of a change is allowed on purpose.** The approval exists so that a human reads the dry run before anything happens, and during a workshop a second organizer may be busy in the room. The hash binding and the audit log keep it accountable.
  - **Rebinding is guarded like teardown, or more.** A changed e-mail, followed by `!provision zulip` and `!rollout`, rewrites `allow_emails` and `allow_user_ids`. That hands another Zulip user the participant's agents, which are signed into the participant's own Claude and ChatGPT accounts, with sudo on the VM. A changed `github` does the same for the participant's repository.
  - **When an e-mail change is not a rebind.** Correcting a roster e-mail is the most common fix on the day (ORGANIZERS §4.1, first row: the bots ignore `!login`). It is a plain **change** when both hold:
    - the row is bound to no Zulip user: no `zulip_user_id`, and no `zulip_invite = joined`;
    - no Zulip user, active or deactivated, has the old e-mail.

    In that case nobody can have used the bots. `allowed_users` (`src/zab/config.py`) is `allow_user_ids` plus the users whose e-mail is in `allow_emails`, and both are empty for that row. `roster_import.py` checks the old address (`GET /users` and `GET /invites`) at the dry run and again at apply, so a join between plan and approval makes the apply refuse the change: that re-check, not the plan's hash, is the guard. The plan's hash holds no Zulip lookup (#589: the unused `Live.zulip` is gone). While any human hides their address from the provisioner's key, the check can't be made, and the change is refused (§3.1). In the same way, a `github` change is a plain change when the recorded login has no collaborator access and no open invitation, checked live (§2.4, the reconcilers' row). Removing or renaming a row bound to a Zulip user (`zulip_user_id`, or `zulip_invite = joined`), or whose e-mail a Zulip user has or an open invitation goes to, is refused for the same reason as a rebind: its slug, re-added for someone else, would hand them `<slug>-sandbox` and its history (§3.1).
  - **The order of a rebind,** as the design had it for `!rebind`, which is not planned (#369); by hand, below. The two allow lists are OR-ed, so a rollout after an e-mail change alone would admit the new e-mail while the old user ID stays allowed. The order is:
    1. `!roster import` with the new e-mail, as a rebind plan;
    2. `!rebind slug`, which replaces the `zulip_user_id`;
    3. `!rollout … slug`.

    With the verb, the bot was to refuse a rollout for a row whose `zulip_user_id` belongs to a user whose e-mail isn't the row's; that check isn't built.
- **Veto.** Any approver of a pending plan may `!reject` it, on its bot.
- **Buttons.** Checked in the Zulip source on Oct 13 (`zulip/zulip` main):
  - the server accepts `widget_content` on `POST /messages`: `{"widget_type": "zform", "extra_data": {"type": "choices", "heading": …, "choices": [{"type": "multiple_choice", "short_name", "long_name", "reply"}]}}` (`zerver/lib/validator.py`, `check_widget_content`). Any sender may send one (V2). The server doesn't require a choice's `type`, **but the web client does**: `web/src/submessage.ts` parses the submessages with `zform_widget_extra_data_schema` (`web/src/zform_data.ts`), which requires a string `type` on every choice, and on a failed parse the widget is never activated and the text stays (V1, corrected Oct 15). `multiple_choice` is what Zulip's trivia bot sends;
  - the web client and desktop render choice buttons (`web/src/zform.ts`, `web/templates/widgets/zform_choices.hbs`). A button shows its `short_name` as plain text, styled by Zulip's CSS, so a bot can't colour it: the labels are `✅ Approve` and `❌ Reject`. **The mobile app shows no buttons**: zulip-flutter renders only poll widgets (its `WidgetType` leaves `zform` out), and the legacy app had no `zform`. Mobile approvers type the command the approval message shows. A click sends the choice's `reply` as an ordinary message from the clicking user, in the same topic, prefixed in a channel with a mention of the widget message's sender (`web/src/transmit.ts`, `reply_message`);
  - the widget **replaces** the message's rendered content.

  So the bot sends a separate approval message:
  - its widget has **Approve** (reply `!approve P-42 3f9a2c`) and **Reject** (reply `!reject P-42`);
  - its plain content is the same commands as text, which is what clients without widgets show;
  - **a plan that needs a typed phrase gets no widget** (#542). No button can type the phrase, and through v0.2.7 such a plan's widget had Reject alone, which the web client showed instead of the text: the one line to send was hidden, and found only at the end of the folded plan. Its text alone shows that line, as a one-line fenced code block, then the approvers it takes and how to reject:

    ````
    P-42 `!stop all`: approve by sending this plan's approval line, exactly as it is. A plan like this one has no Approve button. Point at the line below and click its copy button, at its top right (or type the line), then send it as a new message.

    ```text
    !approve P-42 3f9a2c stop 3
    ```

    It takes 1 approver. To reject, send `!reject P-42`.
    ````

    With two approvers, the last line is ``It takes 2 distinct approvers, or a global admin alone: each one sends that line. To reject, send `!reject P-42`.``; a server plan's, ``It takes 1 global admin. To reject, send `!reject P-42`.``

    **Why a code block, and not a code span** (#544; Zulip `main` at 50d811eb, Oct 21). The web client gives every code block a copy button (`web/src/rendered_markdown.ts`), which copies `$code.text()` through ClipboardJS as plain text, so the paste is the bare command. A copy by hand is less safe, from a code block too. `web/src/compose_paste.ts` converts the clipboard's HTML to Markdown, unless Shift is held. A lone `<code>` becomes an inline code span. So does a `<pre>` holding one line of `<code>` (its `fencedCodeBlock` rule), which becomes a fenced block when the line's newline is copied too. So does a single element styled `white-space: pre` (`maybe_transform_html`). The pasted text then starts with a backtick, which the grammar takes as no command: no 👀, and nothing runs. A plain paste skips the conversion (`compose_ui.shift_pressed`): Ctrl+Shift+V, or Cmd+Option+Shift+V on a Mac, as Zulip's keyboard help gives it (`keyboard-shortcuts.mdx`, `data-mac-following-key`), and as Chrome, Safari and Electron bind "Paste and Match Style" there. So does one undo right after a normal paste (Ctrl+Z, Cmd+Z on a Mac): `do_paste_text` inserts the plain text first, then the formatted text, so the undo restores the plain text. ORGANIZERS §2.3 and §8.1 give both. The bot answers such a line with a hint that names them, and never runs it (§2.2, "A command sent as code", #546).

  Approval is therefore always a message from a human, and one parser handles a click and a typed command. A click on another sender's widget is not a command (§2.2).

  **VERIFY V1** on Zulip Cloud: the buttons render on web and desktop, and the click sends `@**Workshop admin · e2e** !approve …`. Record what the mobile app shows. Run for PR 9 in `#zab-dev` (DECISIONS, verification log): the buttons did **not** render at first, because the choices lacked `type` (DECISIONS, the V1 correction); with it they render on web, as the maintainer saw on Oct 15. The mobile app shows only the text, per zulip-flutter's source, as the maintainer saw on production on Oct 17, approving a plan from a phone by typing its `!approve` line (DECISIONS, verification log). V1 is closed.

  Reactions are not used: they can't carry the hash or a confirmation phrase, and a mis-tap on mobile would count.

#### A rebind by hand (#369)

`!rebind` is not offered through the bots, and not planned: the cases are rare, and stay a deployer's steps by hand (#369). The bot answers ``❌ `!rebind` is not offered through the bots yet; a deployer runs it by hand``. A deployer and an organization administrator rebind a row by hand, with the steps below. Every step is manual, outside any plan, approval or audit record, until the bot commands of steps 5–7. None of it is exercised end to end. The two scripts' rebinding runs, without `--no-rebind`, are the ones the manual path from a laptop (`workshop/admin/README.md`) has always made.

**What it is for:** one participant whose row must move to another identity of their own.
- **Another Zulip account.** `!roster import` refuses the new e-mail as a rebind (§3.1): the row is bound to a Zulip user (a `zulip_user_id`, or `zulip_invite = joined`), or a Zulip user has the old address. It also refuses it, with `cannot check the old address`, while any human hides their address from the provisioner. Steps 1–7.
- **Another GitHub login,** after the old one accepted access to `<org>/<slug>`: `!provision repos` reports `login changed: refused`. Steps 4 and 7 only, once the participant has put the new login in the *GitHub* field of their Zulip profile, which `provision_repos.py` reads first (the roster's `github` is only its fallback, and the run's record of the login it last invited, so leave it alone).

**Never for another person.** The VM's agents are signed in to the participant's own Claude and ChatGPT accounts, and the sandbox, the repository and the VM hold their work. A newcomer gets a row of their own. If the account bound to the row turns out not to be the participant's, that person has had the agents and sudo on the VM: stop, and tell the global admins. That row needs a new VM, not a rebind.

**Before you start:**
- the participant confirms, from the old account or in person, that both identities are theirs;
- on the workshop's bot, `!plans` answers `No plan is pending on this bot.`, and no apply is running (each apply's progress message in the channel ends with its result). The organizers send nothing for this row until step 5: a plan pending now would read the roster that step 1 changes, and an apply could write it at the same moment;
- the new account has joined Zulip, and its address is visible to moderators (*Admins and moderators* or wider, ORGANIZERS §3.3), or step 2 can't bind it. If it hasn't joined, an organizer invites the new address in Zulip first (ORGANIZERS §1.5): zab sends no invitations (#628).

**On the server,** over SSH (BOOTSTRAP §3.6), in one shell. `ORG` is the workshop's `github_org` in `/etc/zab-admin/admin.toml`, which the bot passes as `--repo-owner` and `--org` (`sudo grep -n github_org /etc/zab-admin/admin.toml` lists each workshop's, in the order of its entries):

```sh
cd /
WS=<the workshop>; SLUG=<the row's slug>; NEW=<the new e-mail>; ORG=<its GitHub organization>
R=/var/lib/zab-admin/ws/$WS/roster.csv; OUT=/var/lib/zab-admin/ws/$WS/out
PY=/opt/zab-admin/current/.venv/bin/python; W=/opt/zab-admin/current/workshop
entry() { $PY -I -c 'import sys, tomllib; w = {e["name"]: e for e in tomllib.load(open(sys.argv[1], "rb"))["workshop"]}[sys.argv[2]]; print(w.get(sys.argv[3], ""))' /etc/zab-admin/admin.toml "$WS" "$1"; }
URL=$(entry refserve_url); GIFTS=$(entry gifts_step)   # as the bot passes them (§2.1; #709)
```

1. **The roster: the new e-mail.** As `zab-admin`, with the release's own roster code, which checks the address and that no other row has it, and writes the file atomically, keeping the old one as `roster.csv.bak`. It also clears `zulip_invite`, as `!roster import` does for an accepted e-mail change, so that step 2 hands the sandbox over to the new account:

   ```sh
   sudo -u zab-admin $PY -I -B - "$W/aws" "$R" "$SLUG" "$NEW" <<'EOF'
   import sys
   sys.path.insert(0, sys.argv[1])  # the release's workshop/aws, for its roster code
   from aws_common import check_duplicates, read_roster, validate_row, write_roster
   path, slug, new = sys.argv[2:]
   try:
       rows = read_roster(path)
       hits = [r for r in rows if r["slug"] == slug]
       if len(hits) != 1 or not new:
           sys.exit(f"{slug}: no such row in {path}, or no new e-mail; nothing written")
       row = hits[0]
       was = (row["email"], row["zulip_user_id"] or "-", row["zulip_invite"] or "-")
       row["email"], row["zulip_invite"] = new, ""
       validate_row(row)
       check_duplicates(rows)
   except (OSError, ValueError) as exc:
       sys.exit(f"refused, nothing written: {exc}")
   write_roster(path, rows)
   print(f"{slug}: email {was[0]} -> {new}; zulip_user_id {was[1]}; zulip_invite {was[2]} -> cleared")
   EOF
   ```

   It prints one line, such as `alice: email alice@old.edu -> alice@new.org; zulip_user_id 1234567; zulip_invite joined -> cleared`. A refusal names its reason, such as `refused, nothing written: duplicate email 'bob@example.org'`, and writes nothing.
2. **The binding,** with the provisioner's key and without `--no-rebind`, the bot's other flags as §3 gives them. The dry run first:

   ```sh
   sudo -u zab-admin $PY -E -s -B -u $W/admin/zulip_provision.py --roster "$R" \
     --zuliprc /etc/zab-admin/secrets/provisioner.zuliprc --out-dir "$OUT" --repo-owner "$ORG" \
     --refserve-url "$URL" ${GIFTS:+--gifts-step "$GIFTS"} --only "$SLUG" --dry-run
   ```

   `--refserve-url` and `--gifts-step` are the workshop's, as the bot passes them: without them, the run would set the sandbox's description to the release's default gift step.

   Its one row reads `<slug>  <new ID>  e-mail  joined  codex: reused, claude: reused  exists  would post`, and its notes include `warning: zulip_user_id changed from <old ID> to <new ID>` (and `warning: GitHub field … is also set by user(s) <old ID>; e-mail wins` when the old account's *GitHub* field holds the row's login). Then the same command without `--dry-run`, whose row reads the same with `exists` under `start here`. It records the new ID in the roster, subscribes the new account to the sandbox, and makes it the sandbox's administrator, as at a participant's first run; that run also makes a sandbox with protected history shared again, which the participant may set back. It changes nothing else of the channel.
   - **`- -` and `not on Zulip`** under `zulip id`, `matched by` and `invite`, with the line `<slug>: not on Zulip: invite <the new e-mail> in Zulip (…)` under the table: Zulip shows the provisioner no user with the new address. Either the account hasn't joined: an organizer invites the new address in Zulip (ORGANIZERS §1.5), and once the participant has joined, run step 2 again; or it hides its address from moderators: have the participant widen it, then run step 2 again.
   - **Before the workshop's release,** while its sandboxes are held (ORGANIZERS §3.3), add `--hold` to both runs: the new account is bound, and subscribed at the release.
   - **`… cannot add subscribers to it …`** in the notes: the sandbox was made by someone other than the provisioner, such as one made by hand before its fleet had a server (§8.4). This runbook doesn't cover that row: tell the global admins.
3. **The sandbox: the old account out,** by an organization administrator, in the web UI. Zulip lets an administrator see a private channel's subscribers and unsubscribe them without being subscribed (<https://zulip.com/help/channel-permissions>). In *Channel settings* (the gear menu), under *All channels*, open `<slug>-sandbox` (or the name the participant gave it):
   1. in its *Permissions* tab, under *Who can administer the channel* (`can_administer_channel_group`), remove the old account and save. If Zulip doesn't let you, the participant does it from the new account, which step 2 made an administrator. This comes first: a channel administrator can add subscribers, the old account included;
   2. in its *Subscribers* tab, unsubscribe the old account. Leave the two bots, the new account, and anyone the participant invited.

   The *Subscribers* tab then lists `Claude · <slug>`, `Codex · <slug>`, the new account and the participant's own guests, and not the old account.
4. **The repository: the GitHub login,** on the server, without `--no-rebind`. `--invites-only` changes only the invitations of a row already provisioned, and needs no SSH, so the VM may be stopped. The token goes into the script's environment, never onto a command line. The dry run first:

   ```sh
   sudo -u zab-admin sh -c 'GH_TOKEN=$(cat /etc/zab-admin/secrets/github-token) && export GH_TOKEN &&
     exec "$0" -E -s -B -u "$1/admin/provision_repos.py" --roster "$2" \
       --zuliprc /etc/zab-admin/secrets/provisioner.zuliprc --org "$3" --known-hosts "$4/known_hosts" \
       --invites-only --only "$5" --dry-run' "$PY" "$W" "$R" "$ORG" "$OUT" "$SLUG"
   ```

   - **The same login** in the new account's *GitHub* field (or none there): it prints only `invites: 0 invited, 0 accepted, 0 removed, 0 failed; 1 unchanged (dry run)`, then `nothing to change: no invitation to send or withdraw, and no acceptance to record`, and exits 3 (#708). Nothing to do.
   - **Another login:** a table, `<slug>  <new login>  would invite <new login>; would remove <old login>`, then `invites: 1 invited, 0 accepted, 1 removed, 0 failed; 0 unchanged (dry run)`. Then the same command without `--dry-run`: `invited; removed <old login>`. The old login's invitation is withdrawn and its collaborator access removed, and the new login is invited; the roster's `github` becomes the new login. **If the row reads `failed: …` instead,** read the failure: the run may have stopped before the old login's removal (at the repository's lookup, say), in the middle of it, or after it, at the new login's invitation. So the old login may still have its access, or may have lost it already, and the new login may not be invited. The roster keeps the old login. Don't treat the old account as cut off yet: fix the cause, and run the same command again until the row reads `…; removed <old login>` with no `failed` (a removal already done is no error then). Then confirm on GitHub, in the repository's collaborator settings, that the old login is neither a collaborator nor invited. In the bot's runs, with `--no-rebind`, a row that fails keeps its access as it was, except that its old login's open invitation, when its login changed, may already be withdrawn (ORGANIZERS §3.4). On a VM launched before #142, the next `!rollout` then refuses the row until step 6's fix is made.
5. **The waker,** if the workshop's waker is on (ORGANIZERS §3.5), through the bot: `!waker on <slug>`. The waker obeys the row's Zulip user as the row's last `!waker on` found it (ORGANIZERS §3.5), so until this, the new account's messages don't wake the VM, and the old account's do.
6. **The bridges,** through the bot: `!rollout <version> <slug>`, with the release the row runs (`!status <slug>` shows it). It rewrites `allow_emails` and `allow_user_ids` together: until it has run, the two lists, which are OR-ed, still admit the old account by its ID (the order of a rebind, above). If `!status <slug>` shows the VM stopped, `!start <slug>` first. A VM launched before #142 records the GitHub login whose SSH keys its first boot imported, and the rollout then refuses it with `VM records GitHub login …: nothing copied`: do what that message says, over the break-glass key, then roll out again.
7. **Check,** through the bot and with the participant:
   - `!roster show <slug>` shows the new e-mail and the new Zulip ID;
   - `!provision zulip <slug>` (`!provision zulip hold <slug>` while held), which always passes `--no-rebind`, applies with the row `joined` and no note: the binding holds. Its apply rewrites the roster, so the bot sends the deployers the encrypted backup that holds the change (§1.7);
   - the participant sends `!vm` in a DM to `Claude · <slug>` from the new account, which gets the answer, and from the old account, which gets no answer, only the bridge's hint for a stranger, at most once an hour, naming the bot's allowed user: `This bot belongs to <the new account's full name>. …` (SPEC §4.5). That hint also shows that the rollout wrote the new ID;
   - for a new login, the participant accepts GitHub's invitation; `!provision repos invites-only <slug>` then reports `1 accepted`;
   - in the workshop's organizers channel, post what was rebound, from what to what, and why: it is the only record of steps 1–4.

### 2.6 Running the scripts

- **Code.** The scripts run from the release tree (`/opt/zab-admin/current/workshop/…`), so a script and the bot that calls it always come from the same signed tag.
