- **Paths.** Every run gets its workshop's roster, `--out-dir`, `--known-hosts` and `--spares-file`. Nothing writes into the release tree, which is read-only.
- **Rollout wheels.** `!rollout v0.2.2` uses `/var/lib/zab-admin/releases/wheels/zulip_agent_bridge-0.2.2-py3-none-any.whl`, which only `deploy.sh --wheel` places (§1.6). A version without a wheel there is refused at plan time.
- **Regions.** Each workshop has exactly one region, and every region-scoped script (`launch`, `protect`, `teardown`, `power`, `adopt_spare`, `grow_disk`, `status`) gets it. Different workshops may use different regions. A roster row whose `region` is set to another region is refused at plan time.

### 2.7 Scrubbing

Everything the bots post or log, and everything the collector writes for the console, goes through one function, `zab.admin.scrub`. Encrypted backups (§1.7) are ciphertext and skip it.

**One invariant: `output = redact(strip(raw))`.** The scrubber strips and never consumes, redacts once on the stripped text, and emits only that result. Nothing is redacted before stripping, and the raw text never reaches the output.

0. **Strip.** Each invisible character is deleted on its own, from a fixed table as of Unicode 15.0:
   - Unicode Cc, except newline and tab, which are layout;
   - Cf: bidi controls, zero-width characters, BOM, soft hyphen;
   - Zl and Zp;
   - surrogates;
   - the zero-width variation selectors.

   ESC goes, but never a visible character after it: an ANSI sequence leaves `[31m` behind, as cosmetic noise. Deleting only invisible characters can make a secret contiguous, never shorter. Deleting the bidi controls also keeps a VM from reordering a plan's dry run on screen.
1. **Exact secrets.** Each process loads the secrets **it can read**, at start and again on each change of `out/`, and replaces them with `[redacted secret]` wherever they occur as a substring. Invisible characters, such as a BOM, are stripped from each value too.
   - **Base64.** Each secret is also registered as its base64, at every byte alignment, in the standard and URL-safe alphabets, as an HTTP Basic header would carry it.
   - **A zuliprc's key** is read in every form the bots' loader accepts, since a hand-edited or copied file need not say `key=` (#526):
     - **line by line,** always: every line that reads as a `key` option in a form configparser takes (`=` or `:`, any spacing, the name in any case). This alone learns every key `key=` learned before, and a duplicate's first value and an indented `key` line, which configparser reads as another option's continuation, count as well;
     - **as the loader reads it,** with configparser, when the file is at most 4096 characters (a real zuliprc has a few hundred): this adds a value continued on indented lines, in any section. Where the loader would refuse the file (a duplicate, options before any section header, a line it can't read), the scrubber reads on, since the file's keys are secrets all the same. The bound is configparser's cost: before Python 3.13 it is quadratic in a line's length and in its count of unreadable lines, and the server runs 3.12. Its worst case takes 0.07 s at 4096 characters and 11 to 18 s at 64 KiB.

     A value counts whole. A continued value also counts line by line and with its lines joined. A value with an inline comment (`#` or `;` after a space, which the loader keeps in the value) also counts without the comment, and as its first word. Each of these also counts without the quotes around it. A malformed file never fails the reload.
   - **Per process.** The registry is per process, so the separation of §1.2 stays intact:
     - **bot and collector** (`zab-admin`): the provisioner key, the admin bots' keys (every `secrets/bots/*.zuliprc`, so a key the config helper adds at runtime is registered before the restart that loads its bot, §2.9), the GitHub token, each participant bot key in every workshop's `out/*/*.zuliprc`, and the lines of the fleet SSH private key;
     - **console** (`zab-console`): its own signing secret;
     - **waker** (`zab-waker`): the opted-in bots' keys it can read.

   A secret a process can't read is a secret it can't leak, so no process needs another's file. The runner also reloads it before any post of an apply's output, so that a key a step just wrote (a new bot zuliprc) can't slip out: each progress edit, after taking its snapshot of the output, reloads it if `out/` changed (a key the snapshot holds was written to its file before it was printed), and the result follows a full reload. This is the strong layer: it needs no pattern.
2. **Patterns.**
   - **The SPEC §11.6 patterns** (`zab.render.redact`).
   - **AWS secret access keys and session tokens after their names,** since the instance role's credentials come from IMDS and can't be registered. The names are those of IMDS and STS JSON, `aws configure export-credentials`, the config files, and botocore's `ReadOnlyCredentials` repr.
   - **The one rule of `admin_common.redact` that §11.6 lacks:** a Zulip key after `key=` or `key:` anywhere in a line, as in a Zulip API response's `"api_key": "…"`. It is restated here because zab doesn't import `workshop/`.

   **Where matches are found.** Items 1 and 2 find spans in the stripped text, and in up to two more views:
   - the text without its 7-bit CSI sequences (`ESC [`, then parameters and a final byte), so that colors inside a secret or before a token don't hide it;
   - when the text holds U+009B, the text without its 8-bit SGR sequences too (`U+009B 31m`). A lone U+009B is also just an invisible character, so it is read both ways, and as an introducer only in an SGR's shape, so that it never eats a letter after it.

   **Boundaries.** A pattern that wants a token boundary (`ghp_`, `sk-`, `AKIA`, `eyJ`, `token`) would miss a token that a removed character glued to a word: `x<NUL>ghp_…` becomes `xghp_…` once stripped. So, in each view, such a pattern is also matched without its boundary guard, anchored at each place where it could start and where a removal fell into the run of word characters before it. It is also anchored where the view shows, right before that place, the rest of an escape sequence whose introducer alone was removed: `x U+009B 2Kghp_…` reads `x 2Kghp_…`. That rest is checked up to 64 characters.
   - **After U+009B**, the rest is a CSI's parameters, intermediates and final byte (`107;254u`, `1 q`).
   - **After ESC**, it is only intermediates and a final byte (`( B`, `M`). An ESC sequence has no parameters: a final such as `7` or `=` ends it, so `ESC 7` before `ask-…` (as `tput sc` prints it) leaves the word whole.
   - **Before `sk-`**, the rest may end before a diff's deletion marker, which §11.6 counts as a key: a terminal shows `x U+009B 2K-sk-…` as `x -sk-…`.

   A removal that was a whole sequence leaves nothing behind and never counts, so `ESC [1m` before `flask-app-…` leaves one word, as §11.6 wants. These anchored matches run on the same unsplit view: nothing is inserted, so a removal inside a token, or a prefix inside a token, splits nothing. A place inside a token already found is checked too, since its own token can run past that one's end. A cost bound applies, with three separate budgets per view, so that no kind of place can use up another's:
   - at most 4096 places with a removal in the run;
   - at most 4096 escapes checked;
   - at most 4096 places inside a token already found.

   Each place is matched within 8192 characters. These bounds are an accepted limit, set out under the threat model below.

   The end of a token gets the same treatment. A pattern that wants no word character after it, such as a Zulip key (exactly 32 characters), is also matched without that guard, and the match is kept where a removal fell right at its end: `key=<32><NUL>suffix` is a key, as in the input.

   Every span maps back to the input and is redacted in the stripped text; overlapping spans become one redaction. More views can only add redaction.

   **Structured records.** `Scrubber.record` scrubs a JSON value, such as an audit record, keys included. A string value whose spans beside its key differ from its spans alone is replaced whole; the scrubber finds those spans by scanning `"key": "value"` through the same strip and views.

   **JSON in script output.** `status.py --json` passes a VM's own text through in `idle_stop.last_verdict`, `doctor_failures` and `last_error`, and its `json.dumps` writes a U+200B as `\u200b`: a token that character splits is two printable runs in that text, out of reach of item 0's strip. So the status read of a plan, and `!status`, go through `Scrubber.json_text` (#361, #380):
   - **What it does.** It turns each JSON escape back into its character (`\u…`, a surrogate pair as one, `\b`, `\f`, `\r`), then scrubs the text. It keeps only the escapes of `"`, `\`, newline and tab, and an escaped backslash is never read as the start of an escape. JSON stays JSON: the strip deletes every control character the pass made raw.
   - **The escapes it keeps.** Each ends in a word character (`\t`, `\u0009`, `\u0022`), which a pattern that wants a boundary before its token reads as glued to it: a JWT, `ghp_…` or `token=…` right after a tab would get through, where the decoded string shows the tab. So spans are also found on a view of the same length in which each kept escape reads as spaces, and the union of both is redacted in the text.
   - **Where it holds.** It decodes nothing, so it holds wherever JSON lies in the text: after other text on a line, two documents on one line, or a partial or malformed one, as a kill leaves it. A fuzz test on `status.py`-shaped documents checks that it redacts every token that a scrub of the decoded strings redacts.
   - **Its bounds** (item 5). `MAX_SCRUB_CHARS` cuts the text first. Two linear passes follow, which only shorten it, then one scan of it, and a second of the view when a kept escape makes one. So it costs at most two of the scans item 5 bounds, of at most a million characters each, and its output is never longer than its input. The slowest shape measured is `\u009beyJ` repeated, with a tab on each line: undone, it puts a U+009B before every token-like place (the scan's slowest path) and has the view scanned too. It costs 0.7 s for a million characters, about ten times a plain scan of the same text. An earlier version decoded each document and scrubbed its values: lines of `[`, unterminated arrays and deep nesting made that quadratic, or 1.8 GB.
   - **Before a cut.** A cut can leave only a token's head, which no scrubber can match. So `admin_common.redact`, which status.py's `first_line` cells (160, 300 and 80 characters) go through, strips the same invisible characters first (the bots' table, copied, a test keeping the two equal), and `first_line` cuts only after it. It also has the bots' JWT and AWS secret and `token=` rules, since VM text reaches those cells.
   - **The table.** Its own decode of the status answer tries at most four places, builds at most 1,000 rows, and reads at most 20 turns per VM.
3. **Mentions.** Text from VMs and scripts passes through `zab.render.mentions.disarm_mentions`, which breaks every non-silent mention, in prose and code alike, so a VM can't trigger `@**all**` or an organizer ping.
4. **Fencing.** Script and VM output always goes inside a code fence one backtick longer than the longest backtick run inside it. Nothing from a VM can close the fence and inject markdown, links or fake buttons.
5. **Limits.**
   - **One call scrubs at most a million characters.** Longer input is cut at its last newline before that, and a note says how much was left out. So a VM's output can't make the server, a 2 GiB `t4g.small`, spend gigabytes on it, and the time one call takes is bounded. Measured on a fast x86 core:
     - ten MB of colored output: about 0.3 s and 20 MB;
     - dense invisible characters, and most megabytes of anchored places: at most about 0.8 s and 46 MB;
     - **the slowest input measured:** about 1.4 s and 47 MB for 1 MB, where main took 0.6 s. That input has a U+009B before every token-like place (`U+009B eyJ` repeated), after thousands of glued token prefixes have spent the run budgets of item 2, so every place is examined. This is an accepted limit (issue #351, the last item below says why), and a test keeps it from growing.
   - **The cut is safe:** a cut between lines splits no secret in scope, every bounded pattern lies on one line, and a cut private-key block still matches.
   - **Audit records:** a string value keeps at most 65,536 characters, cut the same way.
   - **Script output in posts:** at most 40 lines and 4000 characters inline, fences included; the full text goes in an attachment of at most 1 MB. A plan's message and a `!status` answer go past this: they have their own caps on the dry run's and the status table's lines (§2.4), and are cut to fit Zulip's message limit with the lines under them and the audit footer included (#767), the whole attached when cut.
   - **The runner** keeps at most a million characters of a run's output (`KEEP`, cut at a line), so each of its scrubs is one such call: a plan's dry run once, a result once, and a progress edit at most once a minute per apply under way, 19 at most, each in a thread.

**Threat model.**

- **In scope:** a registered secret, or a §11.6 or field pattern, that a script prints contiguously. It may sit next to or inside control characters, ANSI or bidi sequences, or be split by non-printable characters. Plain text and base64 are both in scope.
- **Out of scope:** output whose producer splices printable characters into the middle of a secret, including the printable remnants of an escape sequence. Output whose producer re-encodes a secret: homoglyphs, URL encoding, a line wrapped inside a secret.
- **Why:** only two kinds of producer could do that.
  - The server's own scripts never do.
  - The participant VMs never hold the server's registered secrets: the provisioner key, the GitHub token and the bot keys. The participants' own bot keys are theirs already.
- **Accepted limit, not claimed as covered (issue #351):** the anchored matches of item 2 have bounds.
  - **The bounds:** three budgets per view and per call, of 4096 places each:
    - places with a removal in the run;
    - places inside a token already found;
    - escapes checked.

    Each match looks at most 8192 characters ahead, and an escape's tail is checked only up to 64 characters.
  - **What gets past them:** every case needs output with thousands of glued token prefixes, a token over 8 KB, or an escape sequence over 64 characters.
    - Thousands of glued token prefixes in one call can use up a budget. A later **pattern-only** token that needs an anchor (one glued to a word by a removal, or after an escape's tail) then goes unredacted.
    - A pattern-only token right after an escape sequence whose tail is longer than 64 characters goes unredacted. Real 8-bit colours fit: one with truecolour foreground, background and underline colour, written with colons, takes at most 63.
    - A pattern token over 8 KB that needs an anchor is redacted only in its first 8192 characters, or not at all (a JWT).
  - **Not affected:** registered secrets and their base64 are found in every view without a budget or a window. So are pattern tokens whose boundary the view shows.
  - **Why it is accepted:** only output built on purpose uses up a budget, and the window and the tail limit need a token over 8 KB or an escape tail over 64 characters, which ordinary programs don't print. Only a participant's VM could produce any of them. A VM never holds the server's registered secrets, and the tokens it does hold are its own participant's, who could post them anyway. A matcher with no budgets would add subtle code to the server's most delicate module without changing what an attacker can get, so issue #351 closed without one.

### 2.8 Audit log

`/var/lib/zab-admin/audit.jsonl` holds one JSON object per line:

- the file is opened with `O_APPEND`, and each record is followed by an `fsync`;
- `chattr +a`, set by root at bootstrap, lets the service append but not rewrite;
- records are scrubbed before they are written.

Example:

```json
{"seq": 1234, "ts": "2026-10-31T14:02:11Z", "event": "apply_end",
 "workshop": "harvard-2026", "plan": "P-42", "hash": "3f9a2c…64 hex", "verb": "rollout", "args": ["v0.2.2", "alice", "bob", "idle-stop=60"],
 "requester": {"id": 102, "name": "Alice Organizer"}, "approvers": [{"id": 101, "name": "Grace Admin"}],
 "argv": [["/opt/zab-admin/current/.venv/bin/python", "-E", "-s", "-B", "-u", "…/push_config.py", "--roster", "…", "--idle-stop", "60", "--only", "alice", "--only", "bob"]],
 "exit": 0, "duration_s": 184, "results": {"alice": "ok", "bob": "ok (sign-in pending)"},
 "output": "runs/P-42/apply.txt", "output_sha256": "…", "server": "v0.3.0+a1b2c3d",
 "prev": "sha256 of the previous line's bytes"}
```

- **Events:** `request`, `plan`, `refused` (with the reason), `unchanged` (a plan never offered, its dry run having said there is nothing to change, with its `verb` and the reason: §2.4 step 2, #708), `foreign`, `approval`, `reject`, `expired`, `stale`, `apply_start` (a `!retire`'s with its rows' `chains`, the stages of each, in order), `apply_end`, `retire_step` (a `!retire`'s or a month-end run's row's step as it ends or is skipped: its `plan`, `row`, `stage` and `cell`, as the result's table has it; the ledger marks the row from it in place, #837, #855), `interrupted`, `upgrade` (with the SHA-256 of the reviewed diff), `backup`, `read` (status and other read commands, with no output), `console_login`, `config` (a line of the config helper's `changes.jsonl`, copied into the chain at the bot's start, as `deploys.jsonl`'s are as `upgrade` records: §2.9, #418), `wake_check` (the wake monitor's, §1.2: the start, `matched` with the message's ID and time, `unmatched`, `not_opted_in`, or `failed` with the reason, and the alert's message ID, or `held` when the VM's alert of the hour went out already; `expired` lists the starts that aged out unchecked, under one summary alert), `waker` (a workshop's waker `down` since a time, `up` again, each with its alert's message ID, or `off`, opted out while down: §1.8, #717), `month_end` (the month-end schedule's own events, §11.5, #570, of which `what` is `cancel`, with the rows it took out as `targets`, which the console's projection reads, and the rows `named`; or `look`, a look at a schedule that warned or dropped rows, with its `plan`, its `targets`, and its `rows` by what happened: `notice sent`, `reminder sent`, `notice no Zulip ID`, `notice refused`, `notice unsure`, …, `dropped`; `run`, a month-end run that ran nothing, with the `reason` and the rows `dropped`; or `retired`, written only by the development builds between #835 and #860, never by a release, for a run's row left with every step done, its run's `apply_end` perhaps never written, with the run as `plan`, its `schedule`, the row as `targets` and its `rows` as that `apply_end` would have them, from which the ledger still marks it retired, as from a `!retire`'s; a run's steps are `retire_step` records now). A refused `!month-end cancel` is a `refused` record, with its `verb`, the sender, a `code` and the reason.
- **The chain.** `prev` chains the records. The server keeps one log and one chain for all its workshops, and each record names its workshop. Every result message a bot posts shows `audit #seq · chain <first 12 hex of this line's sha256>`. The channels then hold independent copies of the chain head, and an edit of the file shows. `python -m zab.admin audit verify` checks the chain, and against a head copied from a channel with `--head SEQ:HEX`.
- **Why 12 hex digits.** The head is a commitment that a rewritten log must still match. Six digits are 24 bits, which someone rewriting the log could match within seconds by trying variants of a forged line. Twelve digits are 48 bits, about 16 million times as much work (decided on #319). An approval's six digits are a different matter: they name a pending plan, whose full hash is checked again at apply (§2.5).
- **The console** reads a projection, `console/audit-view.jsonl`, with the event, time, plan, verb, slugs, people and outcome, but no argument lists.

### 2.9 Adding a workshop: `!new-workshop`

A global admin adds a workshop through the global admin bot, in `#zab-admins` (stage 7); the global admins' runbook for it is `docs/NEW-WORKSHOP.md`:

```
!new-workshop mit-2027 region=us-east-2 like=harvard-2026 organizers=105,106
```

- **Who.** A global admin, on the global admin bot (`!help new-workshop` there gives its usage; a workshop's bot refuses it). A deployer who is not a global admin is refused. The grammar takes the name, then `region=`, `like=` and `organizers=`, all three required, and `channel="<name>"`, optional, in any order (§2.2; #418, #627).
- **The participants' channel** (#627). `channel="<its Zulip name>"` names the private channel whose subscribers become the workshop's roster (`!roster sync`): organizers invite the participants by e-mail with that channel ticked. Its name is the one value in quotes, since a Zulip name may hold spaces and capitals; the grammar takes any script's letters, digits, single inner spaces and a few marks (§2.2), nothing Zulip's Markdown reads, since the command shows in the plan and the audit log. It goes to `new_workshop.py` percent-encoded (`newworkshop.channel_arg`), the one value outside the argument lists' alphabet (§2.3). It is one of the two free-text values a script gets; the other, a workshop's `gifts_step` (§2.1), goes to `zulip_provision.py` base64url-encoded, inside the alphabet. The entry records the channel's **ID**, `channel`, so the channel may be renamed later. The dry run says which of these happens:
  - **adopted:** the provisioner is subscribed to a channel of that name (`GET /users/me/subscriptions`), whatever its case. Its ID is recorded and nothing of it changes. Never a channel `admin.toml` binds already (a bot's command channel, an `alerts_channel`, another workshop's `channel`, which the bot passes as `--bound-channel`), nor one named like a workshop's `#…-organizers` or `#…-fleet-alerts`: the plan is refused with a `MISSING:` line, rather than the apply failing at the loader's check, after it made the bot and the channels. The name is checked whether the provisioner sees the channel or not, and a bound ID whether it is in the channel or only sees it, so the plan never says to subscribe the provisioner to a channel a rerun would refuse, nor to an organizers channel, which it must never join (#641, #638). A public one is adopted with a `warning:` line, since anyone in the organization could join it and the next sync would offer them a row: make it private;
  - **refused:** the provisioner sees a channel of that name, a public one, without being in it. The plan is refused: subscribe `zab-provisioner` to it first, then request again;
  - **made:** the provisioner sees none. The apply makes it first, before anything else in Zulip: private, shared history, the organizers and the provisioner subscribed (not the global admins), the organizers its administrators. The organizers and the global admins may add subscribers, so that an organizer can invite into it; so may any subscriber when the organization lets members subscribe others (DECISIONS, 2026-10-06), so `!roster sync`'s plan, which lists every addition, is the check. The provisioner sees no private channel it isn't in, so the plan says, on an `unchecked:` line, that a private channel of that name would fail the apply at it; Zulip then refuses the name, the apply stops with nothing else in Zulip made, and the answer is to subscribe `zab-provisioner` to that channel and request again, which adopts it. A rerun after a later failure adopts the channel it made.
- **The action** (`zab.admin.newworkshop`): one step, `workshop/admin/new_workshop.py`, whose dry run is the plan and whose run is the apply. Every value comes from `admin.toml` or the command's checked words. Refused before any script runs: a name of a workshop of this server; a `like=` that isn't one; an organizer who is an admin bot; a server with no global admin, since only one approves the plan, and nobody could approve it (with one, the new workshop meets the loader's rule for its approvers too; #558); a region not in `[global] regions`. The fleet scope, `zab-scope` and `[global]`'s extra tags, comes from `admin.toml` (`newworkshop.fleet_scope`) and finds the region's image and group; the new workshop's own tag is `workshop=<name>`, which the entry doesn't name, since the loader gives every workshop that and `[global]`'s (#425). The plan is hashed on its dry run, with `admin.toml` among its inputs, so a changed configuration makes it STALE. The dry run prints the entry, the organizers' and global admins' names next to their IDs, what will be created and each prerequisite (`ok:` or `MISSING:`); a missing one fails it, so the plan is refused, never offered. The prerequisites' check is one function, `new_workshop.region_setup`.
- **Plan.** The plan shows the new workshop's entry for `admin.toml`, the organizers' names next to their user IDs, and what will be created.
  - The values the command doesn't give are copied from the workshop named by `like=`, and shown: GitHub organization, file server, instance types, break-glass keys. The scope tags are `workshop=<name>` and `[global]`'s (§2.1, §5): never copied, never typed.
  - The AMI and the security group are the region's, in the fleet's scope (§5), looked up, never typed: exactly one security group named `zab-fleet` in the scope (the name sets it apart from other scoped groups, such as the test rig's per-workshop ones), and the newest image in the scope, two equally new ones refused. The plan shows both IDs, which the entry records.
  - The name has at most 47 characters, so that `#<name>-fleet-alerts` fits Zulip's 60-character channel names; `zab-admin-<name>` then also fits the loader's 64-character key-pair names. The loader allows 64-character workshop names, which an entry added by hand may use with channels named otherwise.
- **Prerequisite: the region is set up** (§5, "Setting up a region"), checked at plan time and again at apply. The account owner must have done it first, since the role can't:
  - the region is one of `[global] regions`, so the role's policy covers the region and the scope (§5; the fleet-wide scope since v0.2.5, CHANGELOG 0.2.5);
  - it holds exactly one security group named `zab-fleet` in the scope, and at least one image in the scope, of which the newest is taken. Two such groups, or none, or no scoped image, or two newest images, is a missing prerequisite.

  A region that is set up needs nothing more: the role's policy names no workshop, so a new workshop changes neither IAM nor AWS. The bot checks with a `DryRun` launch in the region, with the new workshop's tags, that the role may make it (V12).

  **A region not yet set up** is a change of `[global]`, which `!new-workshop` never makes (its helper accepts a new `[[workshop]]` entry only). The account owner sets the region up (§5), a deployer adds it to `[global] regions` and installs that `admin.toml` over SSH, and the policy is rendered again and pasted: BOOTSTRAP §6.5 gives the whole sequence, with its commands, ending with a `!new-workshop` plan for the region that is rejected. **The server never supplies the policy's text:** the deployer runs `render-policy` on their own machine, from the signed release and their own copy of `admin.toml`, and the account owner pastes that. A compromised server could otherwise hand over a policy that drops the `zab-admin` deny or adds `iam:PassRole`.

  A missing prerequisite is named in the plan, which can't be approved until it is fixed.
- **Approval.** The server class (§2.5): a global admin's approval, the requester's own included, and the phrase `new-workshop <name>`, because it creates an allowlist (#558: until v0.2.9, `destructive_approvals` distinct global admins). It needs every workshop's apply lock (§2.4, step 5): an approval is answered `busy` while any workshop applies, and no workshop applies while it does.
- **Apply,** in order:
  1. Create the directories `ws/<name>/` and `ws/<name>/out/`, with an empty roster (its header alone). First, not after the Zulip step, since the directory keeps `new-workshop.json`, the channels made, for a rerun.
  2. With the provisioner's key (`zulip_provision.py`'s client and helpers), create:
     - with `channel=` and no channel to adopt, the participants' channel first (above);
     - the generic bot `Workshop admin · <name>` (short name `<name>-admin`), owned by the provisioner;
     - the private channel `#<name>-organizers`, with the organizers, the global admins and the new bot;
     - the private channel `#<name>-fleet-alerts`, the waker's (§3), with the organizers, the global admins **and the provisioner**. The waker posts a failed wake there with the participant's own bot, which may post in a private channel only if it or its owner, the provisioner, is subscribed. **Decided (#418): subscribed,** as on the rig (§7). Left out, the organizers would never hear of a VM that failed to wake. The cost: every bot the provisioner owns, other workshops' included, may post there, so an alert can be spoofed; the channel takes no commands, and no bot obeys a bot.

     Both channels have shared history; their administrators, and the only ones who may add subscribers, are the global admins. The provisioner, their creator, keeps no access to the organizers channel and is never subscribed to it (V11), unless it is itself a global admin, as on the test server. A rerun reuses the bot, and the channels by their recorded IDs.
  3. Import the server's fleet key as the key pair `zab-admin-<name>` in the region, tagged with the scope (present with this key is fine).
  4. Send a request to the root helper `zab-admin-config` (`zab.admin.confighelper`, started by `zab-admin-config.path`, #418): `/var/lib/zab-admin-config/requests/request.json`, which carries the new entry, as JSON, and the new bot's key. The directory is the bot's (0700) inside the helper's root-owned state, so the bot can't swap it for another. The helper claims the request by renaming it, so that its writer can withdraw an unanswered one the same way and exactly one of them gets it, then deletes it, whatever it holds (a link, a FIFO, an empty directory; a non-empty one stays under its claimed name). The apply then waits up to 60 s for the helper's outcome for its plan, and reports it; with none, it withdraws the request by a rename, or, when the helper has it already, says the entry may be added. The helper, in this order (#332):
     - **checks everything first:** the request's shape and the key's (`[api]` with `email`, `key` and `site` alone); a name no entry has; the bot's key at `secrets/bots/<name>.zuliprc`; the whole new `admin.toml` through the strict loader of PR 5a; that the new file is the old one plus exactly this entry, which it renders itself from the JSON values; and that the entry's break-glass keys pass the bots' own file checks, without which no bot would start;
     - **then writes** the key (root:zab-admin 0640), with `O_EXCL`, never over a file, where the bot process can't write; the new `admin.toml` beside the old one; a line of its own `changes.jsonl`; then renames the old file to `admin.toml.bak` and the new one into place. A failure before the renames removes the key and the new files and writes no record; a failed rename leaves `admin.toml` as it was and removes them too, the record staying, as the outcome says; once the new file is in place the change is made, and a later failure (the directories' `fsync`) is reported as a warning on an applied outcome;
     - writes its outcome to `result.json`, whatever happened, an unexpected error included, which the apply waits for and reports.

     It accepts only a new `[[workshop]]` entry. It never changes `admins`, `deployers`, `[global]` or another workshop, and **a refused request changes no file**, one naming an existing workshop included.

     **It restarts nothing** (a change from the first design). The bot that asked hosts the apply: a restart from the helper would cut that apply short, and the plan would end INTERRUPTED with its result unsaid. So the bot, once it has posted the result of a successful apply, restarts itself (SIGTERM to its own process, then systemd's `Restart=always`), holding every workshop's apply lock until then; the starter restarts itself whenever `admin.toml` changes (§1.2).
- **The helper's residual risk.** The helper can't check approvals, which live in the bot's `state.db`. A compromised `zab-admin` process can therefore add a workshop with organizers it picks. That process already holds the fleet's keys, but such an entry also survives restarts and signed upgrades, and through `!provision` it lends its organizers the provisioner key and the GitHub token. What bounds it:
  - an honest bot asks for a global admin's approval and the typed phrase;
  - **the helper's record** (#332): each change is a line of `/var/lib/zab-admin-config/changes.jsonl`, root's, with the plan, the workshop, its organizers and the digests of `admin.toml` before and after. The bot can neither change nor remove it; at its start, the restart that follows, it copies each new line into the audit log's chain as a `config` record (§2.8), which puts the change under the chain heads the channels show. A compromised bot could skip that copy; it can't keep the helper from writing the line;
  - the new entry is visible: `!version` lists the workshops and their organizer counts, and the console lists the workshops. A diff of the allowlists posted at each start would need a baseline the helper, not the bot, keeps: it is not built;
  - **the bound that holds whatever the bot does:** `deploy.sh` diffs the server's `admin.toml` against the deployer's copy at every deploy.
- **Later changes** to a workshop's organizers are a deployer's edit over SSH until an in-band verb exists (P-i, §8.3). Retiring a workshop after its last VM is gone belongs with the month-end work (§11.5).

## 3. The actions, mapped to the scripts

Every action wraps an existing script and reuses its dry run, except where **new** is marked. The dry-run column is what the plan shows. The last column is the stage of §8.1 that brings it. Every value comes from the bot's own workshop: its roster `R`, its `out/`, its tag `W` and its region `RG`.

| # | Command | Script and argument list (values from config and roster) | Dry run in the plan | Changes needed | Stage |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | `!roster import` with a CSV attached | **new** `workshop/admin/roster_import.py --roster R --input U --zuliprc PROV --org ORG --tag W --region RG --retired RS --workshop-roster WS=R… [--dry-run]`, with `GH_TOKEN` | the diff: added, changed and removed rows, column by column; exit 3 when no row changes: no plan, `☑` (§2.4 step 2, #708) | **new script** (§3.1) | 1 |
| 1 | `!roster show` | the bot reads `R` | n/a | none | 1 |

**Before `!teardown`: wait until EC2 shows the protection** (#378). On Oct 15, after a successful `ModifyInstanceAttribute`, EC2 read the old termination protection for up to 157 s, and enforced the old one too: a `TerminateInstances` right after the protection was lifted was refused. The other direction follows: within those minutes, a VM just protected may be listed `terminate` by the teardown's dry run, and EC2 may terminate it. So:
- **After `!protect`,** read its result: it says per VM whether EC2 shows the change yet. When it says `doesn't show it yet`, request `!protect` again a few minutes later and read the plan's dry run, which reads EC2 afresh: `unchanged (…)` once EC2 shows it; then `!reject` that plan. `!status` doesn't read termination protection.
- **Read the teardown plan before approving it:** each kept VM must be listed `keep`.
- **The bot enforces 5 minutes** (`actions.PROTECT_SETTLE`, a constant): it refuses a `!teardown` plan within 5 minutes of the workshop's last `!protect` or `!launch … protect` apply, counted from its last `apply_start` or `apply_end`, or from the `interrupted` record of one a restart cut short (`fleet.Ledger`), whatever its outcome. A record's `ts` is cut to the second, so the 5 minutes run from the end of that second: never a moment early. The refusal names that plan and the time to request again: `P-41 (!protect) changed termination protection here at 14:48 UTC, …: no !teardown or !retire for 5 minutes after it. Request it again from 14:54 UTC`. While such an apply is still busy, however long it takes, the plan is refused too: `P-41 (!protect) is changing termination protection here now, …: no !teardown or !retire for 5 minutes after it ends. Request it again 5 minutes after its result` (`Planning._protecting`). A rows' refusal (unsynced) comes first, since waiting doesn't help it. The check runs again at apply (`Planning.rebuild`), so a teardown approved within the 5 minutes, planned before the `!protect`, becomes STALE before anything runs. A protection changed outside the bot, in the console or with `protect.py` by hand, isn't in the audit log: wait for `protect.py` to say `EC2 shows it now`.

### 3.1 Roster import

- **The upload.** The organizer attaches a CSV to the `!roster import` message. The bot downloads it with its own key (`/user_uploads/…`, at most 1 MB) and keeps it as `runs/P-n/roster-upload.csv`.
- **Validation.** `roster_import.py` reads it with `aws_common.read_roster` (`utf-8-sig`, headers lower-cased). It applies `validate_row` and both duplicate checks (slug and e-mail, case-insensitive), and refuses `zab-spare-N` slugs.
- **Inputs only.** The upload supplies only the input columns: `slug`, `first_name`, `last_name`, `email`, `repo`, `region`, `notes`, and `github` as the manual override. The columns the scripts fill (`host`, `ip`, `host_keys`, `zulip_*`, `repo_status`, `github_invite`) come from the current roster, matched by slug. An upload that carries them gets a warning, and they are ignored.
  - A column the upload lacks keeps its recorded values. The scripts also write `region`, `repo` and `github`, so an empty cell there keeps the recorded value; the names and `notes` are the organizer's alone, and the upload's value replaces them, empty or not.
  - The result follows the upload's row order. `email` and `github` are compared case-insensitively: a difference of case alone is no change. A slug the grammar reads as a keyword or a setting key (`zab.admin.grammar.RESERVED`: `all`, `spares`, `hold`, `steer`, …) is refused, since no command could name its row, and `!rollout … alice steer` would sweep it in.
  - An accepted `email` change clears `zulip_invite`, the old address's record: before 0.2.10, `zulip_provision.py` would otherwise have taken an open invitation to the new address for the sandbox's.
- **Refused:**
  - removing or renaming the slug of a row that has a VM (`host` set): tear it down first;
  - changing the `region` of a row that has a VM (`workshop/aws/README.md`, "Regions": never change a recorded region);
  - **a rebind** (#369): changing the `email` of a row bound to a Zulip user (a `zulip_user_id`, or `zulip_invite = joined`), or whose old e-mail a Zulip user has, active or deactivated; or changing the `github` of a row whose recorded login has collaborator access. While any human hides their address from the key, nobody can be shown not to have the old e-mail, so that change is refused too (the live checks, below). A deployer does a rebind by hand (§2.5, "A rebind by hand"). The design's **rebind** plan, two organizers, or a global admin alone, and the phrase `rebind <slug>`, in the order of §2.5, is not planned (#369).
  - changing the `github` of a row whose recorded login has an open invitation: `provision_repos.py` withdraws it only while the roster records that login. Withdraw it first, or have the participant correct their Zulip GitHub field and run `!provision repos`, which replaces the invitation. A recorded login that is not a valid login (a hand edit) proves nothing and refuses the change, as `provision_repos.py --no-rebind` refuses it. The invitations the organizers send in Zulip are invisible to the provisioner's key (the residual, below), and from 0.2.10 `zulip_provision.py` sends none (#628), so what follows applies only to an invitation it sent before 0.2.10. An `email` change while an invitation to the old address is open (`GET /invites`) is not refused, but the invitation must go: its holder, joining, would get the sandbox and, until `!rollout`, the agents. The plan says `the apply first revokes the open invitation to the old address`, and the apply revokes it (`DELETE /invites/{id}`) before it writes the roster, which says `revoked: <slug>: …`. A revocation that fails refuses the import, and nothing is written (#322). The apply reads the invitations again and revokes those it lists then, so one that appeared after a plan that said `plain change` is revoked without the plan having said so. The provisioner's key is the server's, so the listing holds the other workshops' invitations too, and an address is unique only within a workshop: when another workshop's row (`--workshop-roster`) has the old address, `GET /invites` can't say whose an open invitation is, since it doesn't name the sandbox. This row's `zulip_invite` proves nothing either, because `zulip_provision.py` recorded an invitation only once Zulip's answer arrived. So that change is refused, and a deployer makes it by hand. The residual: `GET /invites` lists only the invitations the key controls, its own, or every one for an administrator's key. With the provisioner's key, a Moderator's (Q6), an invitation that someone else sent by hand, an organizer's among them, is invisible to the import: its sender, or a Zulip administrator, revokes it (Organization settings → Users → Invitations; ORGANIZERS §1.5).
  - removing or renaming a row that has a `zulip_user_id` or `zulip_invite = joined`, whose e-mail a Zulip user has (checked live: the participant may have joined before `zulip_provision.py` recorded them), to whose e-mail an invitation is open, whose VM EC2 still has (checked live: a launch that failed half-way records `region` but no `host` or `ip`), for which `provision_repos.py` ran, or whose recorded login has access or an open invitation to its repository (checked live), even without a VM in the roster: a new row with its slug, for someone else, would inherit `<slug>-sandbox` or `<org>/<slug>`, and what is in them. A check that cannot be made refuses too. A deployer removes such a row by hand, after its teardown.
  - a new row (or a rename) taking a slug that another workshop on the server uses, or a retired one (§2.1); where a current workshop has it, the refusal carries §2.1's hint. The script checks this itself, at the dry run and at apply: the bot passes `--workshop-roster WS=ROSTER` for every other workshop whose roster exists, and `--retired` with `retired-slugs.txt` when it exists (a missing file holds no slug: a brand-new workshop has no roster yet). A file passed that can't be read refuses everything.
  - a `region` that is not the workshop's, `--region` (§2.6), in any row of the upload.
  - Any other `email` or `github` change is a plain change (§2.5), for example correcting the e-mail of a participant whose bots ignore `!login` because they never joined under the roster address.
  - **The live checks.** The script reads the Zulip users and invitations with `--zuliprc` (`GET /users`, deactivated users included, and `GET /invites`), the old login's permission and invitations with `GH_TOKEN`, and the instances tagged with the `--tag` scope and `participant=<slug>` in the row's region (or `--region`), in any state but terminated. It reads them only for the rows whose `email`, `github` or `region` changes and the rows removed, at the dry run and again at apply. A check it cannot make refuses the change. `zulip_invite = joined` counts as a `zulip_user_id`, which a run without `--no-rebind` may have cleared. A user whose `delivery_email` is null hides their address from the key, so the listing cannot show that nobody has an address: while one does, these e-mail changes and removals go to a deployer. With the provisioner's key, a Moderator's (Q6), that is any human whose visibility is "admins" or "nobody". The check fails closed, and a deployer makes the change by hand. GitHub answers 404 both for a repository that does not exist and for one the token cannot see, so a 404 counts as "no access" only for a login GitHub does not know, or for a row `provision_repos.py` never ran on.
- **The plan.** It shows the counts and the per-row diff, and reminds the organizer what follows, for example "2 new rows: !launch, then !provision zulip …".
- **Apply.** The roster is written atomically, keeping `roster.csv.bak`, as the scripts already do.

### 3.1a The roster from the workshop's channel: `!roster sync` (#627)

Decided on #627: the roster comes from the workshop's private participants' channel (`channel` in its entry, §2.9) rather than a CSV the organizers type. They invite people in Zulip by e-mail with the channel ticked, and each subscriber becomes a row, **linked by Zulip user ID from the start**, so that no row depends on matching an e-mail address. `!roster import` (§3.1) stays, for a roster made elsewhere and as the fallback. One script, `workshop/admin/roster_sync.py`, with the provisioner's key, which must be subscribed to the channel, and the instance role for EC2; the bot passes the same `--retired` and `--workshop-roster` as for `!roster import`, and `--out-dir`.

- **`!roster sync`** (`--sync <channel ID>`): the channel's active human subscribers (`GET /streams/{id}/members`, then `GET /users`), never the key's own user (`users/me`): `zab-provisioner` is a Moderator *user* in the channel, not a bot, and gets no row. Organizers get rows; one who wants no VM is excluded (below).
  - **A subscriber without a row** gets one: `zulip_user_id`; `name`, their Zulip full name without its control or format characters; `email`, their delivery address when the key sees it, else Zulip's `email` field (a placeholder `user<id>@<org host>` for an address hidden from moderators, DECISIONS 2026-10-04). An address the scripts' e-mail rule refuses (`aws_common._EMAIL_RE`: an apostrophe, which Zulip takes) gets the placeholder too, and its `+` line says so: one such subscriber never refuses the whole sync, and the row is linked by its ID. The plan shows each addition on one line (slug, Zulip ID, name, e-mail), so that a first sync of 35 people fits the 40 lines a phone sees inline.
  - **Its slug** (`roster_sync.slug_for`): the name NFKD-folded to ASCII, with a small table for the letters NFKD leaves alone (`ø`, `ß`, `ł`, `æ`, …: an interpretation of "folded to ASCII"), lower case, each run of other characters one hyphen, outer hyphens trimmed, cut to 32 characters at a hyphen where possible. Empty, reserved (the grammar's keywords, `zab-spare-N`) or otherwise unusable: `p-<user ID>`. Unique on the server for its whole life (§2.1): a slug of this roster, of another workshop's roster or `retired-slugs.txt`, or one the provisioner's key sees a bot (`Claude · <slug>`, `Codex · <slug>`) or a sandbox (`#<slug>-sandbox`) named after, or with an `out/<slug>/`, takes `-2`, `-3`, …, its stem cut to fit.
  - **A row** is matched by `zulip_user_id`; a legacy row without one, by its e-mail. Its `name` is refreshed; nothing else changes.
  - **A row no subscriber matches** (they left, or were deactivated) is removed if nothing is provisioned for it (`roster_sync.holds`): no VM (`host`/`ip`, or an instance EC2 has with its `participant` tag, in any state but terminated), no bots (their keys in `out/`, or a bot of their names), no sandbox (`zulip_channel_id`, or a channel of its name), no repository (`repo_status` or `github_invite`). Otherwise it is reported, `= <slug>: left the channel …, but <what it holds>: left alone`, and its end is the organizers' (`!retire`). A check that can't be made keeps the row and says so, and a sync with nothing else to change is then refused (exit 1, `UNCHECKED`), never answered as nothing to change (#708).
  - **An excluded row** is never added again, refreshed or removed: it is listed, `x <slug>: excluded`.
  - **The channel's privacy** is read at every sync: a public channel's plan starts with a `WARNING:` line, since anyone in the organization could join it and be offered a row. A private channel's subscribers may add others too where the organization lets members subscribe others (DECISIONS, 2026-10-06): the plan, which lists every addition, is the check, and ORGANIZERS §3.1 tells the organizers to read it.
  - **The plan** is hashed on its dry run (§2.4), whole (`actions.normalize_exact`: the listing holds no path, time or duration, and `normalize_dry_run` would read a slug like `bob-5s` as a duration): every addition with its slug, every name changed, every removal, every row left alone and every exclusion. A join or a departure between the plan and its approval makes it STALE. A sync with nothing to change exits 3 (`actions.NOTHING`, the verb's `nothing`): the bot offers no plan, and answers `☑`, a success (§2.4 step 2, #708), rather than offer a plan that changes nothing.
  - **The apply** reads everything again and writes the roster only if the changes it finds are the approved ones: each dry run writes its list of changes to the plan's `runs/P-n/roster-sync.txt` (`--changes`), the last one being the runner's recheck at the apply's start, whose hash must be the plan's (§2.4, step 5), and the apply compares its own list with that file, writing nothing on any difference (`… are not the approved changes: request !roster sync again`). So someone who joins or leaves in the seconds between the recheck and the apply is never added or removed unapproved (#641). It writes the roster atomically with its `.bak` (`write_roster`), and only if the file is still the one it read; the workshop's apply lock serializes it with every other apply.
- **`!roster remove slug`** (`--remove`): marks the row excluded, its `excluded` cell `yes`. `all` leaves it out of every verb (`Fleet.in_all`), and a change verb that names it is refused (`actions.KEEP_EXCLUDED`: only `!status`, `!roster restore` and `!roster rename` may). `!roster show` marks it. Refused while anything is provisioned for the row (`holds`, as above), and for a row excluded already.
- **`!roster restore slug`** (`--restore`): clears the mark. Refused for a row that isn't excluded.
- **`!roster rename slug new-slug`** (`--rename`): `remove`'s precondition; `new-slug` the grammar's slug shape, no keyword, no `zab-spare-N`, and free under the rules above, a bot, a sandbox or an `out/` directory of its name included. A slug of another workshop's is refused as `!roster import` refuses it (`… is in <ws>; a slug is never shared between workshops`), with §2.1's hint.
- **Their hash** (remove, restore, rename): their inputs, the roster and the others' (§2.4's `roster import` row). The script checks Zulip and EC2 again at apply, and refuses what changed since (#589's rule).
- **Downstream of a row linked at creation** (criterion 4 of #627):
  - `!provision zulip`: with `--no-rebind`, a row whose e-mail binds nobody (a placeholder the key no longer sees as such, once the user widens their address's visibility) and whose recorded `zulip_user_id` is an active human's is matched by that ID (`zulip_provision.ZULIP_ID`), and no other row may take that user. A row whose e-mail binds *another* user stays refused, `binding changed`, as before. Without `--no-rebind` (a deployer's rebind by hand, §2.5), the e-mail alone binds;
  - `!rollout` writes `allow_user_ids` with the ID, beside `allow_emails` with the row's e-mail (a placeholder resolves to the same user); `!waker on` passes both; `!provision repos` reads the GitHub field of the user the ID names;
  - git's `user.name` on the VM is `name`, else `first_name last_name`, else the slug (`aws_common.git_identity`); `user.email` the row's e-mail;
  - `!roster import` keeps `name` and `excluded` from the current roster (script columns), and still takes CSVs with `first_name` and `last_name`.

### 3.2 Script changes, collected

The PRs in §8 carry these.

**Stages 1 and 2.**

Done on `main`:
- **#316 (PR 1):**
  - `launch.py`: repeatable `--only`, and `--spares-file`;
  - `teardown.py`: repeatable `--only`; it **skips any instance or Elastic IP without a `participant` tag**, so that nothing but a participant VM or a spare can be selected even by `--tag` alone (a guard for the manual path too);
  - `adopt_spare.py`: the retag sends only `Name` and `participant`, never the unchanged `workshop` key, so that it passes §5's `aws:TagKeys` condition.
- **#320 (PR 3):**
  - `status.py --json`, with the zab version, the idle-stop timer and its last verdict, and the last activity. VERIFY V7 found that `zab status --json` lacked the field, and #320 added `last_activity`, which VMs report from the release after 0.2.3;
  - `status.py --tag`;
  - `zulip_provision.py --repo-owner` and `--no-rebind`;
  - `provision_repos.py --no-rebind`.

Done since:
- **New scripts:** `workshop/aws/power.py` (PR 2) and `workshop/admin/roster_import.py` (PR 4, #317).
- **Dependencies:** `boto3` (and its dependencies) in `uv.lock`, in an `admin` dependency group; `hatchling` pinned to an exact version, with a hashed `build-constraints.txt` (§1.6).

Not built, and not planned: `zulip_provision.py --rebind SLUG=OLD:NEW` and `provision_repos.py --rebind SLUG=OLD:NEW`, for `!rebind` (#369).

No change needed:
- **`protect.py` and `push_config.py`** already have `--dry-run`, an `--only`, and flags for every path.
- **`scan_hosts.py`** has no dry run and needs none: it writes only the server's own `known_hosts`, and `--replace` is not in the grammar.

**Later stages:**

- Stage 3: **new** `workshop/admin/collect_exports.py` (done in PR 18). `final_sync.py` takes the idle-stop hold before it syncs a VM, as `push_config.py` does: done in #370. `teardown.py --yes` needs no further change (PR 19): the bot bypasses its prompt after the typed confirmation in Zulip.
- Stage 3 (#559): `teardown.py --strict`, for `!retire`: a VM selected but still read as protected is `refused`, its address kept, and the run fails (§3.3).
- Stage 3 (#604): **a released address given again.** EC2 gave `zab-test-a`'s Elastic IP, released by A9's teardown, to `zab-test-r`'s new VM on Oct 23. `zab-test-a`'s row kept it, and every admin script refused the roster (`duplicate ip`), so the workshop was stuck. Three changes, one at each place the address changes hands:
  - `teardown.py --roster`, which `!teardown` and `!retire` pass, clears the `ip` of every row at an address it released and keeps its `host`, so that `launch.py` still leaves the row alone;
  - `launch.py` and `adopt_spare.py` clear the `ip` of any other row at the address EC2 shows on the VM they record, and say so. That also unsticks a roster stuck already: `!launch` of the row whose VM has the address, which `check_duplicates`' refusal now names as the fix. **Except a spare handed over by hand** (ORGANIZERS §7.2, "A broken VM"; #606): the participant's row then holds the spare's `ip` and `host_keys` rightly, while the VM keeps the spare's tag (`power.py` and `fleet.read_spares` accept that). So `launch.py` only reports a row at an existing spare's address (`check …: holds zab-spare-N's address`) and leaves it alone; a spare it launches in the run, whose address is new, still clears. And `adopt_spare.py` clears a row at the spare's address only when its `host_keys` and the spare's share no fingerprint, and otherwise refuses the hand-over before any change: one in common is a hand-over by hand, and with no fingerprints on either side the two can't be told apart;
  - `scan_hosts.py` replaces a pinned key that differs when the row's `host_keys`, from the VM's console, vouch for the new one, as it records a first key: the old one was the previous VM's at that address. An unverified key is still refused (`CHANGED`).
  - **Why the roster, and not the readers.** Every script that reaches a VM does so by its row's `ip` (`admin_common.host_of`; `power.py`, `status.py`, the console's mapping, `push_config.py`, `final_sync.py`, `collect_exports.py`, `scan_hosts.py`), and the bot counts a row with an `ip` as launched (`all`, the above-half phrase rule's fleet, `!retire`'s start). A stale `ip` is wrong for each of them, collision or not: once the address is another VM's and its key pinned, a script would reach that VM as the torn-down row's. So the roster stops claiming it, rather than each reader guessing. A torn-down row's `host` stays, so `!launch` still leaves it alone, and #565's refusal of a retired row's `!launch` or `!adopt-spare`, which reads `host` or `ip`, still holds. One case none of this heals: a row made stale before #604 whose address EC2 gave to a VM of another workshop (the same account and region). It jams nothing and the pinning protects it, but it still counts as launched; a deployer clears its `ip` by hand, as §3.3's roster command does, keeping its `host`.
- Stage 4 (PR 21): `push_waker.py` gets `--remove`, `--tag`, `--region`, and the `--config-dir` mode of §3, action 5. The waker (`src/zab/waker`) gets its workshops from config, one waker each in one process, the group-readable key rule, each workshop's alerts channel, and the starter (§1.2).

### 3.3 `!retire`: one plan that ends named rows (#559)

Ending two kept VMs took nine plans (#559). `!retire alice bob [export] [unsynced]` does it in one, built from the separate verbs' scripts (action 7); they stay for repairs (ORGANIZERS §5.8).

- **Each row in turn, alone.** For each named row, in roster order, the runner runs that row's steps (`actions.STAGES`): start and wait (`power.py --start --wait`), idle stop off, the waker off, `!uninstall`, `!final-sync`, `!export` with `export`, its protection off, `!teardown`, `!deactivate-bots`, each with `--only <row>`. So each exit code is that row's own. Rows go one after the other, never at once: `push_waker.py` and `zulip_provision.py` rewrite files the rows share. Before the rows, one `power.py --start` without `--wait` of every row with an `ip`, its failure tolerated, so that stopped VMs boot together.
- **A row stops at its first failure** (`runner.Rows`): its later steps are `not run`, and the other rows go on. A row whose sync fails is not torn down, nor a row whose protection can't be lifted. `unsynced` tolerates the failure of the steps that save the row's work (start, idle stop, uninstall, sync, export), for every row of the plan: the plan says so, and the organizers retire the rows that need it in a plan of their own.
- **The VM synced is the VM torn down** (#564). The steps reach a row's VM by the roster's address or by its `participant` tag in any case (`provider.targets`, `Live.vms`); its protection and teardown end the VMs tagged `participant=<slug>` exactly (`provider.tagged`, `Live.tagged`). At plan time and at the apply's recheck, both hashed, a row for which the two differ is refused, naming it and what to fix: a legacy tag in another case, a spare handed over by hand that keeps `participant=zab-spare-N`, or a second VM at the row's address. The plan shows each row's VMs as found, and flags a VM away from the row's roster address, which its steps can't reach. (Passing instance IDs to the scripts would give the same guarantee; the argument lists are built, and hashed, before the lookup, so the selection is pinned and checked instead.)
- **A deployer's fix of a refused row** (#566). The bot has no verb that retags a VM, so a deployer does it, with their own AWS profile in the fleet's account. The case in mind: VMs launched before slugs existed (#97, #114), tagged with the participant's login as written (`participant=<Login>`), which `!retire` refuses. Run these in bash, with `R` the workshop's `aws.region` in `admin.toml` and `W` its name:
  1. **The check, read-only, well before any month end:** every live VM of the workshop with its `participant` tag. Run steps 2 and 3 in the same terminal: they reuse `P` and `R`.

     ```sh
     export AWS_PAGER=""   # the AWS CLI prints, never pages
     P=<your profile>; R=<region>; W=<ws>
     LIVE=Name=instance-state-name,Values=pending,running,stopping,stopped
     aws ec2 describe-instances --profile $P --region $R --filters Name=tag:workshop,Values=$W $LIVE \
       --query 'Reservations[].Instances[].[InstanceId,Tags[?Key==`participant`].Value|[0],NetworkInterfaces[0].Association.PublicIp]' \
       --output text
     ```

     It prints one line per VM: `i-…`, its tag and its address. The address is its network interface's, which a stopped VM keeps with its Elastic IP (`PublicIpAddress` may read `None` then); `None` there means a VM with no public address. Compare each tag with that row's slug in `!roster show`, exactly, case included. A spare not handed over reads `zab-spare-N`. A tag that differs from its row's slug, in case or as a spare's name on a participant's VM, needs step 2. A row with two VMs (two lines with its slug, or one with its slug and another at its `ip`) needs step 3.
  2. **The retag** of one row's VM: the instance, its volume and its Elastic IP. `teardown.py` releases an address only if it is tagged `participant=<slug>`, so an address left with the old tag would stay allocated, and billed, after its VM goes. These are the two keys `adopt_spare.py` sets, the only ones the instance role may change after creation (§5's `Retag`); never change the `workshop` or `zab-scope` tags.

     ```sh
     I=<i-… from step 1>; SLUG=<the row slug>
     V=$(aws ec2 describe-volumes --profile $P --region $R --filters Name=attachment.instance-id,Values=$I \
       --query 'Volumes[].VolumeId' --output text)
     E=$(aws ec2 describe-addresses --profile $P --region $R --filters Name=instance-id,Values=$I \
       --query 'Addresses[].AllocationId' --output text)
     echo $V $E                      # one vol-… and one eipalloc-…
     aws ec2 create-tags --profile $P --region $R --resources $I $V $E \
       --tags Key=participant,Value=$SLUG Key=Name,Value=ws-$SLUG
     ```

     Then read the volume's and the address's tags back, since the teardown finds both by them:

     ```sh
     aws ec2 describe-volumes --profile $P --region $R --volume-ids $V --output text \
       --query 'Volumes[].[VolumeId,Tags[?Key==`participant`].Value|[0],Tags[?Key==`Name`].Value|[0]]'
     [ -n "$E" ] && aws ec2 describe-addresses --profile $P --region $R --allocation-ids $E --output text \
       --query 'Addresses[].[AllocationId,PublicIp,Tags[?Key==`participant`].Value|[0],Tags[?Key==`Name`].Value|[0]]'
     ```

     It prints two lines: `vol-…`, then `eipalloc-…` and the row's `ip`, each with `$SLUG` and `ws-$SLUG`. A VM with no Elastic IP (step 1 printed `None` for its address, and `echo $V $E` no `eipalloc-…`) has no address to tag, and prints only the volume's line. Another value, or an error, means the retag didn't take: run step 2's block again. Then step 1 again: the VM's line shows `$SLUG`. The row's `!retire` now finds one VM, tagged with its slug.
  3. **A row with two VMs** (refused: #564). The participant's is the one at the row's `ip` in `!roster show`, which step 1 prints; retag it with step 2 if its tag isn't the slug. The other is a stray, such as a launch cut short left: nothing reaches it at the roster's address. Snapshot its volume first, so that nothing on it is lost, with `X` its instance ID and `IP` the row's `ip` as `!roster show` prints it, both from step 1:

     ```sh
     X=<the stray i-…>; IP=<the row ip>
     XV=$(aws ec2 describe-volumes --profile $P --region $R --filters Name=attachment.instance-id,Values=$X \
       --query 'Volumes[].VolumeId' --output text); echo $XV    # one vol-…
     SNAP=$(aws ec2 create-snapshot --profile $P --region $R --volume-id $XV --description "stray $X, before its end" \
       --tag-specifications "ResourceType=snapshot,Tags=[{Key=Name,Value=stray-$X}]" --query SnapshotId --output text)
     ST=; echo $SNAP                                             # snap-…
     ```

     Then wait for the snapshot to complete:

     ```sh
     while [ -n "$SNAP" ]; do   # every 30 s until it is complete: minutes for a large disk
       ST=$(aws ec2 describe-snapshots --profile $P --region $R --snapshot-ids $SNAP \
         --query 'Snapshots[0].[State,Progress]' --output text); echo "$SNAP $ST"
       case $ST in completed*|error*|"") break ;; esac; sleep 30
     done
     ```

     It prints `snap-… pending 0%`, then more, then `snap-… completed 100%`. **Ctrl-C is safe here:** it only stops the wait, and the snapshot goes on in EC2. An error message, or a line with no state, means a read failed. In either case run this second block again, never the first, which would make a second snapshot: `SNAP` is still set. With `error`, stop: keep the VM, and read the error. Once it says `completed`, end the VM. EC2 shows the protection lifted late, and enforces it late too (#378: a termination was refused for 157 s on Oct 15), so the block reads it back every 10 s, then tries the termination every 30 s, 5 minutes at most each. It first checks that the snapshot completed and that `X` is the stray: a VM of workshop `$W`, not at the row's address. Both VMs carry the row's tag, so a mistyped `X` could otherwise end the participant's VM:

     ```sh
     XW=; XIP=   # set only by a lookup that succeeds: a failed one leaves them empty, and stops
     XI=$(aws ec2 describe-instances --profile $P --region $R --instance-ids $X --output text \
       --query 'Reservations[0].Instances[0].[Tags[?Key==`workshop`].Value|[0],NetworkInterfaces[0].Association.PublicIp]') \
       && read -r XW XIP <<< "$XI"
     if [ "${ST%%[[:space:]]*}" = completed ] && [ -n "$W" ] && [ "$XW" = "$W" ] \
        && [ -n "$IP" ] && [ -n "$XIP" ] && [ "$XIP" != "$IP" ]; then
       aws ec2 modify-instance-attribute --profile $P --region $R --instance-id $X --no-disable-api-termination
       for try in $(seq 30); do
         ON=$(aws ec2 describe-instance-attribute --profile $P --region $R --instance-id $X \
           --attribute disableApiTermination --query DisableApiTermination.Value --output text)
         [ "$ON" = False ] && break; sleep 10
       done; echo "protected: $ON"   # protected: False
       GONE=
       [ "$ON" = False ] && for try in $(seq 10); do   # a refusal prints an error, then tries again
         GONE=$(aws ec2 terminate-instances --profile $P --region $R --instance-ids $X \
           --query 'TerminatingInstances[0].CurrentState.Name' --output text) && break; sleep 30
       done; echo "terminating: $GONE"   # terminating: shutting-down
       case $GONE in shutting-down|terminated) aws ec2 wait instance-terminated --profile $P --region $R \
         --instance-ids $X && echo terminated ;; esac   # terminated, within a few minutes
     else
       echo "stop, nothing done: snapshot '${ST%%[[:space:]]*}' (must be 'completed'), workshop '$XW'" \
         "(must be '$W'), address '$XIP' (must not be the row's, '$IP')"
     fi
     ```

     It ends `terminated`. **`stop, nothing done`** means a check failed, and the line says which: the snapshot not `completed`; `X` in another workshop, or none (a mistyped ID); `X`'s lookup failed (an empty workshop and address, with its error above); or `X` at the row's address, which makes it the participant's VM, not the stray. A stray with no public address reads `None`, and passes. **One case the check can't catch:** a participant's VM that has no Elastic IP and is stopped reads `None` too, so a mistyped `X` naming it would pass. Every fleet VM has an Elastic IP (the roster's `ip` is one), so this is a VM made by hand. If step 1 printed `None` for both of the row's VMs, look at `X` before running the block, with `` aws ec2 describe-instances --profile $P --region $R --instance-ids $X --output text --query 'Reservations[0].Instances[0].[InstanceId,State.Name,LaunchTime,Tags[?Key==`Name`].Value|[0]]' ``, and compare it with the participant's VM. If you can't tell which one is the participant's, stop, and keep both. Check `X`, `IP` and step 1's lines, and don't go on until it is right. When the checks pass but it doesn't end `terminated`, the VM is still there: `protected: True` (or no value) means EC2 never showed the protection lifted, and `terminating:` with no state that every try was refused (`OperationNotPermitted`). Read the errors, and run this block again a few minutes later. The snapshot stays, at about $0.05 a GB-month: once you know nothing on it is needed, delete it with `aws ec2 delete-snapshot --profile $P --region $R --snapshot-id $SNAP`. The stray's Elastic IP, if it had one tagged with the row, is released with the row's own by the row's `!retire`.
- **A row with no VM.** At plan time, a row with no VM found is refused without `unsynced`, naming it. With `unsynced`, the steps that need the VM are `skipped: no VM` (`Step.gone`), and the waker, the teardown (which finds nothing) and the bots still run: a VM terminated by hand is retired so. A row with no `ip` (never launched) needs `unsynced` too, as for `!teardown`. A VM that appears or goes between plan and apply makes the plan STALE.
- **Protection.** `protect.py --off` reads the protection back every 10 s until EC2 shows it, for 300 s at most (#378), and a failure of it stops the row; `teardown.py --strict` then fails a VM that EC2 still reads as protected, rather than keep it and exit 0, and its own retries cover EC2's late enforcement (300 s more). So no VM counts as torn down while EC2 reads it as protected, and there is no fixed wait. A `!retire` is not a protection change for #378's guard (`fleet.protects`): it only lifts the protection of rows it then tears down, and the guard is for a protection turned on and read late. The guard does refuse a `!retire` as it refuses a `!teardown`, within 5 minutes of the workshop's last `!protect` or `!launch … protect`.
- **Export and time.** One VM's export may take 55 minutes (`collect_exports.py`: 20 to export, 20 to fetch, 15 to re-pack), so `export` takes at most 3 rows (`actions.EXPORT_ROWS`), and the apply's deadline is the action's own (`Action.timeout`): 20 minutes a row and 60 at least (one row's worst case: the start's wait, a 30-minute sync, the read-back, the teardown's retries), 55 more a row with `export`. The apply holds the workshop's lock throughout, so the organizers retire in batches of about 8 rows, after one row alone for its timings (ORGANIZERS §5.2).
- **The hash** (`RETIRE`, §2.4): each row's VMs and the teardown's dry run of every row, normalized as `!teardown`'s, with the roster and the rows' bot keys among the inputs. A VM that appears or goes, a protection or an address changed, makes the plan STALE (`carol: no VM → i-3`).
- **The class:** destructive (§2.5), with the phrase `retire <n>`, and `unsynced` after it when the command says it. `all` is refused, by the grammar (`VerbSpec.named`) and again by the action.
- **The plan** shows each row's address, its bots (from `out/<row>/*.zuliprc`) and what becomes of its work, the teardown's dry run, the rows' status, and a reminder to have the participants sign their VMs out of Codex and Claude before the approval.
- **The result** is one table: a row per participant, a column per step (`ok`, `skipped: no VM`, `failed: exit N`, `failed: no VM`, `not run`), its `time` and `retired`. The headline names the rows torn down after a failure `unsynced` tolerated. Under the table, each stopped row: what its VM is left as (idle stop off, the waker off, its bridge uninstalled), how to send it again, and the commands that put it back in service instead, from the ledger's release and idle-stop minutes (not run on their own: the organizers may retire it again once fixed). Then each archive exported, and the reminder to revoke the participants' other sessions (ORGANIZERS §5.3). A result too long for a message attaches the whole of it. `apply.txt` frames each run with its row, and `apply_end` names each step's row and each row's seconds.
- **The ledger** (§2.8; `fleet.Ledger`). `!retire` is a `TOUCH` verb: its `apply_start` forgets its rows' syncs. Its `apply_end` names each row's cells (`rows`); whatever the plan's exit, a row's uninstall that is `ok` marks it uninstalled, and its sync, which follows it, synced, as the separate verbs would. A row that ran to its last step is **retired**: `all` leaves it out for every verb (`power.py` gets its rows by name then, not `--all`), the above-half phrase rule's fleet leaves it out, `!status all` names it once under its answer, and the console leaves it out once it has no VM. Named, a retired row is refused by the verbs that reach its VM by its address or its tag (`actions.REACH_VM`, and `!waker on`): its address was released, and may be another VM's by now. **What brings it back** (#565). It keeps its `host` in the roster (its teardown cleared its `ip`, #604), with which `launch.py` leaves a row alone and exits 0, and `adopt_spare.py` refuses it, while its `apply_start` would put the row back in `all`. So `!launch` and `!adopt-spare` of a retired row are refused while its roster keeps either (`actions.BACK`). A deployer clears both, as `zab-admin` on the server, while no plan of the workshop applies, from BOOTSTRAP §2.1's terminal, with `S` the server as BOOTSTRAP §3.6 pins its host key:

  ```sh
  S=<host>; W=<the workshop>; SLUG=<the row>
  ssh $S sudo -u zab-admin python3 -I - /var/lib/zab-admin/ws/$W/roster.csv $SLUG <<'EOF'
  import csv, os, shutil, sys, tempfile
  path, slug = sys.argv[1], sys.argv[2]
  with open(path, newline="", encoding="utf-8-sig") as f:
      reader = csv.DictReader(f)
      fields, rows = reader.fieldnames, list(reader)
  hits = [row for row in rows if (row.get("slug") or "").strip() == slug]
  if len(hits) != 1:
      sys.exit(f"{slug}: {len(hits)} rows in the roster; nothing changed")
  hits[0].update(host="", ip="")
  fd, tmp = tempfile.mkstemp(dir=os.path.dirname(path), prefix="roster.csv.", suffix=".tmp")
  try:
      with os.fdopen(fd, "w", newline="", encoding="utf-8") as f:
          writer = csv.DictWriter(f, fieldnames=fields)
          writer.writeheader()
          writer.writerows(rows)
      os.chmod(tmp, os.stat(path).st_mode & 0o777)
      shutil.copy2(path, path + ".bak")
      os.replace(tmp, path)
  except BaseException:
      os.remove(tmp)
      raise
  print("cleared; the roster as it was is roster.csv.bak")
  EOF
  ```

  It prints `cleared; the roster as it was is roster.csv.bak`: the roster is written whole at once, its mode kept, with the old one as `roster.csv.bak`, as the scripts write it (`aws_common.write_roster`); a failure leaves the roster as it was, and no temporary file. `!roster show <slug>` then shows no `host` and no `ip`.

  **Then its bots, which the `!retire` deactivated.** No verb reactivates a bot, and `!provision zulip <slug>` can't make new ones in their place, since the deactivated bots keep the e-mail addresses that bots of the same names would take (§7's throwaway row meets the same limit). Zulip lets a bot's owner, or an organization administrator, reactivate it (`can_admin_user`; DECISIONS, the verification log, #762). The `!retire` deactivated only bots the provisioner owns: `zulip_provision.py --deactivate` leaves any other bot alone, and fails. So the provisioner's key reactivates them, from the same terminal:

  ```sh
  ssh $S sudo -u zab-admin python3 -I - /etc/zab-admin/secrets/provisioner.zuliprc \
    /var/lib/zab-admin/ws/$W/out/$SLUG <<'EOF'
  import base64, configparser, json, sys, urllib.error, urllib.parse, urllib.request
  def api(rc):  # a zuliprc's [api]; never shows a parse error, which may quote the key's line
      config = configparser.ConfigParser(interpolation=None)
      try:
          found = config.read(rc, encoding="utf-8")
      except (configparser.Error, UnicodeDecodeError):
          found = []
      if not found or not all(config.get("api", k, fallback="") for k in ("site", "email", "key")):
          sys.exit("%s: missing, or not a zuliprc" % rc)
      return config["api"]
  prov = api(sys.argv[1])
  pair = base64.b64encode((prov["email"] + ":" + prov["key"]).encode()).decode()
  def call(method, path):
      url = prov["site"].rstrip("/") + "/api/v1/" + path
      request = urllib.request.Request(url, data=b"" if method == "POST" else None, method=method)
      request.add_header("Authorization", "Basic " + pair)
      try:
          with urllib.request.urlopen(request, timeout=30) as answer:
              return json.load(answer)
      except urllib.error.HTTPError as error:  # Zulip's answer names the cause, never the key
          answer = error.read(300).decode(errors="replace")
          sys.exit("%s %s: HTTP %d %s" % (method, path, error.code, answer))
  me = call("GET", "users/me")["user_id"]
  for kind in ("claude", "codex"):
      email = api("%s/%s.zuliprc" % (sys.argv[2], kind))["email"]
      bot = call("GET", "users/" + urllib.parse.quote(email, safe="@"))["user"]
      if not bot.get("is_bot") or bot.get("bot_owner_id") != me:
          print("%s: %s is not the provisioner's bot: left alone" % (kind, email))
      elif bot.get("is_active"):
          print("%s: %s is active already" % (kind, email))
      else:
          call("POST", "users/%d/reactivate" % bot["user_id"])
          print("%s: %s reactivated" % (kind, email))
  EOF
  ```

  It prints a line per bot, `claude: <its e-mail> reactivated`, then codex's; run again, `is active already`. It reads each bot's e-mail from the zuliprc in `out/<slug>/` that the `!retire` read, and prints no key. A bot that another user owns is `left alone`: only that owner, or an organization administrator, can reactivate it, in Zulip's bot settings. A row without a zuliprc there stops at `…/claude.zuliprc: missing, or not a zuliprc`. Its `!retire` then found neither that file nor an active bot of the provisioner's with the row's names, which it reports `absent`: none was made for it, or they are another user's. (A bot of those names with no file there is reported `is missing` instead, and fails the `!retire`.) `!provision zulip <slug>` makes the bots of its names that are missing, or `!provision zulip hold <slug>` while the workshop's sandboxes are still held (ORGANIZERS §3.3), since without `hold` the run would subscribe the row's participant and post "Start here". A refusal stops it with the call and Zulip's answer, such as `POST users/…/reactivate: HTTP 400 {…"msg":"Insufficient permission"…}`, or `There is already an active bot named "…"` when an active user of the organization has the bot's full name since: rename that user first.

  **The bots come back with their keys.** Zulip keeps a bot's key through its deactivation, so the key `out/<slug>/` holds works again, and so does every other copy of it: the server's backups hold one (§1.7, *Old backups are never deleted*), and so did the row's old VM. When that isn't wanted, give the row's bots new keys after the reactivation, while no plan of the workshop applies, since the script writes the roster. In a shell on the server (`ssh $S`), run §2.5's first block, with `WS` the workshop and `SLUG` the row (`NEW` is unused). That shell is not this section's terminal: its `W` is §2.5's, the release's `workshop/` directory. Then the bot's own flags, with `--no-rebind`, `--regenerate-keys` and `--only`, its dry run first:

