## 10. Decisions (Q1–Q16)

The design's questions, as decided between Oct 15 and Oct 25, 2026. Each answer is also in the sections it affects.

- **Q1: Which account runs the participant VMs and the admin server?** One AWS account runs both: the server lives in the account of the fleets it manages. The deployer creates the server's IAM role and instance profile, security group, Elastic IP and DNS record with the account's own credentials (or the account's owner does), following BOOTSTRAP §3–§4 (§1.1, §5).
- **Q2: Who is on the allowlists?** The global admins are the deployer and the lead organizer(s), named at bootstrap (§1.6, §2.1). Each workshop's organizers are scoped to it. Helpers who only answer the participants' questions stay off every list.
- **Q3: Do destructive actions need two approvers?** Yes, for `teardown`, `deactivate-bots`, `retire` and rebinding, with the typed phrase; but a global admin's approval alone completes such a plan, and a server plan (#558, DECISIONS D36): the two-approver rule binds the organizers who are not global admins. A change needs one approver, who may be the requester, and the phrase above half the fleet (§2.5).
- **Q4: The console's hostname.** A name in the deployer's own domain, an A record for the server's Elastic IP that the deployer adds (BOOTSTRAP §4); `console_url` names it (§4.3). On the test rig, A11 reaches the console over SSH (§7).
- **Q5: Release signing.** Tags are signed with the deployer's dedicated key (§1.6), a plain `ssh-ed25519` key file with a passphrase (#429). It serves zab's four roles: SSH to the server, signing, break-glass access to the VMs, and backups, whose recipients must be plain key files (§1.7). It is never a personal key that also reaches other systems, and zab work runs with an agent that holds it alone (BOOTSTRAP §2.1).
- **Q6: A provisioning account.** `zab-provisioner`, a **Moderator** (#397). A Zulip organization administrator creates it. It owns the participant bots and is never subscribed to an admin bot's channel (§1.5, §5).
  - **Why a Moderator:** it binds roster rows by e-mail wherever a user's visibility includes moderators, which a Member's key can't where the visibility excludes members (V5). It is the lowest role that can, and it can't change org settings or roles.
  - **The org's default visibility** for new users stays "Admins, moderators and members" (API 2). The participants' bots, which are members, can then read their participant's address too.
  - **No invitations (#628).** Where only administrators may invite (*Who can send email invitations to new users* under *Organization settings → Organization permissions*, `can_invite_users_group`), a Moderator's `POST /invites` answers `Insufficient permission` (#610). So the provisioner stays a Moderator, and `!provision zulip` sends no invitations: the organizers invite in Zulip, with the participants' channel ticked (ORGANIZERS §1.5).
- **Q7: The GitHub token.** A fine-grained PAT. Its expiry must cover the kept month after each workshop: set it to the end of a workshop series rather than renew it per workshop. Renewing it is a secret replaced on the running server, by hand: BOOTSTRAP §16 (#406). A GitHub App (P-c, not built) would replace it; it becomes a higher priority at scale (§5, §11.8).
- **Q8: A fleet launched before its server.** It moves to the server rather than keep a waker of its own, and its rollouts, waker opt-ins and idle stop then go through its own admin bot (§8.4).
- **Q9: Test infrastructure for the admin e2e.** Three test instances at once, about $1 a run, are allowed (§7). The rig provisions with a test user's key, `zab-tester`'s, a Moderator as `zab-provisioner` is; production always uses `zab-provisioner`. On the rig the provisioner is also an organizer in the test channels, so the rig doesn't test §1.5's separation; a third test user would. The test server's GitHub token is a short-lived fine-grained PAT, for the rig only; production gets its own at bootstrap (Q7).
- **Q10: Exports.** They come with the other end-of-workshop verbs (stage 3, §8.1), and are delivered (P-b, not built) only to participants who ask.
- **Q11: The merge policy for code that runs on the admin server.** A signature certifies that the commit passed CI, automated code review and the end-to-end suite, and that the deployer read its privileged surface themselves: the files `workshop/adminserver/privileged-paths` lists, the root helper's code, `pyproject.toml`, `build-constraints.txt` and `uv.lock` among them (§1.4, §1.6; D31, #432; D32, #436). The unprivileged code is the automated PR reviews' and the tests'.
- **Q12: A tag ruleset.** Recommended, on the repository whose tags the deployers sign (§1.6, BOOTSTRAP §2.4).
- **Q13: The go/no-go.** The day before a fleet's launch, on §8.2's criteria. On a no-go, the launch runs on the manual path, from a deployer's laptop.
- **Q14: Teardown.** Participants who opt in keep their VM for one month after every workshop; the others' VMs are synced and torn down the day after the workshop, so that no requests to the organizers trail on. Every workshop runs idle stop and the waker throughout, from launch day, and the kept VMs keep them for their month (ORGANIZERS §3.5, §4.2).
- **Q15: The cut order.** Superseded: all of zab's administration moved into one plan, whose stages §8.1 lists.
- **Q16: `!grow-disk`'s approvals** (#553). **As built: one approver,** as for any change, the recommendation. It adds a cost every month for the VM's life, $0.08 per GB, and EBS never shrinks a volume back. But the size is capped at 1,000 GB, so a VM costs at most $64 a month more; the plan shows that cost before anyone approves it; EBS allows one modification of a volume every 6 hours; and the audit log names who asked and who approved. The alternative, `destructive_approvals` approvers and a phrase such as `grow-disk alice 300`, needs a second organizer, who may not be there when a participant's disk is full in the middle of a workshop.

## 11. Scaling: 25 workshops, 1,000 participants

**The target:** about 25 workshops of 40 participants over six months, so 1,000 participants, each with one month of VM access, and several workshops at once. Nothing above may preclude it. This section says what changes with scale and what the design already does. Every figure here is an estimate. The unit prices of §11.2 and §11.9 match workshop fleets' bill (V13, Nov 7, 2026); the rest is still to verify (V13's per-VM figures, V14).

### 11.1 Concurrency and quotas

- **Kept VMs.** 1,000 participant-months over six months is about 170 kept VMs at any time, on average, and more where months overlap.
- **Workshop days.** On top come 40-VM workshop days, all running at once, and two workshops may overlap.
- **vCPU quotas are per region.** An `m7a.2xlarge` counts 8 vCPUs, so a quota of 192 runs 24 VMs at once, and a 40-VM workshop day needs 320 in its region, kept VMs running there included: check the quota before each launch, or use `r7a.xlarge` (4 vCPUs, 32 GiB; `workshop/aws/README.md`). Stopped VMs don't count, so idle stop keeps most kept VMs out of the quota. One region per workshop lets workshops spread over regions.
- **Elastic IP quotas** are per region too, and every VM holds its address, running or not. §11.9 would remove that limit.
- **The server.** It runs one event queue per admin bot, one apply at a time per workshop, and the collector over every region. V6 measures the `t4g.small`; a larger instance type is a change of the server alone.

### 11.2 Cost

Per participant-month on an `m7a.2xlarge` with a 200 GB gp3 volume (estimates; their unit prices, $0.46368 an hour, $0.08 a GB-month and $0.005 an hour for the address, match workshop fleets' bill, V13, Nov 7, 2026):

| Item | Estimate |
|---|---|
| Fixed, per kept VM: the volume (about $16) and its public IPv4 address (about $3.65) | about $20 a month |
| Running | about $0.46 an hour |
| A participant-month with idle stop, at 3–4 hours of use a day | about $60–80 |
| A participant-month without idle stop (730 hours) | about $355 |

- **The budget.** 1,000 participant-months then cost about $60–80k with idle stop, and about $355k without. **Idle stop and the waker are therefore essential to the budget**, not conveniences: every kept VM runs idle stop, with the waker so that its participant can resume.
- **Small by comparison:** workshop days (40 VMs for a day, about $150 each) and the admin server.
- **Levers, not decided:** a smaller volume; a shorter idle-stop timeout; private subnets (§11.9).
- **Cost reporting per workshop** (stage 5). The collector estimates each workshop's spend from what it sees: each VM's running hours from its EC2 state, sampled every 60 s, its volume size and its address, priced from a table of estimates: in `admin.toml` by design; a constant of the collector for now, since `config.py` is privileged (#489).
  - The console shows, per workshop, the month to date and the projection to the month's end, and the same per VM (§4.2).
  - `!status` adds one line, for example `cost (estimate): $412 this month, $1,050 projected · 9 of 40 running · spares: 0 of 3 running`: the VMs but the spares, then the spares (#715), as the console's workshop page and index count them, its totals with a `Spares` line.
  - The table has prices for us-east-1, us-east-2 and us-west-2, which are the same there. A workshop in any other region gets no dollars, rather than US prices, which run low elsewhere (eu-west-1's instances cost about 10 to 12% more; m7a, 11.5%): the console and `!status` say `no prices for <region>` (#499).
  - The exact figure is AWS's. Cost Explorer by a `workshop` cost-allocation tag would give it a day late, but activating that tag is a billing change, and `ce:GetCostAndUsage` is outside the role (§5). So that stays with the account owner, as a check on the estimate.

### 11.3 Subscriptions

Each participant brings their own Claude and ChatGPT subscription, and zab never holds an agent credential (D5, D15, D24). Participants' subscriptions may be sponsored; zab doesn't depend on it. What zab would need from a sponsored arrangement:

- **Bulk provisioning:** seats issued for the month to the participants' own addresses, in bulk, so that organizers don't buy 1,000 subscriptions one at a time.
- **The participant's own sign-in:** each participant still signs in to their seat in the unmodified CLIs, through `!login` (D24), so zab's rules hold unchanged. Whether a seat issued through an organization meets each provider's terms for hosted use is theirs to confirm.
- **An end date:** seats that end at the month's end, or that organizers can release, in step with teardown.
- **Limits for agent work:** usage limits that hold up through a workshop day of agent turns.

On zab's side this is little: perhaps a roster column for the seat's state, and a line in the month-end warning (§11.5).

### 11.4 Zulip

- **Bots.** Two bots per participant: about 2,000 over six months, and about 340 live at once for the kept VMs, plus 80 on a workshop day. The provisioner creates them, and `!deactivate-bots` retires them at the month's end.
- **The waker's event queues are a scaling risk.** The waker holds one event queue per opted-in bot: two per VM, so 80 for a workshop of 40. With every kept VM on idle stop, that becomes about 340 queues, long-polled by one process, each registered again after a queue expires or Zulip restarts (#295). Neither Zulip Cloud's tolerance of that load nor the waker's behavior has been measured (V14). Options, none decided:
  1. Keep one queue per bot, run one waker per workshop, and measure first.
  2. Zulip's outgoing webhooks: a bot of that type sends each DM and mention to an HTTPS endpoint on the admin server, with no queue. Whether such a bot can also serve the bridge, or a participant would need a second bot, is to check.
  3. One account that waits on the participants' channels. A single queue sees every message in the channels it is subscribed to, but not DMs to a bot. So DMs would still need a queue per bot, or a different way to wake a VM, for example a message to the workshop's admin bot.
- **Plan limits.** Zulip Cloud's limits on users, bots, channels, upload storage and API rates, and the plan's cost for about 1,000 members, are not checked (V14). Participants' uploads, final-sync reports and exports all count against storage.

### 11.5 Automated month-end

Each kept VM ends one month after its workshop, for every workshop (Q14). At about 170 kept VMs that is several a day, too many for hand-run steps. **Built** (stage 8, #570; ORGANIZERS §5.7). The sketch it was built from, each part with what was built (*As built*):

- **The date (built).** A roster column, `keep_until`: a date, `2026-11-30`, whose end is at 15:00 UTC that day (`grammar.END_HOUR`), or a date and a time in UTC, `2026-11-30T18:00`. `!keep-until DATE|none SEL` sets it when a participant opts in, extends it with a later date, and clears it with `none` (§3, #570). `!roster import` takes it as an input column that an empty cell keeps, as it keeps `region`, so that a CSV from before the column never clears it; `!roster sync` keeps it. `!roster show` lists it under `kept until`. A dated `!keep-until`'s plan says how its rows end, from the workshop's schedule in `state.db` as it is then, and the month-end run applying in the workshop, if one is (`Planning._kept`, `monthend.keep_until_note`, #868):
  - with no row scheduled or running, that nothing ends on its own at that time;
  - a row `running` is ending now, whatever the date. In the run applying, the plan can't apply until that run ends, and the run goes on with the row. In a run cut short, which a later look resumes, the date drops the row out only if it applies first. `!month-end cancel` stops it either way;
  - the rows the schedule holds at that end already, with their warnings;
  - the rows it won't end then: those the new date drops out of it, and those it doesn't hold. Each of those ends only with a new `!month-end schedule` or a `!retire`;
  - a schedule that can't be read: the plan still, with a note that says so.
- **Warnings (built, #570).** Seven days and one day before, the workshop's admin bot sends the participant a DM: the date, that their work is on GitHub, and how to ask for an export (P-b).
  - **As built** (`zab.admin.monthend`, `Planning.month_end`): the bot looks at each schedule once a minute (`planning.MONTH_END_EVERY`). The notice is due 7 days before a row's end, or at once when the schedule is approved later than that; the reminder 1 day before, unless the notice went within 36 hours of the end, which then covers it, so that no two DMs come hours apart.
  - **Never twice:** each DM is a new message, never edited (Zulip Cloud's rate guidance, #754), and is recorded in `state.db` before its post, as `unsure`, and as `sent` only once Zulip returned its message's ID: a restart while the post is out leaves it `unsure`, never sent again (review of #829, B2). One that Zulip refused with a 4xx (`AdminBot.delivered`) is tried again at the next looks, 3 times in all (`monthend.TRIES`), and refused at every try stops its row (*A refusal stops the row*, below); one that never reached Zulip (no connection, its rate limit) is tried again without counting; one that may have landed (a 5xx, a lost answer) is never sent again. A row without a `zulip_user_id` gets none, and the schedule's plan names each such row (*No Zulip ID*, below).
  - **A look's DMs stop at the first that fails,** of any kind, and at 25 (`monthend.LOOK_DMS`): the rows after it go at the next looks, a minute apart, so that a blip of Zulip costs one row's warning at most, and 170 rows due at once go in 7 looks (review of #829, B3 and N1). One row's error never keeps the others back. A DM that never reached Zulip leaves no line in the topic and no `month_end` record, only a warning in the bot's log: Zulip is out of reach then, and the row's warning, with its line, goes at a later look.
  - **The claim is the point of no return:** a cancel that lands after a DM's claim, while it waits for Zulip, doesn't stop that DM.
  - **Said once:** each look that warned or dropped a row says so in one line in the schedule's topic, `📨 **P-60**, the month end: …`, with the rows that got no DM, and writes a `month_end` record (§2.8).
  - **Drop-outs:** before its warnings, each scheduled row is checked against the roster (`monthend.changed`). A row that left the roster, was excluded or retired, or whose `keep_until`, `zulip_user_id` or bot keys changed since the approval, drops out; a `keep_until` moved earlier or later alike.
  - **The texts** (`monthend.notice`, `monthend.reminder`), with the row's slug, the workshop's name and the roster's `repo`:

    > **Your workshop VM ends on Monday, November 30, 2026, at 15:00 UTC.**
    >
    > The organizers of harvard-2026 have scheduled the end of the VM that runs your bots Claude · alice and Codex · alice. From that time on, your bots may stop answering at any moment.
    >
    > **What is kept:** before the VM is deleted, your agents stop, and the work in your repository on the VM, with all its topic branches, is committed and pushed to [MathAgents/alice](https://github.com/MathAgents/alice) on GitHub. Your repository and your Zulip channels stay as they are.
    >
    > **What is not pushed:** your agents' session transcripts, anything outside your repository on the VM, files over 50 MB, files your `.gitignore` excludes, `.lake` build folders, and anything that looks like a credential. If you want an archive of your session transcripts and your work folder, or more time, ask the workshop's organizers before November 30. Nobody reads replies to this message.
    >
    > **Before then,** sign the VM out of your accounts: ask one of your agents to run `codex logout` and `claude auth logout`. Once the VM is gone, revoke its sessions in your ChatGPT and Claude account settings.

    > **Reminder: your workshop VM ends on Monday, November 30, 2026, at 15:00 UTC.** From then on, your bots Claude · alice and Codex · alice may stop answering at any moment. The work in your repository on the VM is pushed to [MathAgents/alice](https://github.com/MathAgents/alice) before the VM is deleted; session transcripts and anything outside the repository are not, unless you have asked the organizers for an archive. If you haven't yet, ask one of your agents to run `codex logout` and `claude auth logout`. Nobody reads replies to this message.
- **The end.** On the day, for that row, in order:
  1. `power.py --start --only <slug> --wait`, which returns once the instance runs and SSH answers. A kept VM runs idle stop, so it is usually stopped, and `final_sync.py` reaches a VM only over SSH: an unreachable VM fails the sync, and the row would never get further;
  2. `final-sync`, which takes the idle-stop hold that rollouts use (`/run/zab-idle-stop.hold`, with an expiry an hour ahead) for the sync, so that idle stop can't stop the VM during it, and releases it once the sync has ended on the VM (#370; `workshop/admin/README.md` §7.1). Before the sync, a VM that step 1 started stays up for its idle-stop timeout (idle stop's uptime rule), so the sync must begin within it. `power.py --start --wait` leaves a VM that was already running as it is, and one with nothing else keeping it up may stop at its next tick, before the hold: so step 2 follows step 1 at once, and should the VM stop anyway, step 2 fails as unreachable, which stops the row's chain (below); the row's retry must then start from step 1. After the sync, nothing needs the VM running: steps 3 to 6 act on EC2 and Zulip, and a stopped VM is torn down all the same;
  3. `protect off`. A kept VM has termination protection, and `teardown.py` lists a protected VM as `keep` and still succeeds, so without this step the VM and its volume and address would stay, and keep costing, behind deactivated bots;
  4. `teardown`, **5 minutes after step 3 ended** (`actions.PROTECT_SETTLE`; §3, *Before `!teardown`*; #378). EC2 shows and enforces the lifted protection minutes late, so a teardown at once would list the VM `keep` and leave it. The guard refuses a teardown within that time anyway: the run takes its refusal as a wait, not a failure, and runs the step again from the time it names. Waiting is not a step of its own: a restart in between resumes at step 4, which waits out what is left;
  5. a check that the instance is terminated (EC2 lists it as `shutting-down` or `terminated`, or no longer at all);
  6. `deactivate-bots`.

  A step that fails, or a VM still running after its teardown, stops that row's chain before its bots are deactivated, and alerts the organizers' channel.
  - **As built** (`Planning._run`, `Runner.month_end`, `zab.admin.monthend`): at each look, once a minute, the rows due (`monthend.due_rows`) start a run, when no plan of the workshop applies or is handed over and its last `!protect` is 5 minutes old (that refusal is a wait). A run is a plan of its own, created APPLYING with the bot as its requester (`!retire <rows>`, its `request` and `apply_start` naming the schedule and its approvers), so that it holds the workshop's apply lock, posts its progress and result in the schedule's topic, and a restart marks it INTERRUPTED. Its steps are `!retire`'s chain (`actions.build_run`, with the release running now), which adds idle stop off, the waker off and the uninstall to the six steps above; step 5 is `teardown.py --strict`'s own wait for the termination. They run in two passes (`monthend.order`): every row up to its protection off, then every row's teardown and bots, each teardown no sooner than `PROTECT_SETTLE` after its row's protection off ended (`RunHooks.before`), so that with several rows most of the 5 minutes are over. A row that stops is `stopped`, said in the result, and never tried again on its own: `!retire` ends it, or `!retire <row> unsynced` when it stopped before its bots, its VM torn down (the teardown cleared its `ip`, #604). A read at the run's start that fails (the roster, EC2) stops no row: the run says so in the topic, and the rows wait for the next look, 5 looks in a row at most (`runner.READ_TRIES`), then stop (review of #835, N8). A row whose VM is gone after its protection off, its teardown not on record (a restart during the teardown), stops, saying so: `!retire <row> unsynced` ends its bots (N2). The rows that drop out or stop at the run's start get a `month_end` record (`what: "run"`, `dropped`, `stopped`) before the line that names them (B3). A `!month-end cancel` of a row in a run applying now says so, and that what the run did stays done, its VM gone when its teardown is on record (B1). Before a teardown, the guard also requires this schedule's final sync on record (N5). A run that an error ends before its apply does (its `request` or `apply_start` record not written, its rows not marked, anything before its first step or in its own end) is `failed`, so that it never keeps the workshop busy, and its rows are taken up at a later look: those not yet started run, and those `running` resume at their first unfinished step (Codex round 5). Each says so in the schedule's topic, and the next run waits 2 minutes, then twice as long after each such error in a row, up to an hour (`planning.MONTH_END_HELD`; review of #835, N1). A row cancelled during the run stops before its next step. Before each step that destroys something (protection off, teardown, bots) and after any wait for it, the run checks the row again, last of all (`RunHooks.before`, Codex round 1 on #835): still `running` under its own schedule, its VMs tagged with its slug, read afresh, still the approved ones, and the roster, read afresh, still as approved: its `keep_until`, in service, not excluded, its Zulip ID and its bots' keys (Codex round 2); anything else, a read that fails included, stops the row before the step. The run itself starts by reading the roster afresh, under the workshop's lock, and drops each due row that changed since the look. Progress is its schedule's alone (`month_end_steps` keys it by the schedule's plan): a new schedule of the same row never skips a step on an earlier schedule's record. Each row's step is on the audit log as it ends, a `retire_step` record, as a `!retire`'s is (#855, #837): the ledger marks the row from it in place, uninstalled, synced and retired, a hard stop that cuts the run's `apply_end` losing none of it, and the run's `apply_end` is the summary. A step record that can't be written stops the run, its row there and every other row where it was: the audit log is shared, and the next write would likely fail too; the result says where each row was left and what to send once the log is fixed. Only then does the run record the step in `state.db` (`month_end_steps`), which a resume reads for its position: that record never claims a step the log lacks. A resumed run's cells, in its result and its `apply_end`, include the steps an earlier run of the schedule did, `ok`, without running them again (Codex round 6). A row left with every step done, by a restart before the run's end, is marked done at the next look, retired by its own step's record, and the look's line in the topic names it (`retired`, #840); one whose steps `state.db` has but whose bots' deactivation the log lacks (a run of a development build between #835 and #860, never a release, which wrote no step records; or a plan that named the row since) stops, saying so, for a `!retire … unsynced`.
  - **No notice, no end** (#570, #883): a row runs only once its notice is on record (`run_at`) as sent, `unsure` (it may have landed) or `no Zulip ID`, and never with a warning on record as refused: a notice on record is not a participant told (review of #835, B2).
  - **A refusal stops the row** (#883): a notice or a reminder that Zulip refused at every try stops its row before any step (`monthend.refuse`): `stopped`, its VM neither started, synced, unprotected nor torn down, its bots kept. The row stays in service until an organizer ends it by hand, `!retire <row>`, or gives it a new end, `!keep-until`, then `!month-end schedule`. The look's line in the schedule's topic says so for each such row, with those commands, and the row's note in `!month-end show` gives them too. While a refused warning waits for its next try, the row doesn't run: a notice taken back is not on record, and a reminder refused at a try stays on record as refused (`Planning._warn`), owed: due again until the end, and keeping its row from running (`run_at`). A reminder is due only until the end, so one still refused when the end comes, its retries cut short, stops the row at the next look (`monthend.refused`); being on record, that survives a restart. A new `!month-end schedule` of the same end and recipient tries a refused notice again, with tries of its own: due until it goes, with the row waiting for it, a retry of a notice is always a real one. It carries a refused reminder over as it is (`monthend._carried`, Codex round 1): tried again while the end hasn't come, it stops the row once it has, so a new schedule approved minutes before the end, with no look to try the reminder in between, can't let the row run. A schedule can't be approved after a row's end: the apply's rebuild refuses it, `stale`. A refusal on record on a row still scheduled, which only a release before #883 left, stops it at the next look, with the same line.
  - **No Zulip ID** (#883): the row gets no warning, and the approval of the plan that names it is its consent. The plan names each such row, every one, in its first note, which says first that approving it ends them at their dates all the same, then lists them, so that a plan cut to fit, whose notes get a quarter of its room, cuts the list, never that sentence (review of #908, N5); the look's line says so again, and the row runs at its end, as one whose notice went.
  - **The end a notice names.** A reminder that goes after a notice names that same end. When the notice went before the end, the row runs at its end, however short the notice the approvers gave. When it went only after the end, or less than a day before it although the schedule was approved before that day (the server down, or a release without the warnings, until then: `monthend.late`, #840), its text names an end a day after it (`end_of`), and the row runs then. A schedule approved less than a day before a row's end is the approvers' own short notice, and stands. The end a notice's DM named is kept with the notice in `state.db` (its fourth field in `warned`), and kept with it whenever a new schedule of the same end and recipient carries the notice over, after a cancel too: the run reads that end (`Entry.named`), never recomputes it from a later approval, so no VM ends before the end its participant was told (Codex round 1 on #852). The approval below covers all six steps.
- **Progress, step by step** (built). Each completed step is recorded in `state.db` for its row, as it completes (`month_end_steps`, keyed by the VMs the schedule approved, with each step's start and end; `apply_end` records them too, as `at` and `s`). A run resumes at the row's first unfinished step and never repeats a finished one: after a restart between `teardown` and `deactivate-bots`, it goes on to deactivate the bots, rather than trying to sync a terminated VM. One exception keeps the work safe (review of #835, round 2): a resumed row skips its recorded first pass, up to its sync, only while the fleet's ledger, read under the run's lock, still has its work saved, its sync counting (`Ending.counts`), or before the sync its uninstall (`monthend.unsaved`). A plan that reinstalled its bridge or touched the row meanwhile (a `!rollout` of it or of `all`, while a restart, an error's hold or a failed read left it `running` with no run applying) made the ledger forget them, and so did a hard kill before the run's `apply_end`: its start, idle stop and waker off, uninstall and sync then run again, said in the topic and in a `month_end` record (`redone`), and the run's cells, which a resumed run fills in with the steps done before (Codex round 6), never claim a sync this schedule's work no longer has. A stop of the service lets the run write its `apply_end`, so a plain restart, as A17's, resumes after the sync. Once a row's teardown is on record, nothing is done again whatever the ledger says: its VM is gone, and the teardown's guard had its sync on record (N5), so a resume after a hard kill between its teardown and its bots goes on to its bots (Codex round 7). The final sync's success and its report stay with the row, and in the audit log, as the evidence that `teardown` requires (§3), after the VM is gone.
- **Approval.** Either:
  - a daily plan in the organizers' channel, approved like any destructive plan (two approvers, or a global admin alone, and the phrase); or
  - **a pre-approved schedule (built, but its runs):** one plan, approved once in the same way, that lists the rows and their dates and is hashed on them. Each day's run is a step of it. Its targets are recomputed at each run and may only shrink: an extended or already removed row drops out. A row added, or a date moved earlier, needs a new plan.
    - **As built** (#570): `!month-end schedule`, of every row in service with a `keep_until` (`actions._build_month_end`), destructive with the phrase `month-end <n>`, hashed as `!retire`'s (§2.4). It refuses a row whose end has passed (`!retire` it) or lies more than 366 days ahead (`actions.KEEP_UNTIL_DAYS`, as `!keep-until`, since `!roster import` checks only the form), one without an `ip`, and one that `runner.vms_found` refuses as it does for `!retire`: no VM found, more than one, or VMs found by its address (or a `participant` tag in any case) other than those tagged with its slug exactly. A single VM tagged with its slug but away from its roster address is flagged in the plan, not refused; a recent `!protect` doesn't hold it back, since it tears nothing down at once. Its apply stores the schedule (`zab.admin.monthend`), replacing the workshop's. Once the runs are built, a row whose `keep_until` changed since the approval drops out, a date moved earlier included, so that a row ends only at the end that both the roster and an approval give it. `!month-end show` reads the schedule; `!month-end cancel SEL` takes rows out at once, as `!reject` vetoes a plan, from an approver of the workshop.

  The schedule is what makes 170 kept VMs manageable; the daily plan is its fallback.
- **Across restarts and upgrades.** A schedule runs for a month, while §2.4's plans live for 10 minutes, are hashed with the server's release, and never resume after a restart. So a schedule is stored in `state.db` as approved per-row plans, each with its own targets: the row, its instance ID, its bots' identities and its date. As built, the bots' identities are the SHA-256 of their keys, `out/<slug>/<kind>.zuliprc`, as `!deactivate-bots` hashes them: `out/` holds no bot user IDs. Its hash leaves out the server's release. What the approvers approve is the rows, the dates and the six steps, not one release's argument lists: each run builds the argument lists with the running release, and re-validates the targets first. A target that changed (another instance, other bots, a row taken off the kept list) drops out of the schedule and is reported. A restart or an `!upgrade-server` therefore keeps the schedule. A run that a restart interrupts is reported `INTERRUPTED`, and the next run resumes each row at its first unfinished step (above). `!upgrade-server`'s plan lists the schedules it carries over.
  - **As built:** the re-validation is in two places. At each look, before the warnings, each scheduled row is checked against the roster (`monthend.changed`: gone, excluded, retired, its `keep_until`, its Zulip ID, its bots' keys); a row a restart left `running` too, unless every step is done. At the run, under the workshop's lock and before its steps, the roster is read again and the same check applies (Codex round 2 on #835), then each row's VMs (`vms` and `tagged`) must still be the approved ones while a step that reaches them remains (`monthend.vm_changed`); and before each destructive step, the guard checks both again (`RunHooks.before`). The restart's message says that the bot's next look resumes the run. An `!upgrade-server` (#569) is refused while a run applies (its approval is answered `busy:`, since it needs every workshop's lock, §2.4 step 5), and restarts the bots as any deploy does.
- **Retiring a workshop.** After its last VM, the workshop's bot is deactivated and its entry removed, with its `ws/<ws>/` kept in the backups. Its slugs go to `retired-slugs.txt`, and no later row on the server may take one (§2.1).

### 11.6 Roles

- **Global admins** (the deployer and the lead organizer(s), Q2) act through any bot, and approve server-class plans.
- **A workshop's organizers** are recognized only by that workshop's bot. They act only on its fleet, and see only it in the console.
- **Deployers** hold SSH to the server, and a deployer's dedicated key signs the releases (Q5).
- **The separation between workshops rests on the bots, not on IAM.** One role serves all of an account's workshops, scoped by one fleet-wide tag (§5). A workshop in an account of its own would be separated by IAM as well (§11.8).

### 11.7 The handoff test

The goal is that someone other than the maintainer can run a workshop end to end. That is tested, not assumed: before a workshop that the maintainer doesn't run, its lead organizer goes from roster to month-end teardown with only `docs/ORGANIZERS.md` (PR 17), the bots and the console, and the maintainer at most as a deployer. Every step that needed the maintainer, or that the runbook didn't cover, becomes an issue.

### 11.8 Accounts, regions and GitHub

- **Regions.** One server serves several regions, one per workshop (§2.1). The collector, the waker and the role cover them all.
- **Accounts.** The portability seam (§6) keeps every account-specific value in config. A workshop in another account would take an optional role to assume, under `[workshop.aws]`, as the waker already starts VMs in another account through an assumed role (#298). That seam is noted in PR 5a and not built. One admin server per account is the alternative.
- **Adding a workshop** costs the account owner nothing in a region that is set up (§2.9, §5). **Decided (#422):** the server-scope tag `zab-scope=<scope>` is on every fleet resource, and the role's conditions are on it instead of on each workshop's value. Setting up a new region, once, is a security group, an AMI copy and one policy paste.
- **GitHub.** The PAT's expiry must cover each kept month (Q7), and its Administration write reaches every repository of the organization. Both move the GitHub App (P-c) up as the workshops multiply.
- **Slugs at scale.** Over 1,000 participants, refusing every retired slug (§2.1) will turn away common ones. The alternative is to prefix new sandboxes and repositories with the workshop, which changes `zulip_provision.py` and `provision_repos.py`. Not decided.

### 11.9 Networking: do the VMs need public addresses?

**Today** each VM has an Elastic IP. It gives:

- outbound access for the agents: Zulip, the Claude and OpenAI APIs, GitHub, packages;
- inbound SSH for administration;
- a stable address across stop and start, which the roster's `ip` and the pinned `known_hosts` rely on.

Participants have no SSH, and the files host only receives uploads, so it never connects to the VMs.

**Cost** (the price as workshop fleets were billed, V13, Nov 7, 2026): each public IPv4 address costs $0.005 an hour, about $3.65 a month, in use or idle. At about 170 kept VMs that is about $600 a month, or about $3,700 over six months.

**A plan for scale, not built.** The fleets keep Elastic IPs, the proven path.

- **Private subnets.** The VMs get no public IPv4. Private addresses cost nothing and stay stable across stop and start.
- **Egress** through a small NAT instance per region, or per workshop VPC. It needs two instances, or one that is replaced automatically: a single NAT instance that fails cuts every agent in the region off the internet.
- **Not AWS's NAT Gateway:** about $33 a month per availability zone, plus a charge per GB processed, which could exceed the savings with agents downloading.
- **IPv6 alone doesn't work:** github.com serves no IPv6, so IPv4 egress through NAT is still needed.
- **Administration.** The admin server reaches the VMs at their private addresses within the VPC. Workshops in other regions need VPC peering, or one admin server per region; that seam is decided later. SSM Session Manager is ruled out: it needs an IAM role on each VM, and §5 gives VMs none, since their agents have sudo.
- **A new break-glass path.** §1.7's fallback is SSH from a deployer's laptop to the VMs' public addresses. Without them, losing the admin server also loses that route, so private subnets need a bastion or a VPN for the deployers first.
- **Neither the files host nor the admin server is the NAT.** The files host may be in another region, and it has another role. The admin server holds the fleets' keys and must not be the agents' gateway (§1.4).
- **A bonus:** the Elastic IP quota stops being a per-region limit (§11.1).
